# design.md: rocinante

The architecture details (§4.3 to §4.13, the last being the code map), the language (§5) and the feature parity map (§6). `CLAUDE.md` points here and does not import it, so read the section a task touches; together the two files are the single source of truth. Section numbers are global, so `§5.9` means section 5.9 of this file and `§8` means section 8 of `CLAUDE.md`. When a decision changes, update the file that holds the section, in place.

---

### 4.3 Rendering model

- Immediate-mode verbs (`circle`, `line`, `box`) append to a per-window **command list**. The renderer turns the command list into batched, instanced GPU draws at the end of the frame. Users never see this.
- **Commands describe themselves** (decided 2026-10-27). Each command-list entry keeps its kind, position, size and colour, plus a slot for the `.roci` source span (empty until Phase 2 fills it). Work-count benchmarks (§9), scene descriptions for screen readers (§4.8) and error and highlight locations all read the same records, so they cannot drift from what is drawn.
- **GPU features load lazily** (decided 2026-10-27). Pipelines and shaders are created on first use, and one that fails to build falls back without taking the window down (non-negotiable 3). Shader code that a sketch never uses must never stop it from running on an old GPU (L5 failed this way on a 2009 laptop).
- Geometry that does not change between frames is detected (hash of command + parameters) and cached on the GPU automatically.
- 2D and 3D share one window. A window has a default 2D orthographic projection; using any 3D verb (`box`, `camera`, `light`) switches that draw block to a perspective camera with sensible defaults, including mouse orbit, unless the user sets a camera.
- **SDL3**: Odin's `vendor:sdl3`, `vendor:sdl3/ttf` and `vendor:sdl3/image` exist; use them when current, otherwise our own bindings for the latest SDL3 release (§3).
- **Shaders**: internal shaders are written once in HLSL and compiled offline with SDL_shadercross to SPIR-V, MSL and DXIL, then embedded in the binary. SDL_shadercross takes SPIR-V or HLSL as input and outputs DXBC, DXIL, SPIR-V, MSL or HLSL, and offers both runtime translation and an offline command-line tool. **User-written shaders are HLSL too** (decided 2026-10-24): one shader language for the core and for users. `roci run` translates them at run time through SDL_shadercross, so editing a shader hot-reloads it; `roci package` compiles them offline like the internal ones. A small rocinante shader dialect that compiles to HLSL is on the roadmap (README.md). **Pinned and offline** (decided 2026-10-27): SDL_shadercross has no tags or releases, so the commit that compiles our shaders is pinned in the `SHADERCROSS_COMMIT` file, like `ODIN_VERSION`. Its CLI takes `--source HLSL`, `--dest SPIRV|MSL|DXIL`, `--stage`, `--entrypoint` and `--output` (read from `src/cli.c`). The compiled outputs are committed, so contributors need none of this unless they change a shader, and nothing from shadercross, SPIRV-Cross (Apache-2.0) or DXC (LLVM Release Licence, an NCSA-style licence) is shipped, which keeps all three inside §3 as build tools. Use the CLI's `--msl-version` flag, because it defaults to MSL 1.2.0. **In practice** (decided 2026-10-27): DXC has no macOS build, so on a Mac `shaders/compile.sh` runs shadercross's own steps by hand (HLSL to SPIR-V with glslang, SPIR-V to Metal with SPIRV-Cross), and `shaders/compile-dxil.sh` makes DXIL with `dxc` on Windows; shadercross itself is for computers where DXC exists, and the outputs are committed either way.
- **Gradients** (decided 2026-10-27, §5.9): each distinct gradient is baked on the CPU into a 256-colour ramp, mixed in its colour space (Oklab by default), and kept with the frame's others in one small float texture; the fragment shaders of both pipelines look the colour up from where the pixel is in the shape's own bounding box. A shape's gradient therefore costs one sample, and a thousand shapes sharing a gradient are still one draw. The row after each ramp holds its parameters: texel 0 the kind and three numbers, and for an SVG drawing's gradients (`Placed_Linear`, `Placed_Radial`, 2026-11-22) texels 1 to 3 the transform from the box into the gradient's space, each number a 16-bit value plus what it leaves over, so the texture stays 16-bit (filtering 32-bit floats is not promised everywhere) and the numbers keep about 22 bits; `placed_gradient_at` is the shader's sum written in Odin for the tests.
- **Overlay font** (decided 2026-10-27): until Phase 3's real text, the error overlay and debug output use a built-in public-domain 8x8 bitmap font (printable ASCII, whole-number sizes), drawn by its own small pipeline, so Phase 2's overlay needs neither a font file nor the text-engine decision below.
- **Text** (decided 2026-11-02): SDL3_ttf's GPU text engine (`TTF_CreateGPUTextEngine`), through `vendor:sdl3/ttf`. Spike 5 (`docs/phases.md`) checked shaping (HarfBuzz: Arabic joins and runs right to left, Devanagari gathers its marks), fallback fonts and atlases. The engine gives, per text, triangles in pixels from the text's top left with y up, and one or more atlas textures (B8G8R8A8; plain glyphs and colour glyphs such as emoji come as separate sequences); `roci/gfx` lays them out on the canvas (position, alignment, transform) and draws them with its own "typeset" shader. **Inter** (SIL OFL 1.1, regular and bold) is inside the program (`roci/gfx/fonts/`) so text needs no file and looks the same everywhere. A font file in `assets/fonts` is found by name; the system's CJK, Arabic, Devanagari and emoji fonts are added as fallbacks where they exist (`FALLBACK_FONT_FILES`) and never copied into a sketch (a font the sketch *names* that is installed is another matter, §4.9: it is copied into the sketch). Text is rasterised at the size times the display density, and a transform that scales text resamples it. **Emoji** (`roci/gfx/emoji.odin`, decided 2026-11-02): a colour emoji font is often pictures at one size (Apple Color Emoji: 160 pixels) and SDL_ttf lays them out at that size, so each emoji (with its joiners, skin tone and variation selector) is swapped for an em space before the text is set, the space's box comes from `TTF_GetTextSubString`, and the emoji is drawn on its own scaled into that box; measuring uses the same swap. Symbols Inter has itself (arrows, check marks) stay text. Known gap: flags (two regional indicators) draw nothing on macOS. **Text under a transform** (2026-11-19, #45): text is laid out at the size it is drawn, the window's scale times the scale of the transform in force (`text_scale` in `roci/gfx/typeset.odin`: the square root of its determinant, 1 for a turn or a slide, within a thousandth), so text after `grow 3` is as sharp as shapes there; the vertices are placed in the item's own units and the transform carries them to the size they were made at. It is capped at 4096 device pixels (past that the picture is enlarged as it was) and never below a hundredth. Turned text is still sampled between the glyphs of the shared atlas, so a golden scene of it depends on which scenes ran before it; `text_grown` has none.
- **Pictures** (decided 2026-11-03): a picture file is decoded on the CPU by Odin's own `core:image` (PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA and QOI; nothing is added to the program), kept as 8-bit RGBA with straight alpha, and turned into a GPU texture the first time it is drawn. A picture is drawn as a textured quad through the typeset pipeline, which takes a tint as the vertex colour (`coloured` 2 in the shader), so pictures and text keep their order in one batch and the first slice needs no shader of its own. Samples are linear, so a magnified picture is smooth; `no smooth` (§6) will switch it to nearest. Canvases (`canvas`, `draw into`; done 2026-11-03) are pictures whose texture is drawn into, with two textures each so a multisampled pass can add to what was there; image fills (§5.9; done 2026-11-03 for shapes: the fill is cut into triangles carrying their place in the picture and drawn through the typeset pipeline, whose shader clips and repeats), `blend`, `clip` (done 2026-11-05) and the pixel verbs (below) build on the same textures.
- **Vector export** (decided 2026-11-06): `save "shot.svg"` writes the shapes of the finished frame as an SVG file (`roci/gfx/svg.odin`), made from the command list with no GPU: one element for each shape under the transform it was drawn with (so an outline's width follows it as on the screen), the style as attributes, gradients as SVG gradients (a linear one is exact, a radial one is the box's inscribed ellipse as the shader reads it, the colors between stops are 33 samples of the baked ramp so Oklab mixing is kept), a clip as a clip path and a blend as `mix-blend-mode`, pictures embedded once as PNG, text as `<text>` with a line for each line (wrapped at spaces by the same measure the renderer uses) in the font by name, which the viewer needs. What a file cannot say is drawn as the nearest thing or left out (a conic gradient in its middle color, a picture or shader fill in the fill's color, a canvas, the picture behind everything, a tint, the `subtract` blend, effects) and `svg_write` lists it, which `save` hands back as an approximate save and the interpreter says as the warning `Save_Approximate`. A canvas as an embedded picture was the other choice, and the PDF below the same list. **Recording** (decided 2026-11-06): `record "clip.mp4"` and `no record` (language verbs in `roci/record.odin`, `interp/record.odin`) take the same finished frame `save` takes after each frame of a window (`record_frame`, from `show_frame`) and put it in a movie: an `.apng` made here with `apng_encode` (the frames kept in memory, 512 MB at most, then written), or, for `.mp4`, `.mov`, `.webm`, `.mkv` and `.gif`, a video made by the user's own `ffmpeg` that raw RGBA frames are piped to as they are drawn (started with the first frame, whose size it is told; `-vf pad` to an even size and `yuv420p` for the players; its errors kept in `<file>.ffmpeg.log` and quoted when it fails). ffmpeg is the optional extra of §4.11, never a dependency: with none installed `record` says how to get one, and an `.apng` still works. The movie plays at 60 frames a second (`RECORD_FPS`). A video encoder linked into rocinante (libvpx for VP9 in WebM, or the platform encoders) was the other choice and waits for Phase 6.
**PDF** (decided 2026-11-06): `save "page.pdf"` writes the same command list as one PDF page the size of the window (`roci/gfx/pdf.odin`, no library: the objects, cross-reference table and drawing operators are written by hand, streams deflated by sancho's compressor, no date or random number in the file so the same frame gives the same bytes): paths with the style as operators under a `cm`, gradients as shading patterns (function samples from the baked ramp), opacity and blend as graphics states (`Multiply`, `Screen` and `Lighten`; `add` and `subtract` are said), a clip as a clip path, pictures as image objects with their alpha as a soft mask, and **text as the outlines of its letters**: `roci/gfx/truetype.odin` reads a TrueType font's `cmap`, `hmtx` and `glyf` tables (simple and composite glyphs) so the file needs no font, at the cost of no kerning and no CFF fonts, and a character the font lacks (emoji, the scripts a fallback font draws) is left out and said. Lines of a wrapped text are broken where SDL_ttf breaks them (`TTF_GetTextSubStringForLine` on a layout-only text), so a file wraps as the window does. Text embedded as a subsetted font, which stays selectable, was the other choice and needs a font writer.
- **Pixels** (decided 2026-11-05): a picture never changes, so reading and changing pixels are verbs that read one and make another: `pixel photo at x y` (a color; clear black outside), `pixels photo` (a list of up to a million colors), `picture from colors size w h`, and `crop`, `resize` (a side of 0 follows the picture's shape; `as nearest` keeps hard edges), `filter` (`gray`, `invert`, `threshold`, `posterize`, `blur`, `edges`, `pixelate`, with `by`) and `mask by` (the stencil's brightness times its alpha, stretched over the picture). The work is plain CPU code on the picture's own pixels (`roci/gfx/picture_ops.odin`, which mixes pixels with alpha already multiplied in so a clear pixel's colour never bleeds), so it is testable and the same everywhere; a GPU version of the filters is the shader work. A canvas is read back from the GPU at its logical size, once for each round it was drawn with. **`mask` with a canvas runs on the GPU** (decided 2026-11-27, at Fabrizio's asking, after WILDVEX's logo melt fell from 100 to 12 frames a second on Windows): when the picture or the stencil is a canvas, `roci.mask_picture` closes their rounds, draws the canvases' waiting rounds and draws one pass of the built-in `gfx/effects/mask.hlsl` (an effect shader that reads two textures, translated at run time like the built-in effects, so no compiled copy is kept for any system) into a texture of its own, and gives a fixed canvas (`Canvas.fixed`: every verb that takes a canvas takes it, `draw into` refuses it, and `evict_derived` lets it go like a made picture, its texture dropped by `release_stale_textures`). It keeps the picture's density (a canvas's, or one device pixel to a pixel of a loaded picture), so its edges are sharper than the processor's mask of the canvas read back at its logical size, and it is left out of SVG and PDF files as any canvas is. With no graphics that run shaders it falls back to the processor. Measured on Windows 11 (RTX 2080 Ti, Direct3D 12) with `tests/bench/mask-canvas` (four masks a frame of 1300 by 560 pictures by two canvases drawn every frame): 77 ms a frame on the processor, 4.9 on the GPU, 1.35 once the masks reuse their textures and a replaced one is let go two frames after its last use (below), 1.2 without the masks; WILDVEX's old melt on the title, fullscreen, 31 ms a frame, 15.7, then 9.9, against 5.7 without the melt (most of the rest is its drip edge, worked out in the interpreter each frame); on the processor the read-back of a canvas was about 3.5 ms (the fence wait only 0.5), and the rest was the processor's work on fresh memory for each new picture (about 7 ms a mask) and about 2 ms to make each new texture. The other operations still read a canvas back, and the pictures guide points to an effect inside `draw into` for a canvas that changes every frame. Pictures the operations make are remembered by what made them (picture, numbers and, for a canvas, how many rounds it had), and when they hold more than 256 MB (each counted twice, its pixels and its texture) or are more than 2,048, the ones unused longest are let go (`image_release`), down to nine tenths of the limit, so a sketch that crops every frame does not pile up pictures; a let-go picture draws nothing, like one that is not there. A picture changed in place (`set pixel`, as in Processing) was the other choice.
- **User shaders** (decided 2026-11-05): `effect "wave.hlsl" by { amount: 0.02 }` runs a shader of the sketch's own over a window's finished frame. The file is HLSL in `assets/` (found by name), holding one fragment function `ps_main(position, uv)`; a prelude put in front of it (`EFFECT_PRELUDE`, with `#line 1` so a message names the writer's own lines) gives it `scene` and `scene_sampler` (the frame), `size`, `mouse`, `time` and `frame`, and every other global of the file (`float amount;`) is a value the sketch gives by name from the record after `by` (a number, a vector of two to four numbers or a color, 0 to 1 for the shader). The translation is done while the program runs, before the first frame for every shader an `effect` or `shade` line names by its own text and otherwise when the file is first used, and again each time its modification time changes, by shaderc (HLSL to SPIR-V, Apache-2.0, a C API over glslang) and SPIRV-Cross (Apache-2.0): shaderc cannot make the combined texture and sampler the GPU API takes, so SPIR-V goes through SPIRV-Cross to Vulkan GLSL, which joins them, and back through shaderc, and Metal is made from that SPIR-V (`roci/gfx/translate.odin`; bindings in `vendor/shaderc` and `vendor/spirvcross`, linked, so every build of rocinante needs the two libraries). The uniform blocks are put on set 3, bindings 0 (rocinante's) and 1 (the file's own globals, found by reflection, `Shader_Params`), and Metal's buffer numbers are given explicitly, because SPIRV-Cross numbers them by first use. A mistake gives the compiler's own words as the runtime message `Shader_Broken` and the effect is left out until the next save. The renderer draws the frame into a texture instead of the window and each effect draws one triangle that reads the one before (`roci/gfx/renderer_effect.odin`); effects run in the order written, a frame hash includes them and their values but not the time, and an effect inside `draw into` runs over the canvas after that frame's drawing into it, the result staying on the canvas, so repeated every frame it works on its own result (decided 2026-11-05; the canvas list holds its own effects, which `render_canvas_round` runs through the same path as a window's, and the canvas's colors are premultiplied, so `invert` and `grain` use the alpha; shaders as the outline or text fill and `mask`, and a shader that reads more than the frame follow). **Built-in effects** (decided 2026-11-05): a name without an extension (`effect "glow"`) is a shader that comes with rocinante, kept in the program as HLSL (`roci/gfx/effects/`, `builtin_effects.odin`) and translated through the same chain as a sketch's own; each has the values it starts with (a table beside its source), which the sketch's `by` overrides one by one, and a name that is neither one of them nor a file is `Shader_Missing` listing them. They are single-pass (a disc of taps rather than a separable blur), so a big radius shows a little grain in the blur. **Shaders as fills** (decided 2026-11-05): `shade "spiral.hlsl" by { arms: 3 }` makes the fill of every shape drawn after it a shader (a statement, so it lasts like `fill`, which puts a color back; `Style.fill_shader`, with the values' bytes in the list's `effect_values` and a hash of them in the style so a change of value is a change of look). The shape is cut into triangles as a picture fill's is and each corner carries its place in the shape's box, drawn with the sketch's fragment shader and a shared vertex shader (`FILL_VERTEX`), in the shape's order among everything else and by straight alpha; the fragment's prelude (`FILL_PRELUDE`) has `size` (the box), `mouse`, `time` and `frame` and no `scene`, so a file is translated for the use it is first asked for. `fill` words (`circle ... fill shade`), `stroke`, `background` and text fills with a shader follow. SDL_shadercross (no macOS build, DXC) and compiling the shaders ahead of time only were the other choices. **Ready before the first frame** (decided 2026-11-24): a first use cost a frame about a tenth of a second (WILDVEX's logo melt and title pour, measured on lavapipe: the worst frame 99 to 123 ms, against 4 to 6 ms with every shader run once at the start), spent in the translation and then in the graphics driver making the pipeline, both at the moment of first use (how it splits was not measured), so the checker lists the shaders the program names by their own text (`IR_Program.shaders`), both back ends translate them before the top level runs (the interpreter's `warm_shaders` in `start` and a live reload, the compiled `setup`'s first line `rt.warm_shaders`), keeping a problem to be said when the line first runs, and the renderer makes the pipelines of every translated shader in a window's format before that window's frame is drawn (`gfx.prepare_shaders`). A name made while the sketch runs, and a canvas's first frame with a shader, still wait once. Checked by hand by Fabrizio on a Mac with WILDVEX run without its own warm-up of its shaders (2026-11-25). A packaged app does the same: it carries shaderc and SPIRV-Cross (FXC on Windows) and translates at start, since shaders compiled into the package were not chosen; compiling them in `roci package` would make the start shorter and is not done. **On Windows** (decided 2026-11-11), which draws with Direct3D 12 and nothing else (non-negotiable 9), none of that chain is built: `translate_d3d.odin` compiles the same HLSL, with the same preludes (whose registers, `b0, space3` and `t0, s0, space2`, are those SDL's D3D12 back end binds), by FXC, the shader compiler that is part of Windows (`d3dcompiler_47.dll`), into DXBC of Shader Model 5.1, which that back end takes beside DXIL; the vertex shaders of effects and fills likewise (`b0, space1`). FXC cannot be told where to put a shader's globals, and puts them in `$Globals` on `b0, space0`, so a shader with globals is compiled twice: the first compiled code is asked (by reflection) which globals it has, their declarations are blanked in the text (every line keeps its number, so a message still names the writer's line) and declared again in a block on `b1, space3`, and the second compiled code gives the layout of the values. Its messages are rewritten in shaderc's form (`wave.hlsl:3: error: words`), though the words are FXC's own. A structure as a global, which the other systems leave out of the values, is a message on Windows. rocinante's own shaders stay DXIL compiled ahead of time.
- **Vector tessellation** (paths, thick strokes, curves): our own Odin implementation (decided 2026-10-27), package geom: flattening, ear clipping with holes and a stroker with caps and joins. Basic shapes stay on the signed-distance shader; what it cannot draw exactly as styled (quads, arcs, mitred or bevelled corners, butt or square ends) is cut into triangles on the CPU, and the frame is drawn multisampled (4x where the GPU can) so mesh edges are smooth, which avoids building an edge fringe by hand.
- **3D shapes** (decided 2026-11-13, first slice of Phase 4): `box`, `sphere` and `plane` add an item to the window's 3D scene (`roci/gfx/scene3.odin`, kept beside the 2D command list: a shape's transform as three rows, its color, and the camera), and `move x y z`, `turn d around axis` and `grow x y z` change the 3D transform that `push` saves and puts back with the 2D one (a `move x y` and a plain `turn` are still the flat ones). The renderer draws the scene first, in a pass of its own with a D32 depth buffer (`renderer_scene.odin`, `shaders/scene3.hlsl`: one pipeline, the three shapes' triangles in one mesh uploaded once, an instance for each item, one light and a little ambient light worked out per pixel, a surface lit on whichever side faces the eye), and then the 2D pass loads the color and draws everything flat over it, so a flat verb is on top whether it is written before or after a 3D one. The world is the window's: the origin is the middle, x goes right, y goes down and z comes towards the viewer, a unit is a pixel on the plane z = 0 seen straight on, and a turn about an axis goes the way the 2D `turn` goes (clockwise seen from the end of the axis). The camera orbits the origin (field of view 50 degrees, starting 18 degrees above the floor): a left-button drag turns it and the wheel zooms, except when the sketch reads the mouse (`mouse`, `pmouse`, `pressed`, `wheel`, a mouse or touch event block, remembered for the run in `Win.mouse_read`) or the pointer is over a widget. A color's alpha is not used yet (the shapes are opaque), a `background` gradient or picture is a 2D rectangle and so is drawn over the scene, 3D inside `draw into` is left out, and the C API has the 3D verbs (`roci_box`, `roci_camera`, `roci_light_point`, `roci_mesh`, `roci_load_model` and the rest, `roci_ray`, `roci_triangles` and the `roci_svg` family (and, since the same day, everything else: windows and their settings, input, `roci_on` and `roci_every` with callbacks, gradients, pictures and pixels, `roci_save` and `roci_record`, shaders and effects, collision on `roci_shape` with trees of boxes, every widget and group, messages with lists, tweens on the caller's variables, the sketch's random numbers for C's arrays, style scopes, origins, wrapped text and a headless run; the list verbs and tables are C's own arrays and maps, said in `ABI_LANGUAGE`); a point in space is a `roci_vec3`, and `roci_vec3` forms are the ones that end in `_v` or `_sized_v`; `roci/abi_header.odin`'s `ABI_WIDE` says which vector parameters of a registry verb are three numbers wide). The second slice added `cone`, `cylinder`, `capsule`, `torus` and `icosphere` (all one mesh built once: a cone, cylinder and capsule stand on y, a capsule is a sphere cut at its equator whose halves the instance moves apart by `params.x`, and a torus is a ring each of whose corners the instance pushes out along its normal by `params.y`, so one mesh serves every proportion under uniform scale; an icosphere is 80 flat faces) and the helpers `grid`, `axes` and `arrow`, which are made of those solids (thin boxes, cylinders and cones), not of lines. `mesh` with `wireframe` and `mesh`'s textures wait for a slice of their own, because a wireframe made from triangles shows the diagonals of every face. The third slice added the camera verbs: `camera at x y z` (with `fov`) puts the eye somewhere and `look at` aims it, either one taking the camera off the mouse for the rest of the frame (the orbit comes back with the next frame, as style does, so a sketch calls them in `draw`; the first of them starts from where the orbit had the eye), `ortho` and `perspective` choose the projection (ortho keeps one unit a pixel at every distance and zooms with the wheel like the orbit), `screen point p` gives the pixel where a point of the scene shows (the point in the coordinates of the current transform, `{ 0, 0 }` behind the camera) and `world point pixel` the point of the floor, the plane y = 0, under a pixel (`{ 0, 0, 0 }` when the pixel looks past the floor). One `Camera` in `scene3.odin` (the eye and the camera's three axes in the projection's y-up world, how it is cut) makes the shader's matrix and both pickings, so they cannot disagree. The fourth slice added lights and materials: `light ambient|sun|point|spot` (with `at`, `from`, `to`, `angle`, `fill` and `falloff`; up to 64, the first one of a frame takes the usual lights away, a point or spot light's place is in the coordinates of the moment) and `material metallic roughness emissive` (a statement that `push` puts back) and `no lights` (the shapes after it are flat). The fragment shader now has the uniform block of its own that it needs (`Scene_Lights`, on set 3, the lights in three float4 each) and answers light as a physically based surface does: Lambert's diffuse for what is not metal, a GGX highlight with Smith's shadowing and Schlick's Fresnel, mixed in linear light and written back with the gamma curve, an ambient light that also tints a metal's shine; with no `light` written there is a sun and a gray ambient light as before, so every earlier sketch is lit, though a little differently from the first two slices. `glow` was the first name for `emissive` and was turned down because it is a name sketches use for a style. A scene keeps its first 64 lights (their block is 3,104 bytes, and Metal passes a shader at most 4,096 this way) and says `Too_Many_Lights` once for the rest. The seventh slice added the turtle in space (`roci/gfx/turtle3.odin`): `turtle space` makes the window's turtle the one that walks the 3D scene for the frame (the word is `space` because `3d` is not a word the lexer can read: a number is digits with a fraction, so `turtle 3d` is the error it gives), and `forward`, `back`, `left`, `right`, `pen up`, `pen down`, `home` and `show turtle` then act on it, with `up`, `down` and `roll` for the third turn (said once as `Turtle_Flat` when the turtle is flat). It keeps three directions at right angles (the way it faces, its top and its right side, made square again after every turn, so a long walk never leans), starts at the origin facing up the screen with its top towards the viewer (so `right 90` faces right on the screen as the flat turtle does), and its places are in the coordinates of the 3D transform of the moment. It draws a thin tube in the stroke (or black while there is none) as wide as the `thickness`, with a ball at the end of each so a bend is round, so it is made of the same solids as everything else and is lit and casts shadows; `push` saves it and each frame starts with the flat turtle at home. `up`, `down` and `roll` became words, so a sketch's `down` and `roll` were renamed (`examples/tally`, `examples/sengen`). Reading the space turtle as a `turtle` value is not done. The sixth slice added model import: `model "ship.glb" at x y z size s` draws a glTF 2 model (a `.glb`, or a `.gltf` with its buffers beside it or inside it) from the sketch's assets by name, with its middle at the place and its longest side `size` long, in the colors, metal, roughness and glow of its own materials, and shadows and lights treat it as any solid. **The reader is our own** (`roci/gfx/gltf.odin`, `core:encoding/json`, no library) and not `vendor:cgltf`, which this file first named: cgltf ships a library only for macOS and Windows (Linux has to build `cgltf.a` from its C source, and FreeBSD, OpenBSD, iOS and Android have none), which would have meant a C build step on every platform for a format that is a JSON file and a few arrays of numbers; it reads the meshes of the default scene through their nodes (a matrix or a translation, rotation and scale), triangles, strips and fans, indices of any size, strided and normalized data, data URIs, a missing normal made flat, and says in words what it cannot read (Draco and meshopt compression, a missing buffer, damaged data). It leaves out animation, skins, cameras and lights; textures and what goes with them are read since the eighth and ninth slices (below). A model is kept on this thread by its path with its box (`roci/gfx/models.odin`), a file saved again keeps its number with a new version so it is sent to the card again, each of its parts (a run of indices with one material) is one item of the scene drawn from the model's own buffers, and the buffers of a model are uploaded once. The model's world is glTF's, y up, and every corner and normal is put into the sketch's (y down) on the way in. `examples/model` draws `striker.obj`, a CC0 ship of Quaternius's Ultimate Spaceships pack with its picture, and `tests/models/ship.glb`, which `odin run tools/model` writes from a box and a cone, is the fixture of the tests. The fifth slice added shadows: `shadows` turns them on for the frame, and the first `sun` or `spot` light (the usual sun if no light is written) casts them; a point light's shadows are the tenth slice (below). The scene is drawn first as that light sees it (`shadow3.hlsl`, `scene3_shape.hlsli` shared with the scene shader so both put every corner in the same place) into a 2048 square map of depths held as the red of a `R32_FLOAT` color texture, because a color texture can be read by every system and a depth texture cannot (Metal wants a different type for it); a sun's view is a box as wide as the bounding sphere of the scene (`scene_bounds`), a spot light's a cone a little wider than its own. The scene shader compares each pixel's depth in that view with the map over a three by three block of texels, with a bias that grows on surfaces that face away and a push along the normal, and dims only that light. Everything casts and receives; the map is made when a scene first asks for shadows and a one-texel blank is bound otherwise. The DXIL of the 3D and shadow shaders was made on Windows on 2026-11-14, where every 3D example draws on Direct3D 12; after a change to `scene3.hlsl`, `scene3_shape.hlsli` or `shadow3.hlsl`, `shaders/compile-dxil.sh` has to be run there again. **Textures and OBJ** (the eighth slice, 2026-11-13): a model's material can be painted with up to four pictures, each a kept picture (`Image_Id`, decoded by `core:image` like any other, so PNG, JPEG, BMP, TGA and QOI) in `Model_Part.textures`: the color (which multiplies the material's color), a normal map, metal and roughness (roughness in green and metal in blue, as glTF packs them, each multiplying the number of the material) and the light the surface gives (which replaces the color as what the glow is made of), with a normal map's strength and an alpha cutoff (glTF's `MASK`; `BLEND` is drawn solid, as the alpha of a color is). `Vertex3` gained the corner's `uv`, so a corner is 36 bytes and the shader's inputs are TEXCOORD9 (the corner's coordinates) beside the earlier ones. The scene shader has the pictures as samplers 1 to 4 of set 2 (0 is the shadow map) and a second small uniform block on set 3, binding 1, that tells it for each part how strongly its normal map bends the surface (0 when there is none), whether its glow is a picture, and its alpha cutoff, pushed again before each part; a part's missing picture is a stand-in (one white pixel, one flat-normal pixel) so the shader always has five bound and reads them all. The normal map needs no tangents in the mesh: the surface's sideways directions come from how the picture's coordinates run across it, from the screen-space derivatives of the world position and of the coordinates, with the picture's up where its v gets smaller (glTF's and OBJ's green is up). A model's textures are uploaded once with mipmaps (`image_texture` with `mipmaps`, made on the card by SDL and sampled with a linear, repeating, slightly anisotropic sampler), before the 3D pass begins, and are let go with the model. glTF names them through `textures` and `images` (a uri, a data URI or a buffer view of the glb itself; `texCoord` 0 only, and no `KHR_texture_transform`). **OBJ is read too** (`roci/gfx/obj.odin`, chosen by the `.obj` in the name): `v`, `vt`, `vn` and `f` (negative indices, `1//3` and `1/2` forms, a polygon cut into triangles from its first corner, which is right for convex ones), `usemtl` parts (the faces of one material are one part wherever they are in the file) and the `.mtl` it names with `mtllib`: `Kd`, `Ke`, `Ns` (as Blender writes it from the roughness, so roughness = 1 - sqrt(Ns / 1000)), `Pr` and `Pm`, and the pictures `map_Kd`, `map_Bump`, `bump` or `norm` (with `-bm`) and `map_Ke`, looked for beside the model whatever folder the file names. When a material has a `map_Kd` the picture gives the color and `Kd` is not used, because Blender writes a gray `Kd` beside every picture; an `.mtl` that names no picture has none, and a picture the sketch needs is never guessed from the folders around the model (Quaternius's own `.mtl` files name none, so `examples/model` has a one-line `striker.mtl` of its own). A picture that cannot be had leaves the part in its plain color and is said once as `Model_Texture`. **Not read**: Blender's `.blend` (a memory dump whose layout changes with each version of Blender, which no small reader follows: export glTF or OBJ from it), FBX (a closed binary format), the `KHR_materials_*` extensions the ninth slice does not read, and OBJ maps other than `map_Kd`, `map_Bump`, `map_Ke` and `map_d`. **Pictures on the solids** (2026-11-13): `fill picture` on a solid (or a `fill` statement that lasts) paints it: `scene_add` takes the picture, the item wears it for its color (white, so the picture is shown as it is), and every solid's mesh has texture coordinates (a box shows the whole picture on each face, a sphere and a capsule wrap it from pole to pole, a cone and a cylinder round their sides with the disc of an end seen from outside, a torus round the ring and the tube, an icosphere by longitude and latitude with the faces that cross the seam going past 1 where the picture repeats, a plane across it). The renderer draws a run of the instances of each solid for each picture they wear (`Shape_Run`, in place of a count for each shape), binds the picture as the color of a part with no other pictures and the stand-ins for the rest, and uploads it with mipmaps like a model's. Only the color picture is offered; a gradient as the fill of a solid is not used, and `as` words of a picture fill have no meaning on a solid. `examples/textured`.

**The tenth slice** (2026-11-14) gave the first `point` light shadows too, beside the sun's or spot light's map (each kind has one caster, so a sun and a point light both cast): the scene is drawn from the light six times, along +x, -x, +y, -y, +z and -z of the world, each with a 94 degree view (a little over a quarter turn so a softened edge never reads past its face), into the faces of one 3072 by 2048 `R32_FLOAT` texture laid three wide and two high (`point_faces` in `scene3.odin`, `draw_point_shadow_pass` moving the viewport between six draws of the same shadow shader). A cube map was the other choice and was turned down because every system's cube and cube-array render targets differ in SDL's GPU API and a flat atlas needs none of that. The scene shader picks the face by the axis the place is furthest along from the light, projects it with that face's matrix (passed as rows, 384 bytes, so the block is 3,600 bytes, under Metal's 4,096), turns the stored depth back into a distance along the axis (`near`, `far` are in the block) and compares distances, with the bias and the push along the normal growing with distance; reads are clamped inside the face so no tap reaches a neighbour. `examples/lamp`. The window's `shadows` doc and the sketches that had a point light (`shadows`, `model`, `voyage`) now show its shadow.

**The ninth slice** (2026-11-13) read the rest of what a model can say: each corner has its own color (`Vertex3.colour`, bytes that multiply the material's: glTF's `COLOR_0`, OBJ's `v x y z r g b`; white for the solids) and a second set of texture coordinates (`uv1`, glTF's `TEXCOORD_1`, which a picture asks for with `texCoord` 1), `KHR_texture_transform` moves a picture's coordinates (a 2 by 3 matrix for each of the four pictures, with the set it reads, in `Model_Part` and the part's uniform block), `KHR_materials_emissive_strength` (glow over 1), `KHR_materials_unlit` (a part drawn in its color alone), and `KHR_materials_ior` and `KHR_materials_specular` (how much a non-metal reflects, the shader's `part.w` in place of the fixed 0.04); clearcoat, transmission, sheen, volume, anisotropy and the older specular-glossiness workflow are left out. OBJ's `map_d` is merged into the color picture's alpha on the CPU and cuts at one half, and a face that is not convex is cut by ear clipping (`geom`) after being flattened along the largest part of its normal, a convex one still being a fan from its first corner. **`mesh` and wireframes** (2026-11-14): `mesh corners` draws a list of vectors of three numbers (a vector of two reads with z 0), three for each triangle, as a model: `gfx/mesh3.odin` makes `Model_Data` of one part with a flat normal for each triangle (a triangle with no area or no number is left out) and keeps it by the call that draws it, `mesh:` and the place of the call in the source and, when one call draws several in a loop, which time it was in the frame (`interp/meshes.odin`), so a list drawn again unchanged is sent to the card once and one that changed takes the place of the one before and is sent again. It is flat shaded in the fill and the current material, in the coordinates of the moment (no fitting, as `model` does). `as wireframe` draws its feature edges as tubes `thickness` across (at least a half; 1 unless set) in the fill with a ball at each corner, the shapes the turtle in space is made of, so they are lit and cast shadows; an edge is a feature when one triangle has it, three or more, or two whose normals differ by more than about a degree (`MESH_FLAT_COSINE`), so a square cut in two shows no diagonal and a box has twelve edges; the corners of two triangles are one when they agree to a thousandth. A wireframe stops at 60,000 edges. **Not done:** `wireframe` on the solids and models (their meshes are not marked with which edges are features: a sphere's quads are cut in two like any), per-corner colors and textures on a mesh and indexed meshes. **SVG import and the path shape** (2026-11-14): a path is a command of its own (`Kind.Path`, `roci/gfx/path.odin`): any number of contours of straight pieces, each open or closed, held in the list's `points` (a contour ends at a break, a point whose x is not a number and whose y says closed or open) with the box and the place of its points in the command, filled by the even-odd rule (a contour inside another is a hole) and stroked along each contour with the style's caps and joins, both through the mesh pipeline like a quad or an arc, and written to SVG and PDF files as paths (`f*` and `B*`). It has no verb of its own yet; `svg "logo.svg" at x y size w h` (`gfx/svg_import.odin`, `interp/svgs.odin`) is its first user: the file is read with `core:encoding/xml`, and each shape (paths with every command, rectangles with rounded corners, circles, ellipses, lines, polylines, polygons, in groups and `use`s, under any transform) becomes an item of contours (curves cut short at read time, 0.02 of a unit, arcs by the endpoint form of SVG's own appendix) with its fill, stroke, width, caps, joins and opacity, from attributes, `style` and the simple rules of a `<style>` (`tag`, `.class`, `#id`); the drawing is kept by file name and read again when the file's time changes, and drawn under the current transform in its own colors, with the sketch's style put back. Text, embedded pictures, masks, filters, patterns and markers are left out and said once as `Svg_Lost`; stroke width follows the transform. Fill rules and clips (2026-11-22): the GPU fills by even-odd nesting (`geom.triangulate`), so a nonzero item keeps `fill_contours`, the contours `geom.nonzero_boundaries` says edge the filled area (winding inside and outside a contour differ in being 0; exact for contours that cross neither themselves nor each other), and an item whose outline needs the rest is drawn as a fill and an outline apart; an item's clips become nested clip shapes made inside the sketch's own clip, a clip path being one shape of several parts (`set_clip_union`, `Command_List.clip_parts`: the parts are `Clip_Shape` commands one after another, written to the stencil in one batch, and since the stencil changes only where it is still one level short, overlapping parts make their union), made once a draw for each start of an item's clip list and shared. The SVG writer puts a path for each part in the clip path; the PDF writer makes them one clip path by the nonzero rule, each contour turned by how deep it sits in its own part. Gradients (2026-11-22) are drawn as the file has them: `svg_draw` turns each item's `Svg_Gradient` into a gradient of the kind `Placed_Linear` or `Placed_Radial` (`svg_gpu_gradient`), mixed in RGB, whose `place` is the transform from the point measured from the middle of the path's box over its half size to the gradient's own space (where a linear one is x and a radial one runs from `focus` to the unit circle), with `spread`; the opacity is multiplied into the stops' alpha, more than 16 stops are sampled to 16, a gradient of no length or radius is its last color, and one whose numbers pass what the GPU's 16-bit halves hold (60,000) falls back to the item's color. The SVG and PDF writers write them back with a gradient transform and a pattern matrix (a PDF cannot repeat or reflect, and says so). An extruded `svg` in space and text as outlines are not done.
- **Math**: Odin's built-in array programming, swizzles, matrices and quaternions, plus `core:math/linalg`. No external maths library.

### 4.4 Threads

Users never see threads. The core decides: rendering and windowing stay where SDL requires them (check SDL3 documentation for thread rules per subsystem; `TTF_CreateGPUTextEngine`, for example, must be called on the thread that created the GPU device). Audio runs on its own real-time thread. Asset loading is asynchronous inside `draw` and event blocks (decided 2026-10-24): a sketch using an image before it has loaded draws nothing for that frame rather than blocking. A `load` at the top level of a sketch waits until the file is ready, as Processing's `loadImage` does, so `photo.width` is never 0 in setup code. A file that fails to load yields an empty value (§5.2) in both cases. Machine-learning inference runs on worker threads the same way: a sketch reads the latest finished result and never waits for a model (§4.10). Odin's implicit `context` (allocator, logger) is set up per thread by the core.

### 4.5 Multi-window and messaging

- `window size w h`, `window size w h at x y title "t"` and `window size w h resizable false` (a window fixed at its size) return a window handle; the other settings (`bordered`, `topmost`, `smallest`, `largest`, `ratio`, `fps`, `alpha`, `focusable`, `visible`, `fullscreen`, `grab`, `parent`, `modal`, `cursor`) are words of the same call, and every one is also a property: it is read as a field (`w.width`, `w.height`, `w.title`, `w.at`, `w.mouse`, `w.focused` and the settings above) and changed later with `set` (`set control resizable false`, `set w size 400 300 topmost true`), which takes the words of `window` and the others a window has (`width`, `height`, `focused`, `maximized`, `minimized`); `mouse`, `hovered` and `covered` can only be read. `width` and `height` alone are the window the code runs in (decided 2026-10-28). A bare `title "t"` names the current window and `title` takes no window (decided 2026-10-29): another window is changed with `set w title "t"`. Assigning a property (`w.width = 400`) is a teaching error that shows the `set` line. A verb for each setting (`resizable w false`, `show w`, `ratio w 1.5`) was written first and dropped (2026-10-28), because each took a word from the sketch writer (`show`, `ratio`, `parent` and `visible` are ordinary names), and the dot assignment that replaced it made a window look like a record, so `set` reuses the words of `window` (2026-10-29). The words only a window uses (`registry.WINDOW_FREE_WORDS`) stay free as names, and the parser reads one as a connecting word only where a window call is due one. **Fullscreen on a Mac** (decided 2026-11-24): SDL's Cocoa driver puts a fullscreen window in a Space of its own, sliding in, so a window made full showed its first frames partly below the screen; SDL's hint `SDL_HINT_VIDEO_MAC_FULLSCREEN_SPACES` "0" fills the screen at once but is read when the video starts, for the whole run, and also takes the fullscreen button off resizable windows (https://wiki.libsdl.org/SDL3/SDL_HINT_VIDEO_MAC_FULLSCREEN_SPACES). So the checker notes a line that asks for fullscreen in its own text (`IR_Program.fullscreen`: `window` or `set` with `fullscreen true`, or the verb `fullscreen`), and both back ends call `roci.fullscreen_spaces(false)` before the first window (the interpreter's `start`, the compiled `setup`'s first line), which `platform.init` turns into the hint. A value worked out while the sketch runs keeps the Spaces; a reload that adds such a line changes nothing until the sketch starts again; C has `roci_fullscreen_spaces`. Chosen by Fabrizio over a word (`fullscreen true instant`) and over never using Spaces. Checked by hand by Fabrizio on a Mac (2026-11-25): a window made full fills the screen at its first frame, with no slide. `cursor` (`arrow`, the default, and `none` so far) is a setting like the others: SDL has one cursor for the whole program, so each frame it takes the setting of the window the mouse is over (`apply_cursor`). `fps` (decided 2026-11-24) is the most frames a second the sketch draws while the window is open (`window size 720 1280 fps 60`, `set w fps 30`, `w.fps`; 0 for no limit, at least 1 otherwise), because a window otherwise draws at its screen's refresh and a sketch that counts its time in frames moves faster on a 120 or 144 Hz screen; every window is drawn in each frame, so the lowest limit of the open windows holds the sketch, and bare `fps` stays the rate it is drawing at (the code map entry, Frame rate).
- Each window has its own `draw [ ... ]` block and command list.
- Windows communicate with `send <window> <value>` and `on <window> receive <name> [ ... ]`. Messages are values (numbers, text, lists, records), delivered on the next frame. Input events can name their window first (`on garden key "space"`, `on chooser mouse down`), and without one run in whichever window the event happened in (decided 2026-10-28). Internally: channels, no shared mutable state exposed. **What a message is** (decided 2026-10-28): the checker sees the whole program, so `msg` in `on w receive msg` has the type of every `send w ...` that names that window; a `send` to anything but a plain name could reach any window. Sends of different kinds make `msg` a mixed value that the handler tells apart while it runs, which is allowed because tagged messages are common. Two messages that are records of different fields (`send w { at: p }` and `send w { flash: 1 }`) have one shape with all the fields, so a handler reads the one it cares about and the others read 0 (§5.2).
- On mobile, multiple windows become stacked or tabbed views. Decide the exact behaviour in Phase 8 and document it here.

### 4.6 Audio

- Device I/O through SDL3 audio, so one platform layer covers every target. Verify SDL3's audio backend on FreeBSD and OpenBSD early.
- Decoding: miniaudio (public domain or MIT-0; in Odin's `vendor:`) has built-in decoders for WAV, FLAC and MP3. Vorbis comes from `stb_vorbis` (also in `vendor:`). **AIFF, Opus and AAC** (built 2026-11-16) are backends of our own for miniaudio's decoder (`vendor/sound/c/roci_aiff.h`, `roci_opus.h` and one for each system's AAC decoder), tried before its own and each refusing at once a file that is not its kind. AIFF and AIFF-C (PCM of 8 to 32 bits, big-endian or `sowt`, floats of 32 and 64) is read a piece at a time; Opus is read by our Ogg reader over libopus (BSD-3-Clause, compiled from the files `vendor/sound/c/opus_files.txt` lists into the same library, fetched by `tools/fetch-opus.sh`), the whole file held in memory and cut into packets, seeking from a quarter of a second before the place; AAC (`.m4a`, `.aac`) goes to AudioToolbox on macOS and iOS and MediaCodec on Android (both tried on a phone), Media Foundation on Windows (not yet compiled), and to the user's ffmpeg elsewhere (and where those cannot read a file), decoded whole, since no AAC decoder under a permissive licence exists. HE-AAC depends on the system's decoder.
- **Playing files** (built 2026-11-14, the first slice of Phase 5). `rain = load "rain.wav"` gives a sound (the checker reads the name's extension: `.wav`, `.flac`, `.mp3`, `.ogg`, in any case; a name worked out while the sketch runs is a picture, as before). A sound is one player, like a track in a music player: `play` plays it once from where it is (from its start when it had ended), `loop` over and over, `pause` holds it, `rewind` puts it back to its start or `by` seconds back, `forward rain by 10` moves it ahead (a second shape of the turtle's verb, told apart by the kind of its argument), and `set rain at 30 volume 50 pan -50 speed 1.5 loop false` changes it; `playing`, `at`, `length`, `volume`, `pan`, `speed` and `loop` are read as fields, and assigning one is `Set_Sound`, which shows the `set` line. `stop` is a keyword (it ends a taught verb), so pausing is the way to stop. Volume is 0 to 100, pan -100 to 100 (a balance: both sides at full volume in the middle, the far one fading towards a side), speed 0 to 8 (read between samples by a straight line, so it changes the pitch as a record player does). The decoders are not `vendor:miniaudio`'s library, which Odin ships for macOS and Windows only and expects inside Odin's own folder: `vendor/sound/c/roci_sound.c` includes miniaudio and stb_vorbis from the Odin install's `vendor/` sources with nothing but decoding switched on (no device, threads or engine), built by `tools/sound-libs.sh` into `.cache/sound` (on Android by the phones' `libs.sh`), so their versions are the pinned Odin's (miniaudio 0.11.26, its latest, checked 2026-11-14). A file is decoded whole on `load`, resampled to 48,000 frames a second in two channels, unless it is longer than 30 seconds (decided 2026-11-15): then it is **streamed**. The file stays open on a decoder (`ma_decoder_init_file`, converted by the decoder to the same 48,000 stereo floats; an MP3 gets a seek table made once at open, so a seek never decodes from the start), and a thread of the mixer's own reads it ahead of the sound into a ring of eight seconds, so a five-minute song costs about 3 MB and `load` does not wait for a decode. The audio thread only copies from the ring (a lock held for one chunk, no decoding, no disk): frames that are not there yet, after a seek far from what was read or when the thread is behind, are silence and the sound waits where it is, so music never skips. A looping stream is one long timeline (the thread seeks the decoder to the start where the file ends and goes on), so the seam has no gap; a seek keeps the ring when it holds the place and otherwise throws it away and refills from there (a few thousandths of a second of silence). A mixer with no device (a headless run, the tests) has no thread: each chunk it mixes reads what it needs first, so such a run is exact and may go faster than real time. A file saved again is opened again into the same sound. Seeks in a file whose rate is not 48,000 land within a few thousandths of a second of the place asked for (the decoders do not promise more), and a sound shorter than 30 seconds keeps exact positions because it is whole. The bytes of a file held in memory (`add_sound`, C `roci_load_sound`) stream the same way, from a copy the decoder keeps (`ma_decoder_init_memory`), so a long song loaded from bytes costs its compressed size and not 110 MB; seeking in Ogg Vorbis from memory was measured as fast as from a file (a ten-minute file, a seek to its last minute: 0.1 ms). The ring is 8 seconds unless `load "song.mp3" buffer 20` says another (1 to 60; `roci_load_sound_buffered` and `roci_load_sound_file_buffered` in C), read at the first load of a name; it costs about 384 KB a second, and a bigger one does not lengthen a seek's gap because the ring is thrown away and refilled from the place anyway. The mixer (`roci/audio`) is plain Odin with one lock, filled into SDL's default playback device on its audio thread (`SDL_OpenAudioDeviceStream`, two floats a frame; SDL converts to the device), opened as soon as a sketch with a window makes a sound to play (a `load` of a sound file that reads, a `synth`, a SoundFont, `speakers`) or plays one, so the wait for the device falls where the sketch is being set up and not in the middle of a frame (fixed 2026-11-19, issue #43: WILDVEX saw its first throw hang at the first splash; how long the open takes on each system is not measured); a headless run moves the sounds on by each frame's time instead, so tests read what a window would. Several sounds mix by adding; nothing limits the sum yet. One sound cannot overlap itself (a click played again while it plays goes on from where it is); a sketch that wants two at once loads the file twice, or rewinds it first to start it again.
- **Drawing with sound** (built 2026-11-14). `level` is how loud something is, 0 to 100 (the root mean square of the last 1,024 frames, scaled so a full-volume sine is 100), and `bands` how much of each pitch it holds: `count` numbers 0 to 100 (16 when not written, at most 256), in equal steps of pitch from 40 Hz to 16 kHz, each band as loud as its loudest frequency in decibels from 60 below full volume (0) to full volume (100). They are worked out from a 2,048-frame Hann-windowed transform (our own radix-2 FFT in `roci/audio/analysis.odin`); a band narrower than one frequency of the transform, as the lowest are, is read between the two frequencies around its middle pitch. The source is optional: `level` alone is everything that plays (the mix), `level rain` one sound (what it played, after its volume and pan), and `level mic` the microphone, a built-in value of kind sound that opens the default recording device the first time it is read in a sketch with a window (mono, 48,000 a second; never played through the speakers). What is measured is what was last mixed, a few hundredths of a second ahead of what is heard. `fft`, the plan's first word, was turned down as technical; `spectrum` was the other choice.
- **Instruments** (built 2026-11-14). `synth sine|triangle|square|saw|noise` gives a sound the sketch makes (`roci/audio/synth.odin`): a player with a wave instead of samples and up to 32 notes sounding together (a note asked for past that takes the quietest one's place). `play piano note 60` starts a note held until `pause piano note 60` (or `pause piano`, every note), and `for 0.5` makes it let itself go after that many seconds; `note` counts as MIDI does (69 is A at 440 Hz, a step is a semitone, fractions allowed) and `pitch` is hertz. Each note fades in over 5 ms and out over 80 ms, so nothing clicks, and plays at a quarter of full volume, so four notes reach it. Saw and square are smoothed where they jump (polyBLEP) against the whistle of frequencies above what 48,000 frames a second hold; noise comes from an xorshift generator of the instrument's own, the same on every run. `set piano pitch p` (or `note n`) slides every sounding note there and is the pitch a note played without one takes; `speed` multiplies it. A sound from a file played at a note is taken to be middle C (`speed` becomes the ratio), and `for` pauses it after that long. `pitch` and `note` are fields too. Its envelope is `attack`, `decay`, `sustain` and `release` (seconds and a percent; `synth saw attack 0.5 release 2` or `set piano release 2`), which on a SoundFont instrument are a floor over each region's own envelope, so a piano keeps its shape and can only be made to ring longer; a note that is taken for a new one when all 32 voices sound dies away under its envelope and never clicks. A filter on an instrument is the filter effect (`effect piano "filter"`).
- Synthesis and DSP: our own Odin DSP library (oscillators, filters, envelopes, effects, FFT), with simple user verbs. Bind a permissive C library only if writing our own proves unreasonable.
- MIDI in (built 2026-11-14) is our own on every desktop system, with no library: a USB keyboard is a raw MIDI device file on Linux (`/dev/snd/midiC*D*`, which ALSA makes for every port), FreeBSD (`/dev/umidi*`) and OpenBSD (`/dev/rmidi*`, owned by the group `_sndiop`: a user outside it gets `Permission denied`, the file is skipped without a message, and the sketch hears nothing, so the guide and `docs/building.md` tell OpenBSD users to join it), read by one thread that waits on none of them and looks for new ones once a second; macOS reads CoreMIDI (an input port connected to every source, again when the system says the setup changed) and Windows WinMM (every input device opened with a callback, all opened again when their number changes), both part of the system. The bytes go through one parser (running status kept, clock and active sensing passed over wherever they come, system exclusive, pitch bend, aftertouch and program changes read past) into notes down and up and controller moves, in a locked queue the sketch takes once a frame. The language has `on midi down`, `on midi up` and `on midi control` with the record `midi` (`note`, MIDI's number; `velocity` and `value` 0 to 100; `channel` 1 to 16; `control`), only inside those blocks (`Midi_Outside_Event`), and `midi control n` for a controller's latest value. Reading starts the first time a sketch asks for MIDI. MIDI out and the rest of what MIDI sends are below. **SoundFonts** (built 2026-11-15): `synth "upright.sf2" preset 48` (or `preset "Trumpet"`) makes an instrument from a SoundFont 2 file in the assets, read by our own reader (`roci/audio/soundfont.odin`, after the 2.04 specification; no library, like glTF and OBJ): the RIFF's 16-bit samples and its tables flattened, for each preset, into regions that hold a sample with its key and velocity ranges, loop, root key and tuning (coarse, fine, the sample's own correction, scale tuning), attenuation, pan and volume envelope (delay with attack, hold, decay, sustain, release), an instrument's global zone giving defaults and a preset's zones adding to them. A note plays every region that covers its key and the instrument's volume taken as velocity, reading the sample between frames by a straight line and looping while the region loops; the release fades from wherever the envelope was. Since 2026-11-16 the filter (cutoff and resonance, a two-pole low pass), the modulation envelope, the modulation and vibrato LFOs (pitch, cutoff and volume), key scaling of the hold and decay, a fixed key or velocity, exclusive classes and modulators are read too: a region keeps the modulators that apply to it (the instrument's, the default of velocity to the cutoff, and the preset's added on), a note works them out once when it starts from its velocity and key and the instrument's wheels, and the pitch, the LFOs and the filter's coefficients are worked out every 32 frames and held. `set piano bend 50 vibrato 30` moves the pitch wheel (-100 to 100, two semitones each way, on any instrument) and the modulation wheel (0 to 100, which swells the vibrato LFO by up to 50 cents, the default modulator's); a modulator that reads a wheel is worked out at the note's start, not live. The default modulators for volume, pan and the reverb and chorus sends are not applied (the instrument's own settings and the effects do those). Reverb and chorus sends, stereo links and 24-bit samples are not read. A missing preset is the warning `Preset_Missing`, which lists the presets, and the first one plays; a file that is not a SoundFont is `Soundfont_Unreadable`. The file is read whole when the instrument is made and kept by name and preset like a sound. **Effects** (built 2026-11-15): `effect guitar "delay" by { time: 0.3, feedback: 40, mix: 30 }` puts a sound effect on a sound (the picture effects' verb, with a sound first), and saying it again changes its settings in place, so an echo goes on through the change and a setting can follow the mouse in `draw`; `no effect guitar "delay"` takes it off; `speakers` is the whole mix as a value, whose effects run over the sum of every sound after their own. Sixteen effects, all our own code in `roci/audio/effects.odin`: overdrive (a soft tanh clip), fuzz (a hard, lopsided clip with its offset taken away), delay (a line of up to two seconds, its time gliding so a change does not click), reverb (Jezar's Freeverb, eight combs and four allpasses a side), chorus and flanger (modulated delays), phaser (six swept all-pass stages with feedback), tremolo, compressor and limiter (a peak follower), eq (low shelf at 200 Hz, a peak at 1 kHz, high shelf at 4 kHz, from the RBJ cookbook), rotary (a spinning horn: pan, loudness and a moving delay for its Doppler shift), filter (low, high or band pass), bitcrush, ring modulation and an auto-wah. Units are those of the rest: amounts 0 to 100, seconds, times a second, hertz, and eq bands -100 to 100 for 12 decibels either way, so no decibel shows. A sound is mixed alone through its effects before it joins the others, and keeps running through them after it stops for as long as their sound lasts (a delay until its echoes are a thousandth as loud, a reverb up to seven seconds). A name no effect has is `Sound_Effect_Missing`, a setting it has not (or a filter kind that is no kind) the warning `Effect_Setting_Unknown`. **Limiter** (2026-11-15): what goes to the speakers passes `soft_limit` (`roci/audio/mixer.odin`): untouched below 0.8, bent smoothly towards 1 above it, so a loud chord or several loud sounds never reach the device above full scale, where it would clip hard and crackle; `mix` itself is not limited, so analysis and tests see the true sum. A SoundFont note also comes in over at least 3 ms and lets go over at least 20 ms, whatever its region asks. **Latency**: the speakers are opened with a 256-frame buffer (5.3 ms; SDL's own choice on PipeWire was 1,024, 21.3 ms), which `SDL_AUDIO_DEVICE_SAMPLE_FRAMES` in the environment overrides for a computer that crackles; `ROCI_LATENCY=1` prints when a run ends how long notes waited until their block ran, how many ran early, the buffer and the largest chunk the mixer was asked for. **MIDI blocks run while the loop waits for the screen** (2026-11-15): the loop asks for each window's next swapchain texture before building the frame, without blocking (SDL's `AcquireGPUSwapchainTexture`, which gives no texture while too many frames are on their way), and in the meantime runs the `on midi` blocks of notes as they are read, every 0.5 ms; the window's next frame is begun first, so what a block draws is in the frame that is then built, and those notes are not run again in it. The frame rate is unchanged (measured: the 100,000-circle benchmark and a light sketch at the screen's 105 frames a second). Before, a note waited in the queue until the next frame began, 4.6 ms on average with an MPK mini 3. That covers drivers that wait in the acquire (Mesa's); NVIDIA's waits inside the submit instead, which SDL requires on the thread that made the window, so **while a sketch reads MIDI the loop also starts each frame late**: it sleeps after a frame until just before the next refresh, less the time a frame takes to build and a margin of 1.5 ms or more, running MIDI blocks as notes arrive, then builds the frame, whose submit then has little to wait for. The refresh is learned from the frames; a frame that misses its refresh (shown twice) widens the margin by a millisecond, which narrows again while frames are on time. Measured with an MPK mini 3 on NVIDIA: 0.7 ms on average from a note read to its block running (4.5 before), about 8.7 ms from a key to its sound leaving the computer, the frame rate unchanged and at most one missed refresh in 400 frames. Sketches that do not read MIDI, and windows without vsync, are paced as before. The plan was `vendor:portmidi` (below); it was turned down because on Linux and the BSDs it is a system library `roci` would need at start, against non-negotiable 2, and it has no phone support.
- MIDI: candidate `vendor:portmidi` (MIT-style licence, verified 2026-10-24). Its README names Linux (ALSA), a minimal sndio mode for FreeBSD, OpenBSD and NetBSD, Haiku, and says nothing of iOS or Android, so mobile needs our own backends (CoreMIDI on iOS, Android's MIDI API), and FreeBSD's sndio mode lacks timestamped output and virtual devices. Verify its FreeBSD, OpenBSD (sndio's own home), iOS and Android support before Phase 5; fill gaps with our own backends if needed.
- **MIDI out and recording** (built 2026-11-15, our own). `midi note 60 velocity 80 channel 1 for 0.5` sends a note (velocity 0 to 100, channel 1 to 16; with `for` the note is let go by the sketch's own frames, and every sounding note is let go when the sketch ends), `midi release` lets notes go, `midi set 7 to 100` a controller, `midi bend`, `midi program`, `midi to "name"` chooses the output by part of its name (the first one before), `midi outputs` lists them, `midi clock 120` sends start and then 24 ticks a beat from a thread of its own with a precise sleep (a new number changes the tempo) and `midi stop` ends it. MIDI in also reads `on midi bend`, `program`, `pressure`, `start` and `stop` (`midi.bend` -100 to 100, `midi.program`, `midi.pressure`), and an incoming clock becomes `midi tempo` in beats a minute. The backends are CoreMIDI destinations, WinMM output devices and the raw device files opened for writing on Linux, FreeBSD and OpenBSD, opened by the first send; a missing output is said once (`Midi_Out_Missing`) and the sketch goes on. Only CoreMIDI was run here; WinMM and the BSDs' output are for the release checklist. `record speakers "take.wav"` (or a sound, or `mic`) writes what plays into a file: the audio thread copies the mix, a sound after its effects or the microphone into a ring that another thread writes as a WAV of our own (16-bit, two channels, 48,000 a second) or sends to the user's ffmpeg as floats for `.mp3`, `.ogg`, `.flac` and `.m4a`; the speakers are taken after the limiter, so a take is what is heard. `no record speakers`, the end of the sketch or another take into the same source ends it, and the console says how many seconds it holds. Tests of a take are exact because a headless mixer drains inside `mix`. A buffer size per streamed sound is `load "song.mp3" buffer 20`.
- OSC (built 2026-11-15, our own): `on osc "/fader/*" [ ... ]` runs for every message sent to a matching address (a `*` is any text, a `?` any one character, `[1-3]` one of those and `[!1-3]` any other, `{red,green}` either word, all inside one part of the address; no address means every message), with the record `osc`: `address`, `from` (the sender's address), `value` (the first number, true and false counting as 1 and 0, otherwise 0), `text` (the first text) and `args` (everything it carries in order: numbers, text, true and false, and a blob of bytes as a list of numbers). `osc send "/note" [ 60 "hi" true ] to "studio.local" port 9000` sends one (`to` starts as this computer, `port` as 9000): a whole number that fits an int32 goes as an integer, any other as a float32, text as a string, true and false as `T` and `F`, a list of numbers as a blob of bytes; `osc send [ [ "/a" 1 ] [ "/b" "x" ] ]` sends several messages as one bundle (due at once), and a host of `"255.255.255.255"` reaches every computer on the network. `osc listen 8000` chooses the port to listen on and `osc value "/fader/1"` is the latest first number sent to an address, 0 until one comes; the first `on osc` or `osc value` listens on 9000. The packets are read by `roci/osc` (messages and bundles, which are flattened in order and run as if due now; int32, int64, float32, float64, strings, symbols, characters, true and false and blobs are kept; timetags, MIDI and color arguments, nil and infinity are left out and said once as `Osc_Unsupported`, an array's brackets are skipped) and sent and received over UDP through Odin's `core:net`, a thread of its own reading a bound non-blocking socket into a queue as MIDI does. A damaged packet is dropped whole and never read past its end; a port another program has is `Osc_Port_Busy` and the sketch goes on, and a host that cannot be found is `Osc_Host_Unknown`. UDP says nothing of messages that do not arrive. Bundle timetags (a bundle is always taken as due now), TCP and SLIP and replies to the sender are the other choices, not done.

### 4.7 Execution model (decided 2026-10-22, revised 2026-10-23)

**One front end, two back ends.**

- **Front end (shared).** Lexer, parser, checker and type inference produce a **typed IR**. All errors users see come from here, so they are identical in both modes.
- **Development back end: interpreter.** `roci run` executes the typed IR directly. It owns hot reload, state preservation across saves and the error overlay. It is part of the `roci` binary and needs no toolchain. The core is linked into `roci`, so `roci run` works on a machine with nothing else installed.
- **Release back end: Odin code generation.** `roci package` translates the typed IR into Odin source and builds it, together with the core's Odin source (the core ships as Odin source and is compiled with every sketch, decided 2026-10-24; C and C++ dependencies such as SDL3, Dear ImGui, accesskit-c and LiteRT ship prebuilt per target), using the user's installed Odin compiler (LLVM-based, so the whole program is optimised together), for the chosen target. Odin's Android and iOS subtargets cover mobile. No interpreter ships in released sketches. Generated Odin must be readable: one Odin procedure per verb, names derived from the sketch.
- **Toolchain for packaging only** (decided 2026-10-23, revised 2026-10-24). rocinante does not bundle compilers or linkers. `roci package` needs:
  - **Odin**, at the version pinned by this rocinante release (`ODIN_VERSION`, §3), which `roci` fetches into its own cache on first use, like the Android SDK. It downloads the official release archive from Odin's GitHub releases, checks its checksum and never touches an Odin the user installed. Odin is BSD-3-Clause, so fetching it is within §3. Odin publishes no FreeBSD archive, so on FreeBSD `roci` uses the system's own Odin, the `odin-lang` package (port `lang/odin`), when its version is the pin (decided 2026-10-30, replacing the source build as the first choice; the port was at `dev-2026-10` that day): `roci doctor` reads `odin version` and, when the package is missing, prints `pkg install odin-lang`. A port carries one version and moves on, so when the package is at another version `roci` builds the pinned release from Odin's source tag into its cache instead (decided 2026-10-24): clone the tag, then `LLVM_CONFIG=llvm-config19 gmake release-native` (confirmed in Phase 0; it needs git, GNU make and the `llvm19` package, because the base system's clang has no `llvm-config`), once per Odin version, cached. `roci doctor` checks for those and prints the `pkg install` line. The `dev-2026-11` release (checked 2026-11-06) ships archives for the same four, Linux amd64 and arm64, macOS arm64 and Windows amd64 only: no FreeBSD, no Windows arm64, and macOS Intel was dropped in `dev-2026-10` (`dev-2026-09` still had it). FreeBSD is handled by its package or the source build above. OpenBSD has neither an archive nor a package, so there `roci` always builds the pinned release from source the same way (decided 2026-11-01), with `gmake` and the `llvm%19` package's `llvm-config`. Windows arm64 and macOS Intel are not targets of the first stable release (decided 2026-10-26, §10); they are on the roadmap with arm64 FreeBSD and Linux (README.md). A user-installed Odin of the pinned version is used when it is on the path and the cache is empty, and `roci doctor` says which one is in use;
  - **Windows**: MSVC and the Windows SDK from Visual Studio's "Desktop development with C++" component, which Odin requires;
  - **macOS**: the Xcode command-line tools; **iOS**: full Xcode and an Apple Developer Program account, on a Mac only (§7);
  - **Linux, FreeBSD and OpenBSD**: clang, which Odin uses for linking;
  - **Android** (decided 2026-10-25): the SDK, the NDK and a JDK, which `roci` fetches into its cache on first use (§7), like Odin. The JDK is only a build tool, for `javac`, `d8` and `apksigner` (the Android spike, `docs/phases.md`), and nothing from it ships in a sketch, so its GPL-with-Classpath-Exception licence (Eclipse Temurin; to be confirmed against its licence file when it is first used) is within §3. Temurin 21 (LTS) has builds for Linux x64 and arm64, macOS x64 and arm64 and Windows x64 and arm64 (checked 2026-10-25). Google's NDK has hosts for Windows, macOS and Linux x86-64 only, so **`roci package android` runs on those three hosts, not on FreeBSD, OpenBSD or arm64 Linux**; on those, `roci doctor` says so and suggests packaging on another machine.
  `roci doctor` checks all of this per target and prints the exact install steps for the user's OS. Packaging failures are reported in rocinante's plain style, never as raw compiler output; the raw log is saved to `output/` for the curious.
- **Determinism rules** (non-negotiable 7). Both back ends use the same maths: no fast-math and no contraction of multiply-adds in either (Odin and C flags set once in the build script), one shared implementation of the random generator, noise and the trigonometric functions that both call, and no iteration order that depends on addresses or hashing. **Every run has a seed** (decided 2026-10-27): random unless the sketch sets it with `seed`, shown by `roci run` on request and stored in the metadata of exports, so the same seed gives the same picture. The one random generator and noise implementation live in `roci/` from its first commit. A new built-in that breaks one of these fails the equivalence suite (§9).
- **Stable verb ABI.** Generated code and the interpreter call core verbs through the same functions, so behaviour stays identical (non-negotiable 7). **The verb registry is data** (decided 2026-10-27): the ABI is also a table holding each verb's name, parameters and kinds, connecting words, whether it gives a value, and a one-line description. The checker's "did you mean", the reserved-word list (§5.15), the docs reference and language-server completion are all generated from it, so none can drift from the core.
- **No bytecode VM, no JIT.** A third back end would be one more thing to keep equivalent. Revisit only if the interpreter proves too slow for development use before Phase 7.
- **Optimisation passes** (decided 2026-10-27, from Goose's optimisations, https://aardappel.github.io/goose/, read 2026-10-27). They run over the typed IR in a stage shared by both back ends (`lang.optimise`, decided 2026-10-29), after the checker and before either back end runs: the interpreter runs the optimised IR, and `codegen` generates Odin from it, using Odin's own controls (`#no_bounds_check`, `#force_inline`, `#soa`, allocators) where they apply. Each pass can be switched off (`ROCI_PASSES=off` turns them all off). The interpreter with every pass off is the reference: a test runs every example with the passes off and on and compares every frame and every printed line, from Phase 2, and the equivalence suite (§9) compares it with the compiled sketch from Phase 7. A pass only adds what it has proved, so a node it leaves alone runs the general way. The first pass, `Typed_Values`, marks the nodes that give a number (`Sure_Number` when the value can never be empty: a literal, arithmetic, a negation, a maths verb) or true or false, and the interpreter works those out as a plain `f64` or `bool` (`roci/interp/fast.odin`). The second, `Field_Slots` (decided 2026-11-11), finds where a field is in a record of a known shape, for each field read (`IR_Field.at`), record literal (`IR_Record.slots`) and field assignment (`IR_Assign.first`): the interpreter indexes the record's fields when the record it meets is of that shape (a comparison of two numbers) and asks by name when it is not, so a record of another shape that fits a verb, and a value the checker could not type, behave as they did; it made `examples/particles` about 3% faster, the lookups by name having been a tenth of its instructions on Linux. Each pass keeps behaviour identical to the interpreter (non-negotiable 7), and the equivalence suite (§9) is how it proves that. Adopted:
  - **Monomorphise everything:** one version of each verb per record shape it is used with (§5.3).
  - **Blocks become plain loops:** a block is passed to a verb and run by it, never stored as a value (§5.2), so every block is known when the sketch is checked. `keep`, `skip`, `collect`, `combine`, `find`, `sort … by`, `for each`, `repeat`, `every` and event blocks are generated as plain loops and procedures with their captured variables passed explicitly, never as calls through a function value. This is the biggest win, and Odin has no closures, so it is needed anyway.
  - **Construct in place:** record and vector literals are written straight into the list's storage or the destination variable, also through `give`, instead of being built and copied.
