  - **Paths and SVG import** (`path.odin`, `svg_import.odin`, `interp/svgs.odin`): a `Kind.Path` command keeps its contours in `Command_List.points` (a contour ends at a break point, x not a number, y 1 for closed), its box and where its points start in the command, so the command stays 64 bytes; `mesh_add_command` takes the path's points as an argument (the batcher passes `path_points`), and a new `Command_List` array must be added to `begin_frame`, `destroy`, `mark` and `rewind` as `points` was. `svg_read` uses Odin's `core:encoding/xml` and keeps an `Svg_Drawing` in an arena of its own (`svg_destroy`); `interp` keeps one for each name and reads it again when the file's time changes. A drawing is in the file's own units with its top left at 0, 0, and `svg_draw` moves and grows the list's transform to fit it (the stroke width goes through that transform).
  - **Painting with no GPU** (`raster.odin`, 2026-11-22, for app icons, which `roci package` makes where there may be no graphics card): `raster_svg` paints an `Svg_Drawing` into a `Raster` (premultiplied sRGB floats, mixed as SVG and browsers mix them). The reader keeps for it what `svg` does not draw: each item's fill rule (`nonzero`; `svg` fills even-odd), linear and radial gradients instanced per item (which `svg` draws as well, as it does the fill rules and clips; `Svg_Gradient.to_gradient` maps drawing units into the gradient's space through the element's transform, its box when the units are the box's, and `gradientTransform`; attributes and stops come down an `href` chain; spread pad, reflect, repeat; the focal point is pulled just inside the circle, as SVG 1.1 says), and clip paths in user units (`Svg_Clip`, shapes with their own clip rule, read by `read_clip` with the reader's `capture` set so `emit` collects contours instead of items). `lost` says what `svg` leaves out (it draws the gradients, fill rules and clips too, since the same day, so `lost` and `raster_lost` now say the same); `raster_lost` says what even this leaves out (text, pictures, masks, filters, patterns, markers, clips measured by the box or clipped again). Coverage (`cover_edges`): 16 rows a pixel row, each crossing the edges at exact points, the winding (nonzero or even-odd) deciding the runs, each run adding the exact part of each pixel it covers; strokes are `geom.stroke_polyline`'s triangles, all wound one way, so their nonzero union is the stroke. `raster_image` scales a picture by the area each pixel covers (so a 1024-pixel PNG made 16 pixels keeps its detail), and `raster_apply_mask`, `raster_extend`, `raster_flatten`, `raster_rounded_rect` and `raster_circle` are the icons' shapes. A full set of about 57 icons takes about 3 seconds in a default build and 0.6 at `-o:speed`, PNG filtering being some 40% of it.
  - **Vector files** (`svg.odin`, `pdf.odin`, `truetype.odin`): written from the command list with no GPU; `text_lines` breaks a wrapped text where SDL_ttf does; the PDF has text as glyph outlines (TrueType `glyf` fonts only, CFF text is said and left out).
  - **Blend and clip** are part of a look (hashed); a change of either starts a new batch; one pipeline per (program, blend); `set_clip` keeps the upright box round the transformed corners; `draw_overlay` puts both back first. No `darken` (a minimum colour would turn clear corners black).
  - `renderer.odin`, `png.odin`: pipelines are made on first use and a failure is recorded once; drawing is 4x multisampled, premultiplied. PNG is **sancho** (own repo, 0BSD), vendored as a plain copy in `vendor/sancho` with the commit in `vendor/sancho/SOURCE`: a change is made in sancho and copied with `tools/vendor-sancho.sh [ref]`. Shaders are HLSL in `shaders/` with SPIR-V, Metal and DXIL committed in `shaders/compiled/`; Metal needs texture-upload offsets that are multiples of 16.
- 3D (`roci/gfx/scene3.odin`, `renderer_scene.odin`, `roci/scene3.odin`, `shaders/scene3.hlsl`; the design is in §4.3): the scene is plain data beside the 2D list and the renderer draws it in a pass of its own before the 2D pass. **Metal trap**: SPIRV-Cross numbers an MSL shader's textures by first use, not by the HLSL registers, so the scene shader read its normal map and its metal-and-roughness picture swapped on a real Metal GPU (roughness came out half, so every highlight was too sharp) while lavapipe was right; `shaders/compile.sh` gives `scene3` `--msl-decoration-binding`, and a new shader that reads its pictures in another order than it declares them needs the same. **Trap**: a fragment shader cannot read the vertex stage's uniform block (SDL puts fragment uniforms on another set), so anything the fragment stage needs rides along as a varying; reading it anyway crashed lavapipe inside its JIT with no message. The orbit is `Win.mouse_read`-gated: any verb that reads the mouse must call `read_mouse`. Loaded models (`gfx/gltf.odin`, `obj.odin`, `models.odin`, `interp/models.odin`) are read by our own glTF and OBJ readers (`model_read` picks by the file's extension), kept per thread by path with a version, and drawn from buffers of their own (`Model_Group`s in `renderer_scene.odin`); a test helper that builds JSON with `fmt` must double its braces, as `tools/model` does. **Model pictures** are `Image_Id`s of the ordinary picture store held in `Model_Part.textures` and let go by `model_data_destroy`; the scene shader reads four of them on samplers 1 to 4 (stand-ins for a missing one) and a per-part uniform block pushed before each part draws; they are uploaded with mipmaps before the 3D pass begins, because a copy pass cannot open inside a render pass. `tests/models/ship.glb` (made by `tools/model`) is the glTF fixture of the interpreter tests, and `tests/golden/model_scene.odin` paints a model made in code with every kind of picture.
- `tests/golden`: the pictures of lit 3D examples are listed in `SHADED_PICTURES` (`examples.odin`) and judged by looser limits on lavapipe (worst channel 40, 60 pixels past 12), because the per-pixel light differs a little between versions of lavapipe's compiler (the first ORION run found a pixel 17 off against references drawn on a Mac); add a new lit 3D example to that list. `scenes.odin` is where a new golden scene goes; `examples.odin` runs the examples at a fixed seed and compares each window with `example_<name>_<window>.png`; an example with no picture goes in `NO_PICTURE` with a reason. A window with no reference is said and does not fail.
- `roci/toml`: the subset of TOML `ui.toml`, `memory.toml` and `sketch.toml` use (arrays of text, mixed arrays and tables in curly brackets too; tables, `key = value` with a bare or quoted key, text, whole and decimal numbers, booleans, one-line arrays of numbers), read without a library and written back in the order given; a line it cannot read is a `Problem` with its number and is left out, never an error that stops a sketch. A decimal is always written with a point, so what is read back is the same kind.
- `roci/data`: data files read and written without a library (§5.19): `parse_json`, `parse_table` (CSV and TSV), `parse_toml` (over `roci/toml`) into a `Datum` (nothing, true or false, a number, text, a list, an `Object` whose members keep the file's order), `write_json`, `write_table`, `write_toml`, and `format_of`, `parse` and `write` by the end of a file's name. A reader gives a `Problem` with the line and the reason in plain words and never stops at a mistake it can say; JSON that goes past `MAX_DEPTH` is a problem, not a crash. `field_name` is how a key or a column title becomes the name of a field (lowercase, `_` between words, `_` before a leading digit, `column_n` when nothing is left). It knows nothing of the language: `roci/lang/check_data.odin` gives a file its type and `roci/interp/data.odin` its values. It also reads Excel and OpenDocument files (`zip.odin`, `xml.odin`, `office.odin`, §5.19).
- Scenes (`roci/lang/check_scene.odin`, `roci/interp/scenes.odin`, `roci/gfx/commands.odin` `hold`, `roci/loop.odin` end hook): §5.18. The interpreter registers a scene's blocks tagged with its place (`it.registering`) and runs only the current scene's; `go`, `visit` and `return` only set `scene_pending`, which `end_hook` applies after every window has finished its frame. `roci.hold_frames` keeps a window's last finished frame under a scene that is visited over (the loop calls `begin_frame_from` instead of `begin_frame`).
- `memory.toml` (`roci/memory.odin`, `roci/abi_memory.odin`, `roci/interp/memory.odin`, `roci/lang/check_memory.odin`): what `remember` keeps (§5.17). The store lives in `roci` (so reload keeps it), is written like `ui.toml` (a temp file renamed over, after a second of calm, at exit and from the platform's background watch), and headless runs read it and never write it. `roci/toml` reads and writes it with `exact` set (`nan`, `inf`); the interface files do not. The codec in `interp` reads a value back as the kind the checker settled for the `recall` expression (`e.type`), and the checker types a name from every `remember` and `start` (`mem_types`).
- `vault.bin` (`roci/vault.odin`, `cli/vault.odin`): the sealed twin of `memory.toml` (§5.17): `vault_seal` and `vault_open_bytes` are pure (a test flips every byte), `vault_persist` keeps a running sketch's, `keep_beside` opens both stores of a sketch folder.
- `ui.toml` (`roci/ui_persist.odin`, `roci/ui/persist.odin`): `roci run` of a `sketch.roci` remembers the widgets' values in `ui.toml` beside it (`roci.ui_persist`; a lone file, `--frames` runs, tests and the golden images never read or write one). A table for each window (`[ui]`, `[ui2]`...) and a line for each widget under its readable name (panel and tab path, text, `#n` in a loop); a drop-down, radio group or list is remembered by the item's text, a color as `"#rrggbbaa"`. Values are taken as a widget is first made (`ui.set_loaded`), written when the sketch ends and after a second of stillness (`ui_touch`, `ui_autosave`, `ui_flush`), through a `.part` file and a rename. What the file says of widgets not made this run, and of windows not open, is kept when it is written; `forget ui` drops it. Every `ui_*_value` procedure has to `mark` its kind, or its value is not written.
- `roci/ui` (the interface of one window, package imports `vendor/imgui` and `roci/platform`, no sketch language): `State` is a Dear ImGui context made by the first widget (`roci/ui_window.odin` makes it, feeds it the window's events as ImGui input events, finishes the frame after the window's `draw` and gives the renderer `Renderer.overlay` so the interface is drawn in a pass of its own over the finished frame, effects included, also in `picture`). No ImGui SDL backend: events are fed by hand so a headless window can be clicked by a test; only the SDL_GPU renderer backend is used (made on first draw, again when the device or target format changes). Widgets are procedures that return what the user did and leave a `Record` (role, label, value, rect, focus) for screen readers and tests. Traps: the panel (`rocinante`, top left, autosizing) shows its widgets from its **third** frame; ImGui trickles input, so a click is seen over two or three frames; a test helper must not short-circuit (`ok ||= widget()` skips the widget); a font passed to `create` must outlive the state. A widget that keeps a value (`kept`) holds it in the state by its text, which is what survives a reload. `State.target_scale` is set by `picture` so a window laid out at its own density is drawn across a picture of another scale. An `image button` leaves `IMAGE_TEXTURE_FLAG | picture number` as its texture id and `ui.draw` swaps it (`patch_pictures`, with the renderer's `gfx.image_texture` through `Texture_Of`) just before the pass; a picture with no texture is drawn with zero elements.
  - **Screen-reader tree and actions** (`access.odin`, `actions.odin`; platform-free, the bridge to AccessKit is in `roci/access*.odin` below): `build_tree` turns `State.finished` into a `Tree` of plain `Node`s (role, name, value, state, `actions`, bounds), nesting what is made inside a panel, tab or menu by the record's `path`; node ids are the item ids lifted past the small ones (a line of text has none, so its place in the frame), unique and steady from frame to frame (tested); `dump_tree` is the text the examples' stored trees (`tests/ui-tree`) are compared with, with no positions in it. An action (`push_action`, from any thread, or `roci.ui_access_action` in tests) waits in a locked queue and `begin_frame` makes it the frame's; the widget it names takes it (`take`, `nudge`) before it draws and does what a hand would have: click, step, set a number or text, focus (in `note`), select a tab or radio button or list item, fold a panel. A node's `actions` must only list what a widget really takes.
  - **The screen-reader bridge** (`roci/access.odin`, `access_darwin.odin`, `access_unix.odin`, `access_windows.odin`, `access_other.odin`): `ensure_ui` makes an `Access` (heap, shared with the adapter's callbacks) for a window with a handle when the library is there; `ui_finish` builds the tree, hashes it (`ui.tree_hash`) and calls `platform_access_update` only when the hash changed; `access_factory` serves AccessKit a locked copy of the stored tree, never the live state, and `access_action` queues a request (`asked_of`, `ui.push_action`) for the next frame. Locks are never held across a call into the adapter. macOS: `macos_subclassing_adapter_for_window` on SDL's `NSWindow` (plus the focus forwarder on `SDL3Window`, once). Linux, FreeBSD, OpenBSD: `unix_adapter_new` (AT-SPI over D-Bus; its handlers run on the adapter's own threads, so everything they touch is behind `Access.lock`), told where the window is on every update and move (`platform_access_bounds`). Windows: `windows_subclassing_adapter_new` on the `HWND`, which **must be made before the window is first shown and stops the program if it is visible**, so `platform_access_attach` hides a showing window for the call (one blink at start); bounds there are device pixels, so `Access.scale` multiplies them. Other systems have no adapter (`access_other.odin`). **Traps**: Orca speaks a change only from what it holds as the focus, so a record that does not say it has focus is silent when changed (`orca --replace --debug-file=FILE` shows why), and it speaks a progress bar at most every 10 seconds; AccessKit takes a radio button's state from `checked`, not `selected` (ARIA). Tests cover the tree, the actions and the conversion; a real screen reader is checked by hand (VoiceOver, Orca, Narrator and NVDA all on `examples/ui`; which packages each system needed is in design.md §4.8). On Windows, driving UI Automation from PowerShell (`docs/release-checklist.md`, 4b) checks the tree and actions before anyone listens.
  - **Keyboard** (`input.odin`, `keyboard_test.odin`): an arrow key pressed while a slider, knob, range, pad or number box (not being typed in) has focus is kept from Dear ImGui's navigation (`takes_arrow`) and moves the value (`State.arrows`, `arrow_step`), right and up adding. Traps: `EndGroup` adds an item of no identity, so after any group (ours, or one inside a Dear ImGui widget: `InputScalar` with steps, `ColorEdit4`, `ListBox`) `is_item_focused` is false, so focus is read before the group closes (the number box and color picker are built from their parts for this, and the list calls `end_child`, then `end_group`); a radio group, list or open drop-down gives the item with focus as `Record.focus_item`, and so do a range (its handle) and a pad (its axis, `State.pad_axis`), which the tree shows as a group of two sliders (`part_slider`, `Record.second` the other value); `InvisibleButton` is no stop for the keyboard without `ButtonFlags_EnableNav`; a click on a number box's step leaves Dear ImGui with no focus, so the box claims it (`State.claimed`) until the keyboard focuses something; Tab turns a Dear ImGui slider into a box of text, so ours have `SliderFlags_NoInput`.
  - **Placing**: `ui.place_next` (from the interpreter's `place_widget`, through `roci.ui_place`) says where the next widget or group goes; `place` opens a floating Dear ImGui window for a widget with `at` (closed lazily by the next widget, so a `tip` still finds it) and `begin_container` one for a group (closed by `end_container`); the one panel is opened only by a widget that is not placed (`ensure_frame` no longer opens it). `Win.in_draw` makes `ui_place` apply the transform, and `Interp.block_depth` makes a `ui` statement inside a running block (`draw`, event) run at once instead of registering.
  - **The language side** (`ui [ ]`, `label`, `button`, `toggle`, `slider`): `ui` is the `Draw` statement with `ui` set (parsed by `parse_draw`, run after `draw` by `Win.ui_draw`, the interpreter keeps `Interp.uis` beside `draws`); the widget verbs (`label` to `forget ui`, 28 of them with `ui theme`, containers included: `panel`, `row`, `column`, `tabs`, `tab`, `menu`) are `LANGUAGE_VERBS` rows with no Odin procedure of their own in the registry or C ABI, answered by `interp/widgets.odin` through `roci.ui_*`. all of them and `ui` are reserved words now. Typed text reaches the interface as `platform.Text_Input` (held in the event, 63 bytes at most, cut at a whole character) and a window with a text box in use asks SDL for it (`platform.text_input`); the fonts for other scripts are added to the interface font only when such text is first typed or shown (`add_fallback_fonts`).
- `vendor/imgui` and `tools/imgui`: Dear ImGui as Odin. `imgui.odin` is **generated** (`tools/imgui` reads dear_bindings' JSON; edit the generator's `WANTED` list to bind another procedure, never the file) and `c/` holds the C wrappers dear_bindings made, committed so a build needs no Python (`ui-libs.sh regenerate` rewrites its `reinterpret_cast<T>(NULL)` as `static_cast<T>(nullptr)`, because where `NULL` is `nullptr`, as on OpenBSD, C++ refuses the first); `tools/ui-libs.sh` compiles them with Dear ImGui (fetched at its pinned tag) into `.cache/ui/libroci_ui.a` (on Windows `roci_ui.lib`, with MSVC and `-MT`). Enum values are plain constants (`WindowFlags_NoTitleBar`), flags are `i32`, `ImVec2`/`ImVec4` are `[2]f32`/`[4]f32`, and defaults are written for the plain cases only. Dear ImGui's current-context pointer is `thread_local` (`c/imconfig_roci.h`), so parallel tests may each have a context. **Every program that opens a window links the library**, so `tools/ui-libs.sh` must have run (`ci` runs it when the library is missing; the pipelines run it as a step).
- `vendor/accesskit` and `tools/accesskit`: accesskit-c (Apache-2.0 OR MIT) as Odin. `accesskit.odin` is **generated** from the library's header (`.cache/ui/accesskit/accesskit.h`) by `odin run tools/accesskit` (edit its `WANTED` list, never the file); a C compiler (`cc`) measures every struct so sizes and offsets are `#assert`s (Spike 4's hand-written layouts were wrong), and `ci` writes it again into `output/accesskit` and fails when it differs, except on Windows, which has no `cc`. The library comes from `tools/ui-libs.sh accesskit`: the release archive (macOS, Linux x86_64, and on Windows the static MSVC `accesskit.lib`, opened with `unzip` or PowerShell; sha256 pinned) or a build with git, cmake and cargo (FreeBSD, OpenBSD, arm64 Linux; OpenBSD links no `dl`, and `cargo rustc --release -- --print native-static-libs` in `.cache/ui/accesskit-c-src` lists what Rust needs). Where it cannot be had `ci` skips with a note, and fails when `ROCI_REQUIRE_ACCESSKIT` is set, as both pipelines do. It links on every desktop system (Windows since 2026-11-11). The generated file declares `LIBRARY_PRESENT` (`#exists` of the library) and, when false, a do-nothing stand-in for every procedure, so a build without it still runs (no adapters).
- `tools/ci/`: the install scripts both pipelines use, and `orion.Dockerfile` (a new package for the pipeline goes there and needs a rebuild).

---

## 5. The language

### 5.1 Design principles

- One statement per line. No terminators. (Decided 2026-10-24: inside a `[ ]` block that fits on one line, statements may be separated by two spaces, as in `[ step p  circle p.pos 3 ]`; `roci fmt` keeps a block on one line only when it is short and otherwise puts one statement per line.)
- **Labels for geometry, no commas between arguments** (decided 2026-10-28, after comparing how other languages write calls: Ruby, Nim, Elixir and Perl use commas because their arguments are free expressions and pay for it with spacing and precedence traps; Logo, NetLogo, Rebol and Clojure style use none; no study compares the two for novices, and the nearest evidence, Kotlin and Swift guidance, is to label same-typed numbers). A call never takes commas between its arguments, and `circle (100,100,50)` and `rgb (20,24,70)` are not forms: round brackets stay around a whole call, `(rgb 20 24 70)`, and a stray comma is a teaching error. **Geometry is written with labels**, so a number is never mistaken for another and the line reads as what it draws: `circle at 100 100 radius 50`, `circle at mouse radius 20`, `ellipse at x y size w h`, `rect at x y size w h`, `square at x y size s`, `point at x y`, `line from x y to x y`, `arc at x y size w h from a to b`; `triangle` and `quad` take their corners as vectors, `triangle { 0, 0 } { 50, 0 } { 25, 40 }`. `at`, `from` and `to` take two numbers or one vector, and so does `size` where the shape has a vector form; one call may mix the two (`rect at p size 80 40`, `line from mouse to 10 20`), and the two numbers then make the vector (decided 2026-10-29; `arc` has no vector form, so its words take numbers only). The labelled words may come in any order, and `roci fmt` writes them in the order above, so there is one form on disk; a positional geometry call is a teaching error that shows the labelled line. Pairs are vectors (`{ 320, 220 }`) and `rgb`, `move`, `turn`, `mix`, `noise` and the other non-geometry verbs stay positional. The registry's shapes keep the ordered inputs the C API and the typed IR use; a table of label forms in `roci/registry` says how the language writes them (`radius` and `title` joined the connecting words). A record spread over lines needs no commas: a field that starts a line with `name:` is separated from the one above by the line break (commas stay legal on one line), and `roci fmt` writes one record per line.
- **`polygon [ corners ]`** (decided 2026-11-17): a filled shape through any number of corners, a list of vectors joined in order and closed, in the style words every shape has; it records one `Path` command (the one SVG import uses), and a contour that crosses itself is filled whole (corrected 2026-11-18: it was said to leave a hole, which `geom.triangulate` does not make from one contour: it makes holes from contours nested inside others). A list of lists is the contours of one shape, so `polygon [ wall hole island ]` has a hole and an island in it (`roci.polygons`). Fewer than three corners draw nothing. C has `roci_polygon(corners, count)` and `roci_polygons(corners, counts, contours)`. A city of walls and roofs needs no quads and triangles (`examples/city`).
- **Comments, colours and spacing** (decided 2026-10-28; the grammar is `docs/grammar.ebnf`).
  - **`//` is a comment, `#` is a colour** (decided 2026-10-28; this replaced `#` for both, because a hash that had to be told apart from a colour by what followed it made every tool repeat that rule, and an editor could not colour `x = #a9b8f2` and `x = # a9b8f2` correctly). `//` starts a comment that runs to the end of the line, on its own line or after code; `/` is still division and there is no integer division, so the two never meet. A `#` followed by exactly 3, 4, 6 or 8 hex digits and then a space, a bracket, a comma or the end of the line is a colour (`#ff8800`); any other number of hex digits is a colour with an error that shows the nearest right length. A `#` that is not a colour is a teaching error that shows the line with `//` instead, and a colour at the start of a statement (`#add`) gets the same fix. `roci fmt` always writes comments as `// text`.
  - **Two spaces end a call inside a one-line block.** While the parser collects a call's arguments inside `[ ]`, a token with two or more spaces before it on the same line ends the call, so `[ step p  q = 1 ]` is two statements. Spacing means nothing anywhere else (indentation is free, below).
  - **Numbers** are digits with an optional fraction: `.5`, `5.`, `1e3` and `1_000` are teaching errors. **Text** takes the escapes `\"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\t`, `\{` and `\}`, and **holes** (decided 2026-11-16): a curly bracket no backslash escapes opens an expression up to its matching closing bracket on the same line, and its value is put into the text as `+` would (`"you have {lives} left"`, `"{n + 1}"`). The lexer keeps the whole text one token and skips over the hole (which may hold brackets and text of its own); the parser lexes and parses each hole from where it stands in the file, so its errors point at the right column, into a `Text_Interp` that `roci fmt` copies as written and the checker turns into `+` joins, so the interpreter and the code generator need nothing of their own. A lone closing bracket is plain text; a missing one, an empty hole and a hole with more than one value are the messages `Unclosed_Brace`, `Empty_Hole` and `Hole_Extra`. **Names** are letters of any script, digits and underscores, starting with a letter.
  - **`every`** counts in `seconds` or `frames` (`second` and `frame` for 1): `every 2 seconds [ ... ]`, `every 10 frames [ ... ]`.
- One shape: `verb argument argument ...`. Verbs have a fixed number of required arguments, so the parser always knows where a call ends (as in Logo).
- **One thing per argument** (decided 2026-10-23). Each argument of a verb is a single visible item: a number, text, a name (including built-in values such as `width`, `mouse` and `pi`, and verbs that take no arguments such as `anywhere`), a field (`p.pos`), a `{ }` or `[ ]` literal, or a group in round brackets. A call that takes arguments, or a calculation, used as an argument goes in round brackets: `circle (random width) (random height) 5`, `slider "size" 10 (width / 2)`. The rule turns the readable habit into the grammar, so nested calls never depend on the reader counting arities.
  - **Full expressions are free wherever there is no argument list:** after `=`, after `give`, in `if` and `while` conditions, as the count of `repeat`, and between the commas of vector and record literals (§5.2). So `{ random width, 0 }` needs no brackets, and `x = random width + 10` means `(random width) + 10`: calls bind tighter than arithmetic, as in NetLogo.
  - **Connecting words belong to the call whose brackets they sit in**, so `add (random from 1 to 3) to scores` can't confuse its two `to`s.
  - **`roci fmt` adds missing brackets** when the reading is unique (every verb involved has one shape): `circle at random width random height radius 5` becomes `circle at (random width) (random height) radius 5`. It writes subtraction with spaces (`x - 10`) and negative numbers without (`-10`), so the two never look alike.
  - **Without the formatter, a missing bracket is a load-time error that shows the bracketed line** (§5.7). When brackets nest three deep, the checker adds a hint (not an error) suggesting a name on the line above: `x = random width`.
  - No pipes, no dot calls (`width.random`) and no commas between arguments. `.` stays for fields and library names (§5.10). List chains are named steps, not pipes (§5.2).
  - Rationale and sources: Logo, NetLogo, Rebol, Smalltalk, Odin and the paren-free languages surveyed all show that fixed arity solves the parser's problem but not the reader's. No study measures nested-call readability directly, so the Phase 2 newcomer test checked this rule, and it stands (2026-10-30, §8).
- Optional arguments are introduced by small connecting words, never by position: `window size 800 600 at 100 100 title "garden"`, `text "hi" at 20 20 size 32`, `image photo at 0 0 width 200`. The connecting words form a short, fixed list (start with: `at`, `size`, `width`, `height`, `from`, `to`, `by`, `in`, `start`, `lines`, `angle`, `stops`, `as`, plus the style words in §5.9: `fill`, `stroke`, `thickness`, `alpha`, `font`, `style`). `to` is only ever a connecting word (`for i from 1 to 10`, `add x to particles`); verbs are defined with `learn` (§5.11).
- **A connecting word is one only after the call's required arguments are filled** (decided 2026-10-24). While a verb still has an open required slot, a word that is also a built-in value or a user name (`width`, `height`, `size`) is read as a value: `rect 0 0 width height fill pattern` reads `width` and `height` as the last two required arguments, then `fill` as a connecting word. The same holds for parameters of `learn` (`learn star size [ … ]`). The checker warns when the other reading would also have been valid.
- **Multi-word verbs** (`pen up`, `no fill`, `look at`, `ask file`, `forget ui`, `on mouse down`, `draw into`, `image button`, `export svg`, `hidden learn`, `for each`) are a fixed table of phrases, matched longest-first before a word is read on its own. The table is part of the core and is listed in the docs; user verbs are always one word.
- **`[ ]` is a block or a list by position** (decided 2026-10-24): where the verb's parameter takes a block (`repeat`, `if`, `draw`, `learn`) it is a block; after `=`, as a verb's list parameter, or as a colour list it is a list. Lists have no commas and vectors do (`[ 1 2 ]` against `{ 1, 2 }`); the Phase 2 newcomer test watched for this mix-up. Logo's brackets held code and data alike, and readers had to work out which; so the checker gives a teaching error whenever a list turns up where a block is expected, and the reverse (decided 2026-10-27).
- Blocks use `[ ]`, as in Logo. Newlines inside blocks are free. (Decided 2026-10-22: `[ ]`, not `end`.)
- **Indentation is free and never changes meaning** (decided 2026-10-23). `[ ]` alone defines blocks; tabs, spaces and pasted code can never break a sketch. Consistency comes from tooling instead:
  - **`roci fmt`** rewrites a sketch in the house style: two spaces per block level, one space inside brackets, one statement per line. Editor plugins run it on save, `roci new` starts formatted, and all docs and examples are formatted with it. It has no options, so there is nothing to argue about. A list or record that passes 100 columns is broken one item or field to a line, except a list whose items are all records of the same fields in the same order (a table: six stages, six lines), where each record stays on one line up to 120 columns (`TABLE_WIDTH`, decided 2026-11-17), so the table still reads across. A comment inside a record or a list (on a line of its own, at the end of a line, or before the closing bracket) stays where it was written, which makes the record or list go over lines (decided 2026-11-18).
  - **Indentation as an error hint.** The parser ignores indentation, but when brackets don't balance, the checker compares the brackets with the indentation to find where the mistake is. Example: "Line 7 is indented as if it belongs inside the `repeat` on line 4, but that block closed on line 6. Did you mean to move the `]`?" Never a generic "unexpected end of file".
- **Plain words, not English sentences** (decided 2026-10-27). The words are plain, but the grammar never imitates English. Every construct has one canonical form with a fixed word order: no filler words, no synonyms for the same job, and no wording that changes how a line parses. AppleScript's naturalistic syntax made scripts easy to read and hard to write, and its multi-word terms made the lexer depend on context, which ruined its error messages (Cook, HOPL III, §12). A new construct ships with a rule in the grammar (§5.14), a rule in `roci fmt` and an entry in the error catalogue (§5.7).
- Few keywords. If a feature can be a verb, it is a verb.
- One number type (64-bit float) for users. The compiler may use narrower or integer representations internally when inference proves it safe and results are identical.
- **Case** (decided 2026-10-23). The language is case-sensitive, with two rules:
  - **Every keyword and built-in name is lowercase**: verbs (`circle`, `draw`, `repeat`), connecting words (`at`, `fill`), built-in values (`width`, `mouse`, `pi`, `tau`) and colour names (`red`). `Circle` or `REPEAT` is a load-time error that teaches: "rocinante's words are written in lowercase. Did you mean `circle`?"
  - **A name written in all capitals is a constant.** All capitals means letters, digits and underscores with at least one letter and no lowercase letters: `SPEED`, `MAX_PARTICLES`, `GRID_2`. It is assigned once and can never change. Assigning it again, or changing anything inside it (`add green to COLOURS`, `PALETTE.bg = black`), is a load-time error: "`SPEED` is written in capitals, so it's a constant and can't change. If it needs to change, write it in lowercase: `speed`." Constants are deeply immutable, so a constant list or record can be read but never modified.
  - Other user names are free (`size`, `myBall`, `big_circle`); `size` and `Size` are different names, but the checker warns when two names in a sketch differ only by case, since that is almost always a typo.
  - Constants help the compiler (their values can be folded into native code) and hot reload (§5.5).

### 5.2 Paradigm (decided 2026-10-22)

rocinante is **procedural and data-oriented**, in the spirit of Logo and of Odin. It is not object-oriented and not purely functional.

- **Verbs act on plain data.** Data is numbers, text, vectors, lists and records. Behaviour lives in verbs (built-in, or taught with `learn`), never inside data. Write `bounce ball`, not `ball.bounce()`. Reading and writing fields (`ball.x`) is fine.
- **No classes, methods, inheritance, interfaces or constructors.** Users learn one concept, verbs on data.
- **Many things = a list of records.** Particles, agents, boids: a list of records plus verbs that act on one record, used with `for each`. This is also the fast path internally: with inferred types, a list of same-shaped records compiles to a contiguous array (or Odin `#soa` layout where that helps).

Borrowed from Odin (see the Odin FAQ in References):

- **Vector literals.** A vector is written with braces and commas, like Odin's compound literals: `{ x, y }` or `{ x, y, z }`. Braces with `name:` fields are a record; braces without names are a vector. There is no `vec` verb. Each item between commas may be any expression, so `{ random width, 0 }` needs no brackets (§5.1).
- **Array programming.** Vectors and colours support arithmetic directly: `pos = pos + vel`, `c = c * 0.5`, `v * 2 + { 1, 1 }`. Swizzles: `p.xy`, `p.zyx`. These map one to one onto Odin's own array programming in generated code, and `{ a, b }` maps onto Odin's context-typed `{a, b}`.
- **Explicit verb groups.** One verb name may accept several argument shapes (`circle at 100 100 radius 50` and `circle at p radius 50`). The shapes are declared together in one place, in the core and in user code. Because every argument is one item (§5.1), the parser finds where a call ends without knowing types; the checker then picks the shape by argument count first, then by argument kinds. Two shapes in one group may not share both count and kinds: declaring them is a load-time error, in the core or in `learn` (decided 2026-10-23).
- **Useful zero values.** A record field that was never set reads as 0 (or empty text, empty list). Reading it is never an error.
- **No exceptions.** A failing operation (missing file, bad URL) shows the error overlay, yields an empty value and lets the sketch continue. Users can test for emptiness (`if photo is empty [ … ]`); there is no try/catch.
- **Implicit context.** Style and transform state (fill, stroke, thickness, transform, font, blend) is an implicit context, reset at the start of every `draw` frame and scoped by `with [ ]` (style, §5.9) and `push [ ]` (style and transforms).
- **Handles are changed with `set`, and read as fields** (decided 2026-10-29). A window (and later a sound, a movie, a canvas or a camera) is something the sketch holds a handle to, not data it made, so its settings are changed by a verb with the words the handle was made with (`set w size 400 300 resizable false`) and read as fields (`w.width`). Assignment with a dot stays for a sketch's own records and the parts of its vectors (`ball.pos = ...`, `p.x = 5`). Sketch-wide settings such as `fps`, `vsync`, `seed` and `background` stay verbs of their own, because there is no handle.

Deliberate differences from Odin:

- **Blocks capture variables.** `every 2 seconds [ size = size + 10 ]` sees `size`. Odin has only non-capturing procedures because capturing closures need automatic memory management; our runtime manages memory, so users never think about it. Generated Odin passes captured variables explicitly.
- **A light functional layer for lists** (decided 2026-10-24), as verbs that take a block. Inside the block, `it` is the current item. Each gives a new list or value and leaves the original alone:

```
alive  = keep particles [ it.life > 0 ]       // the items that pass
calm   = skip particles [ it.speed > 5 ]      // the items that don't
sizes  = collect particles [ it.size * 2 ]    // one new value per item
total  = combine scores from 0 [ result + it ]   // fold: `result` is the running value
big    = find particles [ it.size > 20 ]      // the first match, or empty
order  = sort particles by [ it.size ]        // sorted by what the block gives
mixed  = shuffle particles                    // the same items in a random order (the run's seed repeats it)
```

  The block of these verbs holds **one expression** (decided 2026-10-28), not statements: `[ it.life > 0 ]`. It sits where the verb takes it, or after a connecting word (`combine scores from 0 [ result + it ]`, `sort particles by [ it.size ]`). `keep`, `skip` and `find` need a true-or-false expression, `collect` gives a list of what it gives, `sort` needs a number or text, and `combine` needs it to match the starting value.

  There is no pipe: chains are written one named step per line, which reads more clearly and fits one statement per line. `map` stays Processing's number-remapping verb, and `filter` stays an image effect. `collect` and the idea of plain-English list verbs come from Smalltalk. `it` and `result` are keywords, lowercase like all others (§5.1).

### 5.3 Typing: inferred and static (decided 2026-10-22)

- **Users never write a type.** There is no type syntax in sketches.
- **The checker infers every type it can** from literals, verb signatures, field use and assignments: `x = 10` is a number, `p.pos` is a vector, `particles` is a list of records with fields `pos` and `vel`, both vectors.
- **Conflicts are load-time errors in plain words**, never type jargon. For example: "`size` holds a number on line 3 but text on line 12. A name keeps one kind of value; try a new name for the text."
- **Records are shaped by use** (decided 2026-10-24). Three rules, with the error messages written first in Phase 2:
  - **One shape per place a record is made.** A record literal fixes its fields; assigning a field anywhere else in the sketch (`ball.colour = red`) extends the shape for every record made at that place, and the field starts at its zero value.
  - **Verbs take any record that has what they use.** `learn step p [ p.pos = p.pos + p.vel ]` works for every record with `pos` and `vel`, whatever else it holds (structural records, as in Elm). Compiled code gets one plain Odin struct per shape and one version of the verb per shape it is used with.
  - **Data from files gets its shape from the file.** When `load "birds.json"` names a file in `assets/`, the checker reads that file while checking and takes the shape from it, as F#'s JSON type providers do, so `birds.items` and its fields are known before the sketch runs. When the file name is computed, or the data comes from `fetch` or `ask file`, values use the boxed fallback below.
  - **Messages between windows** take their shape from the `send` lines that can reach each `on … receive`; the checker sees the whole program.
- **Fallback, not failure.** Where inference cannot decide (rare in sketches), the value becomes a general boxed value (an Odin tagged union in generated code): slower, still correct, identical behaviour in both back ends. `roci check --why-slow` (name provisional) lists those places so power users can see them; ordinary users never need to.
- Inference is what makes compiled sketches fast: known types become plain Odin structs, floats and arrays instead of runtime kind checks.

### 5.4 Core syntax (draft)

```
// comments start with two slashes

x = 10                      // variables: just assign
name = "Ada"
SPEED = 4                   // all capitals: a constant, can never change
COLOURS = [ red orange teal ]   // constant list: readable, never modified
points = [ { 10, 20 } { 30, 40 } ]   // lists
p = { x: 10, y: 20 }        // records; access with p.x

learn star size [           // teach a new verb, Logo style (§5.11)
  repeat 5 [ forward size  right 144 ]
]

learn double n [ give n * 2 ]   // `give` hands back a value

star 100                    // use it like any built-in
y = double 21

if mouse.x > width / 2 [ fill red ] else [ fill blue ]

repeat 10 [ circle at (random width) (random height) radius 5 ]
for i from 1 to 10 [ line from 0 (i * 10) to width (i * 10) ]
for each p in points [ circle at p radius 10 ]

draw [ ... ]                // runs every frame (default window)
draw w [ ... ]              // runs every frame for window w
on key "space" [ ... ]
on w key "space" [ ... ]     // only in window w, as with receive
on mouse down [ ... ]
on mouse down right [ ... ]  // only that button: left, middle, right, back or forward
on touch down [ ... ]        // finger is the finger that went down: finger.pos, finger.id
on touch up [ ... ]
on w receive msg [ ... ]
every 2 seconds [ ... ]

v = { 3, 4 }                // vectors: braces and commas, no names
u = v * 2 + { 1, 1 }        // arithmetic and swizzles
circle at v.xy radius 10
circle at anywhere radius 5 // a random point in the window
r = random from 1 to 3      // a random number in a range
c = pick COLOURS            // a random item from a list
```

A complete particle sketch in this style (lists of records, verbs, no objects):

```
particles = []
repeat 200 [ add { pos: { random width, 0 }, vel: { 0, random 3 } } to particles ]

learn step p [ p.pos = p.pos + p.vel ]

draw [
  background 0
  for each p in particles [ step p  circle at p.pos radius 3 ]
]
```

### 5.5 Program model

- Top-level statements run once, in order. If the program has no `draw` block, the window shows the result and stays open. In a multi-file sketch (§5.10), only `sketch.roci` has top-level statements that run; the other files hold definitions.
- If a `draw` block exists, top-level statements act as setup and the block runs every frame.
- **Hot reload** (interpreter only, decided 2026-10-24). On every save:
  - The new code is checked first. If it has an error, the old code keeps running under the error overlay (non-negotiable 3) until the next save fixes it.
  - Every reload says what it did: the console and overlay report "reloaded" (state kept) or "restarted", and name any value that was lost (decided 2026-10-27). Each `learn` body is hashed, so reload knows exactly which verbs changed. A renamed field counts as one removed and one added, so its value is lost and the message says so.
  - If it checks, verbs, `draw` blocks, event blocks and `ui` blocks switch to the new code on the next frame.
  - A top-level variable keeps its running value unless the line that defines it changed, or the kind of value it holds changed; then that line runs again.
  - Records keep their values when their shape gains a field; the new field starts at its zero value (§5.2). When a shape loses a field or a field changes kind, the variables holding those records start again from their defining lines.
  - A line that opens a window or draws while the sketch sets itself up cannot run a second time on top of the first, so when one of those lines is added, changed or removed the sketch restarts from its first line instead (the windows stay open), and the message says so (decided 2026-10-28).
  - Constants (all-capital names, §5.1) always take the value written in the source, so editing `SPEED = 4` to `SPEED = 8` takes effect at once while variables keep their running values.
  - UI values are remembered as before (§4.8).
  - `Ctrl+R` in a sketch window (`Cmd+R` on macOS) restarts the sketch from scratch: every variable from its defining line, every top-level statement again. UI values are kept; `forget ui` clears them.

### 5.6 Turtle

Logo's turtle is built in, in 2D and 3D: `forward`, `back`, `left`, `right`, `pen up`, `pen down`, `home`, plus `up`/`down`/`roll` in 3D. Each window has its own turtle.

- **Where it starts** (decided 2026-10-29): in the middle of its window, facing up, as in Logo; `right` turns it clockwise, as `turn` turns the canvas, and `left` anticlockwise. `home` sends it back there facing up, **without drawing** (Logo and Python draw a line home when the pen is down; a sketch more often wants to start a new figure), and leaves the pen up or down. The middle is worked out when the turtle first moves, so a window resized before then still starts it in its middle.
- **What it draws with** (decided 2026-10-29): its lines are ordinary lines, in the stroke colour, thickness, caps and alpha, under the current transform. While the stroke is off, which is the default (§5.15), it draws in black, so `repeat 4 [ forward 100  right 90 ]` in a fresh sketch shows a square (non-negotiable 1). Setting a stroke colours it; `no stroke` does not hide it (lift the pen with `pen up`).
- **How long it lasts** (decided 2026-10-29): like style and transforms, every frame starts the turtle at home, and `push [ ]` puts its place, heading and pen back afterwards, so a branching tree is a recursive verb with a `push` for each branch (`examples/tree`); `with [ ]`, which keeps the style only, leaves it walking. Set-up drawing in a window with no `draw` block keeps the turtle where the set-up left it.
- **Teaching**: Logo's short words (`fd`, `bk`, `lt`, `rt`, `pu`, `pd`, `penup`, `pendown`) are "did you mean" suggestions for the whole ones (`registry.VERB_SYNONYMS`), never verbs of their own (one word for one job, §5.1).
- **Reading it and showing it** (decided 2026-11-02): `turtle` is a built-in value, a record of `pos` (a vector, in the coordinates shapes are drawn in), `angle` (degrees clockwise from up, from 0 up to but not including 360, so four right angles read 0) and `down` (true while the pen is down); it is the turtle of the window the code runs in. `show turtle` draws it where it is, a small triangle (20 pixels long) pointing the way it faces, solid while the pen is down and an outline while it is up, in the stroke or in black while the stroke is off, and leaves the style and the turtle as they were. A field named `heading` was the other choice for the angle, but `heading` is planned as the vector verb (§6); the drawing verb is two words like `pen up` so `show` stays free as a name. `x = turtle` is a teaching error like any built-in (`turtle` cannot be a name). The turtle in space, with `up`, `down` and `roll`, is `turtle space` (§4.3).

### 5.7 Errors

Format, modelled on Elm:

```
── I don't know the word "circel" ──────────────── sketch.roci:3:1

3 │ circel 100 100 50
  │ ^^^^^^

Did you mean:  circle at 100 100 radius 50
```

```
── This value needs brackets ─────────────────────── sketch.roci:4:8

4 │ circle at random width random height radius 5
  │        ^^^^^^^^^^^^

`random width` works out a number, so when it sits inside `circle`,
wrap it in round brackets so it reads as one thing:

    circle at (random width) (random height) radius 5

Or let `roci fmt` add them for you.
```

The catalogue is data (decided 2026-10-27): each entry has a message ID, the plain explanation and an example fix, so messages can be translated later (§5.15 spelling, Swidan and Hermans in §12). A message must make sense read aloud by a screen reader, so it says "the closing square bracket", never a bare `]` (Hermans, §12). The parser resynchronises at `[ ]` boundaries and reports every error in one pass, not only the first; `roci run` keeps the last good code (§5.5) and `roci package` refuses a sketch that has errors. `roci run --errors json` prints the same entries as data (line, column, message ID, nearest known verb) for tools. Every error class needs an entry in `tests/errors/` with the source that triggers it and the expected message. Error messages are reviewed like features. Because all errors come from the shared front end (§4.7), `roci run` and `roci package` report exactly the same errors.

### 5.8 User interface verbs (draft)

Widgets live in `ui [ ]` blocks (default window) or `ui w [ ]` (window `w`). Each returns a value usable anywhere in the sketch.

```
ui [
  size   = slider "size" 10 200          // number in a range
  speed  = knob "speed" 0 1              // rotary, for audio-visual work
  trails = toggle "trails"               // true / false
  name   = textbox "title"               // one line of text
  notes  = textbox "notes" lines 4       // multi-line
  shape  = dropdown "shape" [ "circle" "square" "star" ]
  mode   = choice "mode" [ "draw" "erase" ]   // radio buttons
  tint   = colorpicker "tint"
  pos    = xypad "position"              // 2D pad, returns a vector
  count  = number "count" 1 1000         // numeric field with steppers
  if button "clear" [ particles = [] ]
  label ("fps: " + fps)
  progress loaded
]

draw [
  background 0
  fill tint
  circle at pos.x pos.y radius size
]
```

Layout and grouping:

```
ui [
  panel "controls" [                     // titled, collapsible group
    row [ button "play"  button "stop" ]  // side by side
    size = slider "size" 10 200
  ]
  tabs [
    tab "shape" [ ... ]
    tab "colour" [ ... ]
  ]
  menu "file" [ if item "save" [ save "frame.png" ] ]
  tip "drag to move"                     // tooltip for the previous widget
]
```

- **Default layout:** widgets stack vertically in a floating panel at the top left of the window, sized to fit. `at x y` places a widget or panel explicitly; `width` sets its width.
- **Starting values:** `slider "size" 10 200 start 50`. Without `start`, numbers start at the minimum, toggles off, text empty, dropdowns on the first item.
- **Control windows:** a second window holding only a `ui` block is the standard way to build a control surface for a performance or installation; its values are readable from every window directly, no messaging needed.
- **Hiding** (decided 2026-10-24, revised): the backtick key (`` ` ``, read by physical key position like `keys w a s d`, §5.12) hides and shows the UI; on phones and tablets a three-finger tap does. `Tab` only moves keyboard focus (§4.8), so no key has two jobs. The key is ignored while a text box has focus, so it never eats typing. `ui key "f1"` (any key name) changes it, and `ui hide` and `ui show` do it from code, so performances can run clean. While the UI is hidden, the screen-reader tree stays and reports "controls hidden, press backtick to show" (§4.8; the key is named, because a screen reader does not pronounce the mark), and showing the UI again moves focus to the first widget, so keyboard and screen-reader users reach the widgets in one press.
- **`ui` inside `draw`** (decided 2026-10-24, built 2026-11-06): `ui [ ]` blocks may also sit inside `draw` blocks, for widgets tied to things on the canvas. `at` there takes canvas positions, so `slider "speed" 0 5 at p.pos` follows `p` as it moves. Widgets still draw on top and keep their size and theme; only their position follows the canvas (`examples/follow`).

**Built so far** (2026-11-06): `ui [ ]` and `ui w [ ]` (a block run every frame after the window's `draw`, as a `Draw` statement with a flag) and the verbs `label`, `button` (true on the frame it is clicked), `toggle` (`start true` or false) and `slider` (`start`, the low end when it is not written, kept inside the range). A widget that keeps a value (`toggle`, `slider`) keeps it in the window's interface under its text, so the value survives a reload and a restart and is the widget's own after the first frame (`start` counts once); a sketch name set from it is one frame behind the person's hand at most. Two widgets with one text are the runtime message `Widget_Twice`, said once, and both still work. Then `number` (steppers, kept inside its range when the box lets go, so "50" can be typed where "5" is below it), `textbox` (`start`, `lines`; up to 4,095 bytes), `dropdown` and `choice` (from a list of text, giving the item; `start` names one), `progress`, `separator` and `tip` (words for the widget before it, kept in its record as a description for screen readers). **Text input** (2026-11-06): the window asks SDL for typed text with `SDL_StartTextInput` while a text box is in use and puts the input method's window beside the box (`SDL_SetTextInputArea`), `Text_Input` events go to Dear ImGui as characters, the system clipboard is Dear ImGui's, and the fonts the system has for other scripts (`gfx.FALLBACK_FONT_FILES`, without the colour emoji ones) are merged into the interface's font the first time a text beyond ASCII is typed or shown. An input method (IME) has to be tried by hand on each system: nothing in `ci` can compose text. **Layout and identity** (2026-11-06): `panel` (a header that folds up, open to start with), `row`, `column`, `tabs` with `tab`, `menu` with `item` (`item` has a second shape beside `item 1 of scores`, told apart by its kind) and `list`. A group runs its block only while it is open (a folded panel, a tab that is not showing, a menu not open) and always closes again. A widget is known by its panel, tab and menu path and its text, so the same text in two of them is two widgets with their own values; the second and later time one call of the sketch makes a widget in a frame (a loop) it gets a number too, kept as a hidden suffix on Dear ImGui's ID (`site_count` in the interpreter). A `tab` outside `tabs` is the runtime message `Tab_Outside_Tabs`. `forget ui` puts every widget of the window back to its start (a widget keeps its value while the sketch runs and reloads, and the sketch cannot set it, so this is how a reset button works). `row` was a variable name in four sketches and was renamed. **Dials** (2026-11-06): `colorpicker` (a swatch with a picker and an alpha bar; `start` is a color, white when not written), `knob` (dragged up or right, or the arrow keys: 200 pixels of mouse is the whole range), `range` (two handles that never cross, each its own focus stop; gives a vector `r.x`, `r.y`) and `xypad` (the place pressed, 0 to 1 across and down, starting in the middle; arrow keys move it a hundredth). They are drawn with Dear ImGui's draw list over invisible buttons, so focus, keyboard and touch work like the built-in ones, and each leaves a record with its text, value and rectangle. `image button` (a picture's own size down to 64 pixels, or `size`; the text after the picture is its name) puts a place for the picture in the draw commands (a bit and the picture's number as the texture) and the window's draw pass swaps it for the renderer's own texture of that picture (`gfx.image_texture`, uploaded on the pass's command buffer); Dear ImGui's SDL_GPU backend takes an `SDL_GPUTexture*` as a texture id since August 2025, so no sampler or binding is made. A picture with no texture (a canvas not drawn yet, a missing file) leaves its button empty. **Remembered values** (2026-11-06): `roci run` of a `sketch.roci` keeps the values the widgets hold in `ui.toml` beside it, which is committed (§4.9): `roci.ui_persist` reads it when the sketch starts, each widget takes its line when it is first made (so `start` is only for a widget the file does not know), and it is written when the sketch ends and after a second in which nothing moved, never on every move of a slider. It has a table for each window, `[ui]` for the first and `[ui2]`, `[ui3]` for the next, and a line for each widget under its readable name, the panel and tab path and the text (`"dot/size" = 60.0`, `shape = "star"`, `mood = "#ffa500ff"`, `span = [20.0, 80.0]`, `again#1` for the second time a loop made one); lists are remembered by the item's text so a reordered list keeps the choice. The tables of windows that are not open and the lines of widgets not made this run stay as they were (a tab nobody opened loses nothing), and a file that cannot be read right is said line by line and what can be read is used. A lone `.roci` file, a headless run and the tests remember nothing, so running an example never leaves a file behind. Our own `roci/toml` reads and writes it. **Hiding** (2026-11-06): the backtick key (by position, name `backtick`, `ui key "f1"` changes it) or three fingers tapping together (down together, up within half a second, none moving more than 40 pixels) hide and show a window's interface, and so do `ui hide` and `ui show`. A hidden interface is still made every frame, so the sketch keeps reading its values, but nothing is drawn and no click, key or typed text reaches it; its records stay for the screen-reader tree. The key is left alone while a text box is waiting for typing. Showing puts keyboard focus on the first widget. An unknown name in `ui key` is the runtime message `Key_Unknown`. **Placing and sizing** (2026-11-06): every widget takes `at x y` (or a vector) and `width w`, and `panel`, `row`, `column` and `tabs` take them after their block (`panel "world" [ ... ] at 330 12 width 140`). A widget with `at` is made in a window of its own, named by its path, text and the place of its call (so it keeps its place and size from frame to frame), with a thin padding that is moved back so the widget itself sits exactly at the point; a group with `at` is a floating window with its widgets inside, and a `width` on a group is that window's width. Anything else stays in the one panel at the top left, which is not made at all in a frame where every widget is placed. `width` is how wide the widget itself is (a slider's bar, a button, a text box, the side of a pad, a knob's diameter, a range's bar, a list or a progress bar); a check box, a label, radio buttons and an image button ignore it. A `ui` block inside `draw` (or an event block) makes its widgets where it sits instead of being handed to the app loop, so a loop can make a widget for every item, and `at` there is in the canvas's coordinates: the transform of the moment (`move`, `turn`, `grow`) is applied to the point, which is why a widget follows what is drawn beside it; in a `ui` block of its own `at` is a place of the window. Known limit: Dear ImGui moves keyboard focus with Tab only inside one window, so a keyboard-only person reaches a placed widget with Ctrl+Tab, which switches window; the screen-reader bridge will list every widget in one tree whatever window it is in. **Colors** (2026-11-11): `ui theme dark`, `ui theme light` and `ui theme system` choose a window's widget colors, the system's light or dark setting to start with (§4.8, Theme). None of the widget verbs is in the C API yet (the Odin API has them as `ui_label`, `ui_button`, `ui_toggle`, `ui_slider` and the `ui_*_value` procedures).

Full widget set for the first stable release: `label`, `button`, `toggle`, `checkbox` (alias of toggle), `slider`, `range` (two-handle slider), `knob`, `number`, `textbox`, `dropdown`, `choice`, `colorpicker`, `xypad`, `progress`, `list` (selectable list), `panel`, `row`, `column`, `tabs`/`tab`, `menu`/`item`, `tip`, `separator`, `image button`, `controls` (remapping actions, §5.12). Additions go through this file first.

### 5.9 Style and colour (decided 2026-10-23)

The same style words work at three levels, so users learn one vocabulary.

**1. Set it and it sticks.** Style verbs change the current style until changed again. The style resets at the start of every `draw` frame, so one frame never leaks into the next.

```
fill orange
stroke white
thickness 3
font "Inter" size 24
circle at 100 100 radius 50
text "hello" at 20 20
```

**2. Style one element.** The same words as connecting words apply to that element only and leave the current style untouched:

```
circle at 100 100 radius 50 fill red stroke white thickness 3
text "title" at 20 20 font "Mono" size 32 fill gray
```

**3. Style a group.** `with` applies a style to everything in its block and restores the previous style afterwards:

```
with fill cyan thickness 2 [
  circle at 100 100 radius 50
  rect at 200 80 size 40 40
]
```

**Named styles are records.** A style is plain data (§5.2), usable with `style` on one element or with `with` on a group. No style classes, no style sheets.

```
glow = { fill: cyan, stroke: white, thickness: 2 }
circle at 100 100 radius 50 style glow
with glow [ ... ]
```

Words:

- `background`: what is behind everything in the window (decided 2026-10-30): wherever it is written, the shapes before it and after it are drawn on it, and the last background of a frame is the one that shows. It never wipes what came before (Processing's `background` does, and so did rocinante's until then); `clear` is the verb that empties the window. Every frame starts empty anyway, so no sketch needs a background to rub out the last one.
- `fill`: colour of areas and text. `no fill` turns it off.
- `stroke`: colour of outlines and lines. `no stroke` turns it off.
- `thickness`: stroke width in pixels (not `strokeWeight`, and not `width`, which already means size).
- `alpha`: how solid fill and stroke are, 0 (gone) to 100 (solid), applied to fill and stroke.
- `font`: a font file in the sketch's `assets/` (§4.9) or a system font name; `size` sets text size in pixels; `weight` (`light`, `regular`, `bold`, or a number) is optional.
- `caps`, `joins`: as in the parity map (§6).
- `blend` (decided 2026-11-05): how what is drawn mixes with what is behind it, a style word like `fill`: a verb that lasts (`blend add`), a word of one shape, text or picture (`circle ... blend add`) and of `with` (`with blend multiply [ ... ]`). The words are `normal` (the default), `add`, `multiply`, `screen`, `subtract` and `lighten`, each a fixed GPU blend of the shape's premultiplied colour with the picture behind it, so a frame costs one pipeline for each blend in use and a batch ends where the blend changes. `darken` is not offered, nor are `difference` and `exclusion`: they need the background's colour inside the shader or a minimum that ignores a shape's transparent corners, which a fixed blend cannot do. The word `add` is also a verb, so the parser takes the word as written after `blend`, as it does after `caps`.
- `clip` (decided 2026-11-05): `clip at x y size w h` (or vectors) keeps later drawing inside a box until `no clip`, a style that `push` and `with` put back and every frame starts without. The box goes through the transform of the moment and is kept as the upright box round its corners, so a turned box clips to its bounding box; the renderer sets a scissor rectangle for each batch (a change of clip ends a batch). Shaped clips are built (2026-11-21, #58): see below; a text as a clip and a picture as a mask are not.
- **The same warnings in compiled sketches** (decided 2026-11-21, #64): `Field_Missing` for a field a taught verb's `{ }` left out and `Constant_Not_Made` are said by a compiled program too, once for each place, on the error output, with the same words and the line of the sketch under them: both back ends make the arguments with `lang.field_missing_args` and `lang.constant_not_made_args`, and `rt` renders them with `lang.render` (the program already links `lang` through `folder`). The generator makes two passes: the first finds the (record shape, field) pairs that a `{ }` inside a verb leaves out (`Gen.unset_found`), the second writes the reads of those fields as `rt.checked(rt.live(r), bit, &site_N).field`, which looks at the hidden `unset__` mask every record struct carries (set by the `{ }` as `unset__ = mask`, cleared by `r.f = v` as `r.unset__ &= ~u64(bit)`, kept by a copy) and warns when the field's bit is set; reads of other fields cost nothing more. A constant whose value is not only written down (it reads a name, a verb or the clock, so it is made where it is written) carries `NAME_made__`, set by its line and looked at by every read of it inside a verb as `rt.constant_read(NAME, NAME_made__, &site_N)` (a procedure and not an `if` expression, whose type became an optional number's where the read went to `image`'s width and the build failed, found 2026-11-26). Each place is a package-level `rt.Site` (offsets into the joined sketch and the names), and a program with at least one has `rt.use_source` as the first line of `setup`, carrying the sketch's text (a program with none carries none); the text is the package constant `source_text__` on a line of its own and the parts the package variable `source_parts__`, because `use_source` keeps the parts and a slice literal in `setup` is gone once it returns (found 2026-11-21, when Odin's escape analysis warned about it under the one line that held the whole of WILDVEX, and printing that warning took 31 GB and the out-of-memory killer ended the compiler). `roci/builds` compares the number of warnings of the interpreter and the compiled program for every sketch of `tests/compile` (`quiet_values.roci` has both kinds). The compiled program does not colour them, and says them on the error output only. A division or remainder whose divisor is not a number written in the sketch is `rt.divide_at`, `rt.modulo_at`, `rt.divide_vector_at`, `rt.divide_vector_by_at` or `rt.box_arith(.Divide, a, b, &site_N)`, which say `Division_By_Zero` as the interpreter does (`lang.division_by_zero_args` makes the words for both) and still give 0 (decided 2026-11-26, #92: a compiled sketch used to give 0 and say nothing, where the interpreter said it).
- **A field a record was made without** (decided 2026-11-21, #66): the run-time `Field_Missing` also fires for a field that is in a record's shape but that no line gave this record, when the record was made by a `{ }` inside a taught verb (`Interp.depth` above 0), which is where a verb gives records of different fields that the checker cannot compare; `Record.unset` holds a bit for each of the first 64 fields the `{ }` left out, the first assignment to the field clears it, a copy keeps it, and the first read at each place says it once. Records made in plain sight, in a list literal or a message, do not warn: a list of circles, triangles and rectangles read through `s.radius`, and the two kinds of message of `examples/two_windows`, use the zero of a missing field on purpose (Fabrizio chose this over warning everywhere, 2026-11-21).
- **`save ... in sketch`** (decided 2026-11-21, #68): `save` takes `in sketch` or `in output` (the default) after its name, for the window's picture, a picture or canvas and data alike: `in sketch` puts a plain name under the sketch's own folder (`save pic "icons/ios/app.png" in sketch`, the folders made) instead of `output/`, and an absolute path is used as it is whichever is said. In the interpreter that folder is the sketch's (`assets_root`, the working folder for a lone file); in compiled and packaged programs it is where the program works from, which is the sketch's folder under `roci run --compile` and the app's own writable folder in a packaged app, so nothing is ever written into a bundle. Writing outside the sketch's folder with `..` is not stopped, as an absolute path is not. C writes where it is told: `roci_save` takes an absolute path, or a name that goes in `output/` of the working folder.
- **`reverse`, `join` and `items`** (decided 2026-11-21, #62): `reverse list`, `join first second` (also `first + second` when both are lists) and `items 2 to 4 of list` make new lists and leave the old ones alone; places count from 1 as `item` does, both ends are in, a place outside the list is the nearest end and nothing between gives an empty list. The two lists of a join have to hold the same kind of thing (`Wrong_Argument_Kind`). In compiled sketches they are `rt.reverse`, `rt.join_lists` and `rt.items_between`; C has `roci_reverse`, `roci_join` and `roci_items` for arrays, as `roci_shuffle` does. An empty list written as `[]` has no kind yet, so a compiled sketch cannot reverse or join one (the same limit as holding one). Not done: slicing by `list[2:4]`, since the language has no brackets for items.
- **Clip to a shape** (decided 2026-11-21, #58): `clip circle`, `clip ellipse`, `clip rect` and `clip square` (the shape's own words, and `round` for the last two) and `clip polygon [ ... ]` (a list of corners, or of lists for holes) keep later drawing inside the shape, until `no clip`, a style that `push` and `with` put back. They are two-word verbs of the registry with C functions of their own (`roci_clip_circle` and the rest, with `roci_origin` and `roci_round_corners` for the next one). The shape is made of corners in steps about two pixels long (24 to 400 for a circle), carried through the transform of the moment, and kept as a `Clip_Shape` command that is never drawn, with the box round it still the scissor; a clip points at its outline by `Clip.shape`. In the renderer the pass of a frame that clips to a shape gets a stencil buffer (`D32_FLOAT_S8_UINT`, cleared to 0, made once per size and kept), every pipeline of that pass is made for it (`Stencil_Mode`: attached and leaving it alone, adding one, taking one away, or testing for a value), and the batcher puts the outlines in before the first batch that is inside a shape the stencil does not hold, as the entry below says. The edge is one sample deep, as soft as the pass's multisampling makes it. SVG writes a `clipPath` with the outline, PDF a path with `W* n`. A frame with no clip to a shape has no stencil and is drawn as it was
- **Clips nest** (decided 2026-11-21, #71): a `clip` written while another is in force keeps only what is inside both, and `no clip` goes back to the clip it was made inside, one at a time (it takes the last one away; `gfx.clear_clip` takes every one away and is what the overlay and the stats box use), as `push` and `pop` do for a transform, as canvas `save` and `restore` do, and as SVG's nested `clipPath` do. This changes what two clips in a row mean: before, the second replaced the first, and a sketch that wants that writes `no clip` between them (`examples/blend` did). A `Clip` holds `outer`, one more than the place of the clip it was made inside in `Command_List.clip_outers` (0 for none), so `no clip` and a `pop` of the style both put back a whole clip with the chain behind it; its box is the box where the two meet, empty when they do not, and a box clip inside a shape clip keeps the shape (`clip_is_shape` tells a shape that is its own from one that is its outer's). Each clip shape knows its parent shape (`clip_parents`), and the stencil counts: a pixel holds how many of the shapes of the chain in force cover it. The batcher moves from the chain it holds to the chain a command needs by taking away the shapes they do not share, innermost first (draw the outline where the stencil equals its depth, decrement), and adding the new ones, outermost first (where it equals the depth below, increment); a batch inside a chain of depth d draws only where the stencil equals d (the reference is set for each batch, `Batch.reference`). A shape inside a shape stops at 200 levels (`MAX_CLIP_DEPTH`), past which a clip cuts everything away. SVG writes one group for each clip, inside one another, PDF one cut after another (`clip_chain`). `begin_frame_from` forgets the clip shapes and the outer clips of what a held frame drops, which it did not do before for shapes.
- **Rounded corners** (decided 2026-11-21, #58): `rect` and `square` take `round r`, the corner radius in pixels, never more than half the shorter side and nothing for 0 or less. It is a word of the shape, not a style, so it never lasts: a rounded one is drawn as a polygon (a path, four quarter turns of 4 to 40 steps by the radius) and so takes a fill, a gradient, a picture and a stroke like any shape, and a plain rectangle stays the cheap instanced shape. C has `roci_round_corners(radius)` for the next rectangle or square only, as `roci_origin` is for the next origin.
- Precedence: per-element words override `with` blocks, which override the current style.
- **Origin** (decided 2026-10-24). `rect`, `circle` (and the other 2D shapes), `image`, `square` and `ellipse` take `as center` or `as corner` to say what their position means: `rect at 200 200 size 80 40 as center` puts the middle of the rectangle at 200, 200; `as corner` is the default for `rect`, `square` and `image`, the top left, as in Processing; `circle`, `ellipse` and `arc` default to `as center`, also as in Processing (decided 2026-10-27). One shared pair of words replaces Processing's `rectMode`, `ellipseMode`, `imageMode` and `shapeMode`, and it is per element, so nothing sticks from one line to the next. `as` already carries fill modes (`fill photo as center`, `fill [ red teal ] as radial`), so the rule is positional (§5.1): an `as` directly after a shape's required arguments is its origin, and an `as` after a `fill` or `stroke` value belongs to that value. `circle at 100 100 radius 50 as center fill photo as cover` reads one way: the first `as` is the origin and the second is the fill mode. A circle, an ellipse and an arc are already centred, so `as center` only matters for the shapes that default to a corner, and `as corner` is how to place a circle or an ellipse by its top left.

**Colours** are accepted anywhere a colour goes:

- Names: the CSS named colours (`red`, `orange`, `teal`, …).
- Hex: `#ff8800`, or `#ff880080` with alpha.
- One number for grey: `fill 0`, `background 30`.
- `rgb r g b`, `hsb h s b`, `oklch l c h`; any of them may be followed by `alpha a`, a percentage.
- **Range: every colour channel is 0–255** (decided 2026-10-23), including grey, `rgb` and `hsb`, **except alpha, which is a percentage from 0 (gone) to 100 (solid)** (decided 2026-10-29): `fill red alpha 50` is half transparent, and `c.a` reads back 0 to 100. Alpha is how much of the picture shows, which people think of as a percentage (gradient `stops` are percentages too); the Odin API and the C ABI keep 0–255 and only the language converts. A hex colour keeps its alpha byte (`#ff880080`). The only exception is `oklch`, which follows its standard CSS notation (lightness 0–1, chroma, hue in degrees) because its whole point is matching that standard.
- Values outside 0–255 are clamped, with a gentle warning from the checker when it can see the value at load time.
- Colours are values: `c = rgb 255 136 0`, `mix c blue 0.5`, `c * 0.5` (§5.2 array programming).

**Gradients** (decided 2026-10-24). Wherever a colour goes in `fill`, `stroke` or `background`, a list of colours makes a gradient. The colours are spaced evenly unless `stops` says otherwise.

```
fill [ red orange teal ]                      // linear, left to right
fill [ red orange teal ] angle 90             // linear, top to bottom
fill [ red orange teal ] stops [ 0 20 100 ]   // uneven steps, in percent along the way
fill [ white black ] as radial                // from the centre outwards
fill [ white black ] as radial at 30 30       // centre at 30% across, 30% down the shape
fill [ red yellow red ] as conic angle 45     // sweeping round the centre
fill [ red blue ] in rgb                      // mixed in RGB, like Processing
background [ navy black ] angle 90
stroke [ white cyan ]

sunset = gradient [ orange purple ] angle 90  // a gradient is a value
circle at 100 100 radius 50 fill sunset
glow = { fill: sunset, stroke: white }        // and works in named styles
```

- **Kinds:** `as linear` (the default), `as radial`, `as conic`.
- **Orientation:** `angle` in degrees. `0` runs left to right and angles turn clockwise, like the turtle's `right`; for `conic`, `angle` sets where the sweep starts.
- **Steps:** any number of colours, each a full colour value (`#ff8800`, `rgb …`, a name, with alpha). `stops` gives each colour's position as a percentage, one per colour.
- **Geometry follows the shape.** Positions are percentages of the element's bounding box, so a gradient fits a circle, a rectangle or a line of text without arithmetic.
- **Mixing:** colours are mixed in Oklab by default, which keeps gradients even and avoids muddy middles; `in rgb` gives Processing's look and `in oklch` mixes along the hue.
- With per-element style words, a bare list works (`circle at 100 100 radius 50 fill [ red teal ]`). A gradient with options is named first or wrapped: `fill (gradient [ red teal ] angle 90)`, because connecting words belong to the call they sit in (§5.1).
- Gradients are drawn on the GPU in one pass per element, like solid colours.
- **A list of colours, nothing else** (decided 2026-10-29): every item of a list used as a colour has to be a colour (a number is a grey), and `[colours]` around a name that already holds the list is the classic slip, so it is a teaching error that shows the line without the extra brackets.

**Images, video and canvases as fills** (decided 2026-10-24). `fill` also takes an image, a canvas, a movie or the webcam. The element becomes a mask: the image shows only inside the shape, including text.

```
photo = load "cat.png"
fill photo                       // cover: fill the shape, crop what overflows
circle at 200 200 radius 150
square at 20 20 size 100 fill photo as fit       // whole image inside, empty bands if needed
rect at 0 0 size 400 100 fill photo as stretch   // distort to fit exactly
rect at 0 0 size width height fill pattern as tile size 64   // repeat at 64 pixels
square at 50 50 size 300 fill photo as center   // original size, centred, no scaling
text "rocinante" at 20 200 size 96 fill webcam
```

- **Modes:** `as cover` (the default: scale to fill the shape and crop the overflow), `as fit` (scale to show the whole image inside the shape), `as stretch` (scale each axis to the shape's box), `as tile` (repeat at the image's own size, or `size n`) and `as center` (no scaling, centred). `cover` is CSS's name for "fill"; rocinante uses it because `fill photo as fill` would read badly.
- `at x y` shifts the image inside the shape, in pixels, for `tile` and `center`.
- Image fills work with `stroke` too, and inside `with` blocks and named styles.
- **The words after a paint** (decided 2026-11-04): after the value of a per-element `fill` or `stroke`, an `as` belongs to the value, and so do the `size`, `at`, `in`, `angle` and `stops` straight after it (`rect at 0 0 size 50 50 fill photo as tile size 20`, `circle ... stroke [ red teal ] as radial`); the parser does this by position alone, and the checker reads them by what the value is: a picture takes `as cover|fit|stretch|tile|center`, `size` and `at`, a gradient list takes `as linear|radial|conic`, `angle`, `stops`, `at` and `in`. A plain color has no words, so there an `as center` is the shape's origin (`fill red as center` keeps its meaning), and `as corner` is always the origin. The label words (`size`, `at`) of the shape therefore go before the style words, where `roci fmt` puts them.
