The same words follow a `fill` of a `with` group (`with fill photo as fit [ ... ]`, where a color has no origin to take `as center`), and a `fill` statement takes them as its own options and so lasts.
- **Built so far** (2026-11-03, words 2026-11-04, the rest 2026-11-05): a picture or a canvas in `fill` on circles, ellipses, rectangles, triangles, quads and arcs, with every mode, on one shape or as a statement or after `with`; the outline is drawn over it. A picture in `stroke` (decided 2026-11-05): the outline of every shape, lines and points too, is cut into triangles by the stroker with the caps and joins of its style and drawn through the typeset pipeline like a fill, each corner placed in the picture by the shape's own box. A picture in `background`: a rectangle the size of the window under everything. A picture in a text's `fill`: the letters are cut out of the picture on the CPU (SDL_ttf's surface renderer gives the coverage, the picture's pixels give the colour by the same placement rules, with the text's box as the shape's box) and kept as one texture for each text, picture and fit, drawn as a textured quad (`gfx/textfill.odin`), because the glyph atlas shader has one texture and a second would mean a new compiled shader for every graphics system; a canvas fills a text since 2026-11-26 (#74) by being read back to the CPU first, as the pixel verbs read it, and the text is made again whenever the canvas was drawn into (`Canvas.drawn` is in the cache key), so a canvas drawn into every frame costs one read-back a frame, `examples/canvas_text`. Not yet: named styles holding a picture, a text's outline with a picture, and movies. The webcam (2026-11-19) is a picture, so it fills anything a picture does (`fill webcam`, a text too, cut out of each frame on the CPU like any picture fill).

**UI elements** accept the same words per widget (`button "stop" fill red`); theme-wide changes use the `ui` verbs in §4.8.

### 5.10 Files, libraries and names (decided 2026-10-23)

Creative coders shouldn't think about modules until they want to share something. What users need to know: "Put more files next to your sketch and they just work together. Write `use "name"` to bring in someone else's code; it's copied into your sketch, so it never breaks. Only when two words clash do you add the library name in front."

**Your own code: one folder, one program.** Every `.roci` file in the sketch folder (not in `assets/`, `libs/`, `output/`, a hidden folder, or a folder that holds a `sketch.roci` of its own, which is another program, such as a project's tool; decided 2026-11-20) belongs to the same program and sees every other file's verbs and constants, with no import line, no header files and no forward declarations. This follows Odin's model, where all files in a directory form one package. `sketch.roci` is where the program starts, and it is the only file with statements that run (below). The files are read together in one order, the other files by name and `sketch.roci` last (decided 2026-10-29, replacing dependency order: a constant read before the file that sets it is the ordinary "read before it has a value" error, and a verb may be called from anywhere, because every `learn` is read first). A message names the file and the line inside it (`palette.roci:4:2`). Splitting a sketch into `particles.roci` and `palette.roci` never changes what it does.

**Other people's code: `use`, vendored into the sketch.**

```
use "flocking"                             // short name from the index
use "github.com/someone/noise-tools"       // any git URL

draw [ flock birds ]
```

- `roci get <name or url>`, or the first `roci run` that meets a new `use` line, copies the library into `libs/<name>/` inside the sketch folder, with a small `source` file recording where it came from and which commit. Built (decided 2026-10-29): `roci get <git address> [--as name]` makes a shallow clone, drops its `.git` folder, writes `source` (`url` and `commit`) and also fetches what the library `use`s by git address; a library that is there from another address is said, never replaced. A `use` of a git address met by `roci run` or `roci check` is fetched the same way the first time. A short name has nowhere to be looked up until the index exists (Phase 9), so `roci get flocking` is an error that asks for an address. `libs/` is committed to git. Vendoring follows Odin's stance against package managers: a sketch runs offline and keeps running years later, even if the original repository disappears.
- `roci update <name>` (or `roci update` for all) fetches a newer version, shows what changed, and replaces the copy. Nothing ever updates by itself.
- **The index** is a plain git repository on GitHub mapping short names to git URLs, curated in the spirit of ofxAddons; Fabrizio curates it at first (decided 2026-10-24). Libraries are listed through a pull request to the index, which Fabrizio reviews (decided 2026-10-24). To be listed, a library:
  - lives in a public git repository under a licence allowed by §3's dependency policy;
  - passes `roci check` on the current rocinante release, and `roci test` when it carries checks (§5.16);
  - has a `README` with at least one example that runs;
  - has a lowercase name that doesn't clash with built-ins or with another listed library;
  - downloads nothing and runs no outside code when it loads;
  - names a maintainer who can be contacted.

  A library is removed from the index when it has been broken for two consecutive releases, or when its licence changes to one §3 doesn't allow. Sketches that already vendored it keep working, because `libs/` is theirs. No central server or registry is needed. Any git URL works without the index.
- **A library is a folder of `.roci` files**, optionally with its own `assets/` (found by name after the sketch's own assets, which always win) and a short `README`. Later (Phase 9) a library can also be an Odin or C extension using the stable verb ABI (§4.7); the `use` line is identical and users can't tell the difference. `roci get` and `roci run` tell the user that a native extension runs code, and ask once before loading one from a git URL (decided 2026-10-24). An extension ships two ways (decided 2026-10-24): as Odin source, which `roci package` compiles together with the core and the sketch (like the core itself, §4.7), and as prebuilt dynamic libraries for the four desktop systems, which `roci run` loads with Odin's `core:dynlib`, so no toolchain is needed while working. `roci` and its interpreter do not run on phones (iOS and Android are targets only, §7), so mobile needs only the source. The index provides a GitHub Actions template that builds an extension's desktop libraries for its authors.
- **Definitions only outside `sketch.roci`** (decided 2026-10-24). Files other than `sketch.roci`, and library files, hold only `learn`, constants, `use` and other definitions, evaluated in dependency order before `sketch.roci` runs, so splitting a sketch into files never changes what it does. A top-level drawing statement or a variable assignment in another file is a load-time error that says to move it into `sketch.roci` or into a verb.
- **Visibility.** Every verb a library teaches with `learn`, plus its constants, is available to the sketch. Helpers are kept inside with `hidden learn helper [ … ]`.
- Libraries may `use` other libraries; `roci get` vendors the whole set flat into `libs/`. Two libraries needing different versions of a third is a plain-language error at `roci get` time; `roci` never picks one silently.
- Packaging bundles `libs/` exactly like `assets/` (§4.9).

**Names: flat by default, qualified only on a clash.**

- After `use "flocking"`, its verbs are plain words: `flock birds`, never `flocking.flock birds`.
- **The sketch wins over libraries**, with a warning, so teaching your own `flock` is allowed and always announced.
- **Built-ins can't be redefined** by libraries or sketches: `learn circle [ … ]` is a load-time error suggesting another name.
- **Two libraries defining the same word** is a load-time error that teaches: "Both `physics` and `ui-kit` define `push`. Say which one you mean: `physics.push ball`." The qualified form `library.verb` exists only for this. The checker always knows whether a name is a library or a record, so `physics.push` and `p.x` can't be confused; a variable with the same name as a used library is itself a load-time error.
- **How a plain word is looked up** (decided 2026-10-29): a call written in a library finds that library's own verb first, then the sketch's, then a library's; a call written in the sketch finds the sketch's own first. So a library keeps using its own `glow` when the sketch teaches another, and a clash is only possible between two libraries (neither the sketch nor the caller's own library teaches the word). `hidden learn` is private to the library of the file that teaches it; in the sketch's own files it hides nothing. A library, like the sketch's other files, holds definitions only. `push` is a built-in, so the example above would be refused as a name; the rule is the same for any word.
- **A file of another place** (decided 2026-11-21, #59): `use "../look.roci"` (a `use` that ends in `.roci`) takes one file, by a path from the folder of the file that says it, with `.` and `..` worked out as text (`lang.use_file_path`; an absolute path stays as it is), into the program as one more of its own files, not a library: the same rules as a file in the folder (definitions only, constants first), its verbs and constants plain words, one reading however many files name it, and what it names in turn read too (a library it asks for is the program's own, found in the program's `libs/`). It is how a project's game, its icon tool and its tests share one source of their shapes and colors, since a folder with a `sketch.roci` of its own is another program. A path that is not there is `Use_File_Missing` at the line, with the path it looked for; a file named `sketch.roci` is never taken in. A library does not `use` a file of another place, since it must stand alone. `roci run` watches it with the rest of the program's files (`folder.read_program` lists it in `paths`), so saving it reloads the sketch by the rules of any file (checked 2026-11-21 by a test; an earlier note here said it did not).
- **Where a library comes from** (decided 2026-10-29): `use "name"` and `use "github.com/someone/name"` both mean the folder `libs/name/` of the sketch (the last segment of the name, without `.git`); the files in it join the program as that library's, and the libraries they `use` in turn. A `use` with no such folder is an error that says to run `roci get`. `use` is written at the top of a file, outside every block.
- `use` and `hidden` are keywords, lowercase like all others (§5.1).

### 5.11 Teaching verbs: `learn` (decided 2026-10-23)

Users extend the language by teaching it new verbs with `learn`, in the spirit of Papert's "teaching the turtle". A taught verb must look and feel exactly like a built-in one.

```
learn double n [
  give n * 2
]

learn star size with points 5 spin 0 [
  turn spin
  repeat points [ forward size  right (720 / points) ]
]

x = double 21
star 100
star 100 points 7 spin 15
```

- **Signature.** `learn name param param … [ body ]`. Parameters are plain names; their kinds are inferred (§5.3), never written. The number of required parameters is fixed, so the parser always knows where a call ends (§5.1).
- **Why `learn` and not Logo's `to`:** it reads as teaching the computer a word, and it frees `to` to be only a connecting word (`for i from 1 to 10`, `add x to particles`), removing an ambiguity.
- **Optional parameters with defaults** follow `with` in the header: `with points 5 spin 0`. At the call site they work exactly like built-in connecting words (`star 100 points 7`), in any order, each optional. Inside a `learn` header `with` introduces defaults; as a statement it opens a style group (§5.9). The two never appear in the same position, so they can't be confused.
- **Results are explicit: `give`.** `give value` hands a value back and ends the verb. There is no implicit "last expression" result: adding a line at the end of a verb can never silently change what it gives back.
- **`stop`** ends a verb early without a value, as in Logo.
- **Two kinds of verb, checked at load time.**
  - A verb containing `give` can be used in expressions: `x = double 21`.
  - A verb without `give`, like `star`, is a statement. `x = star 100` is an error that teaches: "`star` draws but doesn't give back a value."
  - If some paths through a verb `give` and others reach the end without giving, that's an error naming the path: "When `n` is negative, `double` reaches its end without `give`. Add a `give` there too." No hidden zero values come out of verbs.
  - All `give`s in one verb must give the same kind of value (§5.3).
  - Calling a giving verb as a statement and dropping its result is a warning, since it's usually a mistake.
- **One value out.** Several results come back as a record: `give { min: a, max: b }`, then `r.min`. No tuples.
- **Recursion** is allowed (trees, fractals, L-systems, as in Logo).
- **Verbs see their parameters, their own local names, and the sketch's top-level names and constants.** Assigning a name inside a verb that doesn't exist at top level creates a local; assigning a top-level name changes it. The checker warns when a local name hides a top-level one.
- **Sharing (decided 2026-10-23).** Numbers, vectors, colours and text are copied when passed or assigned. Lists and records are shared: a verb that changes a record changes the caller's record, which is what makes `learn step p [ p.pos = p.pos + p.vel ]` work, and what Processing users expect from objects and arrays. `b = a` with a record also shares; `b = copy a` makes an independent copy. Passing a constant (§5.1) to a verb that changes it is a load-time error. Because `b = a` sharing is the classic beginner trap, the checker adds a hint, not an error, when it sees a list or record changed through a second name ("`b` and `a` are the same list, so this also changes `a`. Use `copy a` for a separate one"), and the Phase 2 newcomer test probed it (decided 2026-10-27).
- **Two hints that never stop a sketch** (decided 2026-10-28). Changing a list or record through a name made by `b = a` says the two are one value and shows `b = copy a`; the hint fires at the change, not at the assignment, so a harmless `b = a` stays quiet. A parameter, `with` word or loop variable named like a top-level variable is a warning, since inside it that name means the new one.
- `learn`, `give`, `stop` and `with` are keywords, lowercase like all others (§5.1).

### 5.12 Game controllers and actions (decided 2026-10-24)

Gamepads, joysticks and other controllers work in every sketch with no setup, through SDL3's gamepad and joystick APIs, with hot-plugging on every platform.

```
p = gamepad                       // the first connected gamepad, or empty
circle at (p.leftstick.x * 100 + 200) 300 radius 20   // sticks are vectors from -1 to 1
if p.bottom [ jump ]              // buttons are true while held
for each pad in gamepads [ … ]    // every connected gamepad
```

- **Gamepads** follow SDL3's model: buttons are named by where they are on the pad, not by the letter printed on them, because the same position says A on one pad and B or ✕ on another. rocinante gives SDL3's positions friendlier names (decided 2026-10-24):

| rocinante field | SDL3 | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| `bottom`, `right`, `left`, `top` | `SOUTH`, `EAST`, `WEST`, `NORTH` | the four face buttons, by position |
| `leftstick`, `rightstick` | left and right stick axes | vectors from -1 to 1 |
| `leftstickpress`, `rightstickpress` | `LEFT_STICK`, `RIGHT_STICK` | pressing a stick in |
| `leftshoulder`, `rightshoulder` | `LEFT_SHOULDER`, `RIGHT_SHOULDER` | shoulder buttons |
| `lefttrigger`, `righttrigger` | trigger axes | 0 to 1 |
| `dpad` | `DPAD_UP`, `DPAD_DOWN`, `DPAD_LEFT`, `DPAD_RIGHT` | a vector: x and y each -1, 0 or 1 |
| `start`, `back`, `home` | `START`, `BACK`, `GUIDE` | menu buttons |
| `extra` | `MISC1`–`MISC6`, paddles, `TOUCHPAD` | a list of the remaining buttons |

  Input names use the same words: `"pad bottom"`, `"pad leftstick"`, `"pad lefttrigger"`, `"pad dpad"`. The docs show each pad family's printed labels next to the positions.
- **SDL3 knows most pads out of the box.** For unknown pads, rocinante accepts SDL mapping strings, added through SDL's mapping functions, and `roci` keeps a community mapping database up to date.
- **Joysticks** without a gamepad layout (flight sticks, wheels, arcade panels, custom hardware) appear in `joysticks`, each with `axes` and `buttons` lists.
- **Actions, so input can be remapped.** A sketch names what the player does and binds inputs to it, from any device:

```
bind "jump" to "pad bottom"
bind "jump" to "key space"          // several bindings, any of them works
bind "move" to "pad leftstick"
bind "move" to "keys w a s d"       // four keys become a vector, bound by physical position
on press "jump" [ … ]
if held "fire" [ … ]
pos = pos + (direction "move") * SPEED
```

- **Players remap at run time** with the `controls` widget (§5.8): it lists every action and its bindings, and pressing a new button or key rebinds it. Remapped bindings are remembered values, saved in `ui.toml` like any widget (§4.9); `forget ui` restores the sketch's own bindings. Packaged sketches start from the committed ones.
- **The `controls` widget is built** (2026-11-17, `roci/controls.odin`): `controls` in a `ui` block (placed with `at` and `width` like the others) makes a row for each action, in the order first bound: its name, a button for each input (the button's words are the input's text) and a `+`, then a `reset controls` button below. It is made of ordinary buttons (`ui.button_keyed`, whose identity is a key and not its words, so a button that says "press a key or button" is still the same widget and keeps the focus), so the keyboard and the screen reader work as for any button: the node's name is "jump, key space", "add another way to jump" and "reset controls to the sketch's own". Pressing an input's button starts `listen_begin`; the next key (taken in `step` before the log and the interface see it, so it is the choice and nothing else's, and Escape stops waiting, Backspace takes the input away and a key with no name is ignored), or the next gamepad button, trigger or stick that was not already held when the waiting began (`listen_pads` in `actions_step`, a pad is not told apart), is the new input. An input of four keys asks for four, one after the other. Mouse buttons are not offered: the click on the button would be the choice. The input chosen is ignored until it is let go (`just_chosen`), so the press that chose a key is not also a press of the action. A choice goes into the `[controls]` table of `ui.toml`, one array of input texts for each action (an empty array means the player took every input away), and an action's first `bind` takes the player's inputs and keeps the sketch's own lines in `Action.own` for `reset` (`action_reset`, `actions_reset_all`) and for `forget ui`, which also empties the table. C has `roci_controls`, `roci_rebind` and `roci_reset_controls`. A sketch's variable called `controls` is now the verb (`examples/studio` named its window that, and now calls it `tools`). Not done: a mouse button as a choice, a pad told apart by player number, and conflict warnings (two actions on one key).
- **Feedback:** `rumble 80 for 0.5 player 2` (strength 0 to 100 as volume is, `for` seconds, half a second if not written, `player` the pad's place from 1, the first if not written) where the pad supports it, and nothing, said or done, where it does not (built 2026-11-16). Pad gyroscopes, touchpads and lights come later, through the same SDL3 API.
- **Actions are built** (2026-11-16, `roci/actions.odin`, `roci/interp/lists.odin`): `bind "jump" to "pad bottom"`, `held`, `direction`, `on press` and `on release`, in C as `roci_bind`, `roci_action_held` and `roci_action_direction` (press and release are `roci_on` events 18 and 19). An action keeps its inputs in the order bound, in a thread-local list (`bind` of the same input again changes nothing), and is cleared when a sketch starts again (`begin_restart`; a changed or removed `bind` line is an effect, so a live reload restarts the sketch and runs every `bind` again). The input names are the design's, in lowercase with any spacing: `key`, `mouse`, `pad` (with an optional player number from 1; none means any pad), `keys` (four keys: up, left, down, right) and `joystick n button m` or `axis m`. A trigger, a stick or an axis counts as held beyond `ACTION_THRESHOLD`, 0.5; `direction` is the sum of each input's vector (a stick or the d-pad as it leans, the four keys as they point, an axis as x; a button gives none) shortened to a length of 1. Each step (`actions_step`, after the pads are read) notes the actions that changed as the happenings `Action_Press` and `Action_Release` in the focused window (or the first), so `on press` runs once for a press whichever input did it; with no window the state still changes and nothing is logged. `held "name"` asks the action when something was bound to that name and the keys and mouse buttons otherwise. A name that is no input is the runtime message `Input_Unknown` with the nearest names (`nearest_inputs`). The `controls` widget is built (below).
- **What is built** (2026-11-16, `roci/platform/pads.odin`, `roci/pads.odin`, `roci/interp/touch.odin`, `roci/abi_pads.odin`): `gamepad`, `gamepads`, `joysticks` and `rumble`, in C as `roci_gamepad_count`, `roci_gamepad_at`, the `roci_joystick_*` functions and `roci_rumble`. Not yet: mapping strings and the community database. The record `gamepad` has the fields of the table above and also `id`, `name` and `connected`; with no pad plugged in it is a pad at rest with `connected` false (decided 2026-11-16, instead of an empty value, so a sketch never has to test before reading one). Sticks are -1 to 1 with up negative, as the screen's y is, with a radial dead zone of 10% (`PAD_DEAD_ZONE`) taken off so a stick at rest reads exactly 0 and the full reach reads 1; triggers are 0 to 1; `dpad` is -1, 0 or 1 on each axis with opposite directions cancelling; `extra` is a list of 11 booleans (MISC1 to MISC6, four paddles, the touchpad press). A joystick is `id`, `name`, `axes` and `buttons`, each hat adding four buttons after the real ones (up, right, down, left). Devices are found by comparing the ids SDL lists with the ones open each frame (so hot-plugging is the same on every system), a device with a gamepad layout is never also a joystick, and SDL's own mappings decide which is which. **On FreeBSD** (checked 2026-11-20 with a wired DualShock 4, USB 054c:05c4, which the kernel's `ps4dshock` and `hgame` drivers present as evdev nodes that the `games` group can read) two things are put right in `pads.odin`: SDL's built-in mapping for the pad has the triggers on axes 3 and 4 and the right stick on 2 and 5, but FreeBSD numbers them left stick 0 and 1, L2 2, right stick 3 and 4, R2 5 (read with `EVIOCGABS`) and the buttons in the order of their key codes (cross 0, circle 1, triangle 2, square 3, the shoulders 4 and 5, Share 8, Options 9, PS 10, the stick clicks 11 and 12, read with `EVIOCGBIT`), so L2 moved the right stick, the right stick read as two half-pressed triggers and the four face buttons pressed the wrong ones (SDL's line has them shifted by one); `ps4_evdev_mapping` gives SDL the right line for that pad's own GUID before it is opened (`fix_pad_mapping`, FreeBSD only; the second model, 09cc, shares the driver and is untried). And the pad's motion sensors, a second evdev device that SDL lists as a second gamepad (the accelerometer mark reaches SDL through udev, which FreeBSD lacks), are left out, because a gamepad with no buttons is not one (`has_buttons`, `Pads.rejected`). The other pads tried on FreeBSD (2026-11-20, USB, the generic `hgame` driver): the DualSense (054c:0ce6) and the Logitech Dual Action (046d:c216) need nothing, because the kernel's layout is the one SDL's mapping expects (checked with `EVIOCGABS` and `EVIOCGBIT`). **`rumble` does nothing on the DualSense**: its event node lists no force feedback, SDL says `cap rumble = false` and "That operation is not supported", and `rumble_pad` hands that false back as for any pad that cannot shake (the DualShock 4 was not tried, its driver's manual lists the light bar only; the Logitech has no motors). **Xbox One and Series pads do not work over USB on FreeBSD**: the PowerA XBX Spectra (20d6:4002) and Microsoft's Xbox Series X controller (045e:0b12) speak the Xbox One protocol (class ff, subclass 47, protocol d0), `xb360gp` covers the Xbox 360's (ff/5d/01) only, so no driver takes them and the pad's logo keeps blinking while it waits for the host. SDL's own driver for the protocol (`SDL_JOYSTICK_HIDAPI_GIP`, libusb; it needs raw access to `/dev/usb`, so it was tried as root) finds the pad and then **freezes inside SDL's event handling** until the program is killed; it is not a way round, and a user who gives their account that USB access could have `roci` freeze at start with a Series pad plugged in (setting `SDL_JOYSTICK_HIDAPI_GIP=0` there would prevent it; not done, since nothing reaches it by default). An Xbox 360 pad through `xb360gp` was not tried. Reading starts the first time a sketch asks (`start_pads`), never in a headless sketch or a test, which use `feed_pads` (and read what `rumble` asked in `rumble_requests`). **Checked by hand on a Mac, 2026-11-16**: PS5, PS4 (Bluetooth), Xbox Series X (USB) and Logitech Dual Action are all found as gamepads, and their sticks, triggers, face buttons, shoulders and d-pad read as they should; `rumble` shakes the PS4 and PS5, the Logitech has no motors, and the **Xbox Series X pad does not shake** although SDL says it can and accepts the request (macOS gives it to SDL through Apple's GameController framework, with the direct Xbox driver forced on it behaved the same; not known whether Windows, Linux and FreeBSD differ). Not seen in the hand check: `home` and the stick presses on most pads. `rumble`'s word is `player` and not `pad`, because `pad` is a name sketches use.
- **Input names** are text, so one list covers keyboards, mice, gamepads and joysticks: `"key space"`, `"mouse left"`, `"pad bottom"`, `"pad leftstick"`, `"joystick 1 button 3"`. A name that doesn't exist is a load-time error that lists the closest names.

### 5.13 Collision detection (decided 2026-10-24)

Collision is built in. Shapes are plain records (§5.2), recognised by their fields, so the same record can be drawn and tested:

| Record fields | Shape |
|---|---|
| a vector (`{ 10, 20 }`) | point |
| `pos`, `radius` | circle (2D) or sphere (3D vector) |
| `pos`, `size` (and optional `angle`) | rectangle or box, rotated by `angle`; a record that also has `radius` is a circle or sphere |
| `from`, `to` | line segment |
| `points` | polygon (2D) |

A record that matches no row, or matches rows that disagree (for example `points` together with `from`), is a load-time error when it is passed to a collision verb, and the message lists the fields each shape needs (decided 2026-10-24). **A record is the shape its own `{ }` is written with** (decided 2026-10-29): records made in different places merge into one shape with every field when they meet in one list (§5.3), so a list of walls holding lines and circles is allowed, and each item stays what its braces made it (the checker checks every place such records are written; the interpreter keeps the kind on the record, and `copy` keeps it too). A field given later by an assignment does not change what shape a record is, so `b = { pos: p }` then `b.radius = 5` is still no shape: write the radius in the braces.

```
ball   = { pos: { 200, 100 }, radius: 10, vel: { 2, 3 } }
paddle = { pos: { 180, 380 }, size: { 80, 12 } }

if touching ball paddle [ ball.vel.y = -ball.vel.y ]
c = contact ball paddle            // { point, normal, depth }, every field 0 when apart
for each b in (hits ball bricks) [ remove b from bricks ]
if inside mouse paddle [ … ]
h = cast { from: eye, to: target } walls   // { item, point, normal, distance }: the first thing along a line
```

- **Verbs:** `touching a b` (true or false, edges included), `contact a b` (where they touch: `point`, `normal`, the direction to move `a` to part them, and `depth`, how far), `hits a list` (every item in a list that touches `a`, in list order), `inside point shape` (edges included) and `cast line list` (the first item a line from its `from` to its `to` meets: `item`, `point`, `normal` and `distance`). When there is nothing to give, `contact` and `cast` give a record whose fields are all 0 (and no `item`), the useful zero values of §5.2 (decided 2026-10-29); `touching` is the way to ask first.
- **Fast for many things.** `hits` and `cast` over lists use a broad phase (a tree of boxes) automatically, so a thousand particles against a thousand walls stays cheap: the interpreter keeps the tree of a list it is asked about twice without the list changing (every list and record carries the mark of its last change), and checks shapes one by one otherwise. Measured 2026-10-29: a thousand circles against a thousand rectangles each frame, 300 ms a frame one by one, 3 ms with the tree. A list whose items move while it is being asked about (particles against each other) is checked one by one.
- **Detection first, simulation when asked for.** Collision answers questions; what happens next is the sketch's choice.
- **2D physics** (decided 2026-10-24, first slice built 2026-11-28) builds on the same records and on Box2D v3's world simulation. `simulate ball` gives a circle (`pos` and `radius`) or a rectangle (`pos` and `size`, and `angle`) to the physics, which then moves the record's own `pos`, `angle`, `vel` and `spin` every frame, the fields it has (a record without `vel` is moved all the same, and a body whose record is never read still moves); `fixed wall` makes a shape that is not pushed: a `from` and `to` line is a static wall, and a `fixed` circle or rectangle is kinematic, which stays where it is until the sketch changes its `pos` or `angle` and then moves there in the frame, pushing what it meets (a paddle that follows the mouse); `link a b as hinge|rod|spring|weld|wheel|slider` holds two shapes of the physics to each other, with the options `at`, `to`, `axis`, `length`, `stiffness`, `damping`, `limit low high`, `motor`, `torque` and `collide` (the draft called it `join`, which is the verb that joins two lists, so it is `link`; `no link a b` lets go); two bodies have one link, saying `link` again changes the options named and keeps the rest (the same convention as sound `effect`), and it goes with either body (`roci/physics/joints.odin`: Box2D 3.2 fixes a joint when it is made, so a change makes it again, from the frames it kept in each body's own coordinates, so a hinge's angle 0 and a slider's place 0 stay where they were; `at` is in the world, defaulting to the first shape's middle, a rod or spring's ends to both middles, and a wall's middle is the middle of its line; `limit` is degrees for a hinge and pixels for the rest, a rod with a `limit` is a rope, a `motor` speed is degrees a second for a hinge or wheel and pixels a second for a slider and pushes with `torque`, 1000 by default; `Link_Refused` at run time). `gravity { 0, 400 }` sets the pull in pixels a second each second (the world has `0, 400` until then); `kick ball { 0, -300 }` adds a speed, however heavy the ball is (not `push`, which scopes style and transforms); `no simulate ball` takes a shape out (taking it out of a list does not); `simulate ball` again replaces its body. A field `hit: 0` is written by the physics each frame with the hardest impact its shape took in that step (approach speed in pixels a second, from Box2D's hit events above a threshold of 0.2 metres a second), and 0 after; a shape with `sensor: true` is a zone that is no obstacle, and `touching` is how a sketch asks what is in it (reporting of sensors by events was left out: the collision verbs do the same without the physics). The fields `bounce` (0 to 1, how much of its speed a ball keeps), `friction` and `density` on a record set how it feels, and what the sketch changes between frames (`ball.vel = ...`, `ball.pos = ...`) is taken in before the step. The world is made by the first body, stepped when the frame's blocks are done (so a sketch sees the same positions all through one frame), at a fixed step of the frame's own seconds (a headless frame is a sixtieth), and a sketch that never calls `simulate` has no world at all. Units: pixels (50 to a metre), degrees clockwise with y down, a box's `pos` its top left corner with the box turning about its middle, as `rect` and the collision verbs have it. A shape must be the record that lasts: one made inside `draw` or an event is made again each frame and is refused (`Body_Not_Lasting`); to simulate what a frame makes, `add` it to a list and give `simulate` the list's own copy (`simulate (item (count balls) of balls)`, `examples/pachinko`). An outline (a record with `points`, any number of corners, concave or not, at least three and with some area) is a body too: one convex outline of eight corners or fewer is one Box2D polygon, any other is cut into triangles by `geom.triangulate` (several shapes on one body, so the shape keeps its outline), the body's origin is the average of the corners, and the physics writes the moved `points` back each frame (into the record's list, in the order given) and `angle` if the record has one; a `fixed` outline is a static body (not moved, unlike a fixed circle or rectangle), what the sketch writes to `points` is not taken in, and `vel`, `spin` and the feel are. Meshes and 3D shapes are refused (the checker says so; `Body_Refused` at run time for numbers the physics cannot use). The same in C (`roci_simulate_circle`, `roci_fixed_wall`, `roci_body_pos` and the rest, bodies being numbers) and in compiled sketches. Joints, sensors by hit and outlines are all in; what is left of the plan is a run on the Pixel 9 and the other systems. 3D physics is on the roadmap (README.md).
- **In space** (decided 2026-11-13, Phase 4): the same five verbs take shapes that have three numbers in their places: a vector of 3 is a point, `pos` and `radius` a sphere (`pos` is its middle, as `sphere at` puts it), `pos` and `size` a box (its middle, as `box at` puts it) that `angle` turns (three numbers, the turns in degrees about x, y and z, the same as `turn x around x`, `turn y around y` and `turn z around z` written one after the other before the box), `from` and `to` a segment, and `triangles` (a list of vectors, three for each triangle) a mesh. A mesh is a surface: a ball wholly inside a closed one touches none of its triangles. `triangles "hall.glb" at 0 0 0 size 500` gives the triangles of a model as `model` draws it, so `{ triangles: triangles "hall.glb" at 0 0 0 size 500 }` is a shape of a loaded model, and `ray pixel` gives the line from the camera through a pixel (a record of `from` and `to`, three numbers each, in the world) so `cast (ray mouse) things` picks what is under the mouse. A shape is flat or in space, never both (the checker says so, and that a flat box has a number for its `angle` and a box in space three, that an outline has no place in space and a mesh no flat places), a flat shape and a shape in space never touch, and `contact` and `cast` give 3-vectors when their shapes have three numbers. The code is `roci/collide/space.odin`: every shape is a core (a point, a segment or a box) with a radius around it, two shapes touch when their cores are no further apart than the two radii together (a gap of exactly 0 counts, as in the plane), the shortest distance between two cores is found between their features (each place of one to the nearest place of the other, and each edge to each edge, the places of a box being found in its own turned coordinates), they overlap when an edge of one goes through the other, and the contact of two that overlap is the axis of least movement (the faces of both, the edges across each other, and a few more that cannot do harm). A cast is the slab method for a box (in the box's own coordinates, so a turned box is cut like any), the quadratic for a sphere, Moller and Trumbore for a triangle, and the crossing of two segments for a point or a segment. A list asked about twice unchanged is kept as a tree of boxes (`collide.Tree3`, built as the flat one is, and tested to answer what checking every shape answers); its answers are tested against stepping along the line and against the push-out of each contact. `examples/collide3d`.
- **Box2D arrives** (decided 2026-11-27, first slice, the library): Box2D 3.2.0 (MIT) is fetched by `tools/fetch-physics.sh` (GitHub's archive of the tag; its checksum is pinned and checked) and built static by `tools/physics-libs.sh` with cmake (`BOX2D_VALIDATE` off, no samples, tests or documentation, SIMD on as upstream has it) into `.cache/physics/lib` (`libbox2d.a`, `box2d.lib` with MSVC; not run on Windows yet), and for the phones into `.cache/physics-ios` and `.cache/physics-android` (both built and checked on this Mac; the phones' `libs.sh` copy them into their own lib folders and `tools/mobile-ios.sh` links the iPhone's); `ci` and `docs` build it when it is missing or older than the scripts (`build/physics.odin`, `odin run build -- physics-libs`). The bindings are our own (`vendor/box2d/box2d.odin`, the part of the C API physics uses: worlds, bodies, circle, capsule, segment and polygon shapes, the six joints, events and a ray cast), each structure laid out as the C one, which `box2d_test.odin` checks against the sizes and offsets a C compiler gave (3.2 changed the joints: a joint is two bodies and a frame in each, `Joint_Def` first). Box2D works in 32-bit floats and in metres (its tolerances are about a centimetre), so the physics package tells it how many pixels a metre is (`b2SetLengthUnitsPerMeter`) and keeps sketches in pixels.
- **Implementation** (revised 2026-10-29): 2D detection is rocinante's own Odin, package `roci/collide`, in 64-bit like every number in the language. Box2D was the plan, but `vendor:box2d` is Box2D 3.1.1 (3.2.0 is out), ships libraries only for macOS, Windows and WebAssembly, works in 32-bit and takes only convex polygons of at most 8 corners; Box2D now arrives with 2D physics in Phase 6. Every shape is cut into convex pieces of at most four corners with a radius around them (a polygon, concave or not, into geom's triangles), touching edges count as touching (a gap of exactly 0), a rectangle's `pos` is its top-left corner as in `rect at pos size size` and its `angle` turns it clockwise about its centre, `contact` gives the direction to move the first shape (from the second towards the first) and the depth, and a line that starts inside a shape meets it at its start. `hits` and `cast` go through a tree of boxes (`collide.Tree`) whose answers are tested against checking every shape. 3D starts with rocinante's own sphere, box, segment and ray tests in Odin; Box3D (Box2D's 3D sibling, MIT, also in `vendor:`) is reconsidered once it has releases and covers iOS and Android.
- **Pictures with see-through parts** (decided 2026-11-05): a picture is not a shape, and as its rectangle it counts transparent corners as solid. `outline photo [at p] [size v] [alpha n]` gives the corners of the solid part as a list of vectors, so `{ points: outline photo at p }` is a polygon the verbs above already take; there is no new shape and no change to `collide`. A pixel is solid when its alpha, as a percentage, is more than `alpha` (50 when not written, 0 meaning every pixel not fully clear); the biggest group of solid pixels that touch along their sides is walked round on pixel edges and the corners a straight run within one pixel can replace are dropped (`geom.trace_outline`: Douglas and Peucker on a closed polygon), so holes and the smaller pieces are left out (a collision `points` is one polygon). `at` and `size` put it where `image` draws the picture. It reads the picture's pixels, so a canvas (whose pixels are on the GPU) and a missing picture give an empty list, which touches nothing; it walks every pixel, so a sketch makes it once and not every frame. Per-pixel tests between two pictures, and a list of polygons for a picture of separate pieces, were the other choices, left for later. `examples/outline`.
- Results are identical interpreted and compiled, because both call the same core functions (non-negotiable 7).

### 5.14 Implementation notes

- Hand-written lexer and recursive-descent parser in Odin (for error quality), producing an AST with full source spans. A call collects its arguments greedily until it meets something that can't be an argument: a verb that takes arguments, a verb that gives no value, a connecting word, `]` or the end of the line (§5.1). No types are needed to parse, but the parser does need the verb table: before parsing a file it reads the `learn` headers of the whole program (the sketch's other files and `libs/`), because taught verbs fix argument counts and add their own connecting words. A connecting word is recognised only once the call's required arguments are filled (§5.1). Edge case: a giving verb with no parameters (`anywhere`) is a name, so the checker warns when one follows a complete call on the same line.
- The grammar is written down as `docs/grammar.ebnf` (decided 2026-10-27), kept beside the parser and checked by the parser's tests; it is the grammar itself, not a second design document. It is also what a syntax reference, highlighting grammars and constrained generation for local models are made from.
- Checker and type inference run before anything executes, producing the typed IR (§4.7). Spans are preserved on every AST node, IR node and literal, and carried by every runtime command (§4.3), so runtime errors, highlighting, the source map for generated Odin and later editor features all point at `.roci` lines. This is cheap to build in from the first commit and expensive to add later.
- The interpreter executes the typed IR directly (no separate AST walker). Keep it simple and correct; it is the reference implementation that the code generator is tested against.
- Use Odin's allocators deliberately: an arena per frame for temporary values, a long-lived allocator for sketch state, so hot reload can discard and rebuild cleanly.

### 5.15 Language details (decided 2026-10-24)

The rules below fill the gaps the 2026-10-24 review found. Where they touch another section, that section says so.

- **`count` of a text** (decided 2026-11-17): `count "hello"` is 5, the characters of the text (a letter with an accent is one), as the interpreter always did; the checker used to refuse it. There is still no `length`.
- **Names that are words, calls in lists, shared lists** (decided 2026-11-18, `roci/lang`): a name of the writer's that is also a word of the language (a parameter, or the target of `box = 3`) is said once where it is made, and the parser then reads every later use of it (in that verb for a parameter, in the file for a variable) as the name, not as the verb, so one mistake is one message. A call written as an item of a list without round brackets (`[ resize pic size 10 0 ]`) is one message, `Call_In_List`, whose fix wraps the whole call. A verb that takes nothing, written as an item (`[ spot ]`), is `Verb_In_List` with the same fix. The hint for brackets three deep, when the innermost value reads `it` or `result` inside a list block, names the round bracket that holds the block instead (the line above the block knows neither), and says nothing when there is none. A name given a new value stops sharing the list or record it shared (`b = a`, then `a = []`), so the shared-value hint follows the name to its latest value. A `give` of another kind has no sample when the verb gives a list or a record, because a made-up one is not code anyone would write; a wrong value given to `shade` keeps `shade` in the fix.
- **`for` counts up** (decided 2026-11-18, replacing counting down when the end was smaller): `for i from a to b` runs `b - a + 1` times when `b >= a` and no times when `b < a`, so `for i from 1 to count` is right for an empty count; `for i from 10 down to 1` counts down, and a negative `by` still does. `down` is the one new word, read only there.
- **`min` and `max`** (decided 2026-11-18): `min a b` and `max a b` give the smaller and the larger of two numbers (`clamp` stays for both limits at once); a value that is not a number loses to one that is. They are reserved words now, and C has `roci_min` and `roci_max`.
- **`numbers from a to b [by s]`** (decided 2026-11-18): a list of numbers to go through or `collect` over, counting up (empty when `b` is below `a`; a negative `by` counts down), at most a million long. C has no counterpart: its caller makes the array.
- **`put` on a list** (decided 2026-11-18): `put scores 3 100` gives item 3 of a list (counted from 1, as `item` counts) a new value of the kind the list holds; an item that is not there is the same runtime error `item` gives and nothing changes, and `add` is how a list grows. A constant list refuses it, as `add` does.
- **`flip picture [as vertical|both]`** (decided 2026-11-18): a new picture turned left to right, top to bottom or both, a pixel verb like `crop` (kept by what made it, the picture is never changed; `gfx.pixels_flip`, `roci.flip_picture`, `roci_flip` in C). A picture drawn bigger is still smoothed: hard edges on `image` are the open pixel-art question.
- **`roci man words`** (decided 2026-11-18, changed the same day by contextual words, below): lists the words a name cannot be, the keywords and the grammar words, made from the registry (`registry.words_kept`), so it cannot go out of date.
- **A verb that sets a sketch's name** (decided 2026-11-18): names are sketch-wide, so a name a verb gives a value that the sketch also uses changes it for everyone, which the guide says. The checker adds a hint (`Verb_Sets_Sketch_Name`, no sample) when a verb gives such a name a value and then reads it there (widened 2026-11-18 from values made from the verb's parameters, because `best = 999` before a loop that compares with `best` is the commonest form of the trap), which is what a name of the verb's own looks like; an emptied list (`ships = []`), a new table (`cells = table`, added 2026-11-19), a name read before it is set and an update (`score = score + n`) are the point of those verbs and say nothing; the hint's text says (2026-11-19, issue #30) that a value verbs step is better kept as a field of a record (`state.speed`), which it leaves alone, since WILDVEX moved its stepped values there and found it reads well. A verb that means the sketch's name and reads it afterwards can reorder, or tween to it; examples `life` and `sengen` were changed so they check cleanly. Making a name first given in a verb that verb's own was left as it is (a name only a verb gives is already its own).
- **`language` and fonts in a stack** (decided 2026-11-21, #55): `language` is a built-in text value, the player's first preferred language as SDL lists it (`SDL_GetPreferredLocales`) written `en`, `pt-BR` or `ja`, and empty when the system says nothing and in a headless run (so goldens and tests are the same on every computer; a test says what the system lists with `roci.language_pretend_as`); C has `roci_language`. A font named with commas, `font "Aileron, NotoSansJP"`, is a face of its own (`Face.stack`, made by `add_face_stack`, found by the whole written name): each name is found as a single one is, the first draws and a copy of each other at the same size is added as a fallback of the first before the system's, so a character the first lacks comes from the next that has it, in the order written; one name nowhere makes the whole line a missing font. C has `roci_add_font_stack(name, members)`. Not done: a font found by the characters alone, with no names written, beyond the computer's own fallbacks that were already there; and what a missing word in a language does, which is the `Give_Fields_Differ` hint below.
- **Saying when a value is quietly empty** (decided 2026-11-21, #60, #52, #55): three more places that did nothing and said nothing. `Give_Fields_Differ` is a hint at check time on a `give` whose record has other fields than the one an earlier `give` of the same verb handed back (found in the pass that does not report, because the sites of record literals keep their shapes and the second pass sees them already merged), with the record written with the first missing field added; the case it was made for is a verb that gives one record per language and a language that forgets a word. `Field_Missing` is a run-time warning, said once for the place, when a line reads a field a record's shape does not have (a record that came out of a list of mixed values, a box), naming the fields it has and the nearest one; a field that is in the shape but was never given a value is not known to be missing at run time (an unset field holds the zero of its kind), which is why the hint is made at check time. `Constant_Not_Made` is a run-time warning, said once, when a constant that was never given its value is read: the constant is a constant, so reading it early is always a mistake, and it is how a verb that reads a constant written below it (one the pass could not move up, because it holds a variable, `time` or a taught verb) was silent before. A variable read before its first value still reads as the zero of its kind with no warning, since a variable that is set later is ordinary.
- **A loop that runs no times** (decided 2026-11-20, #60): `for i from 12 to 0` counts up, so it never runs; the checker adds the hint `Loop_Runs_No_Times` when both ends are written as numbers (`12`, `-3`), there is no `by` and no `down`, and the end is below the start, and shows the line with `down` in it. A `for` whose ends are names or sums is not looked at, since only a run knows. The other two quiet cases of that issue (a field a record lacks, a constant read before it is made by a verb) have no hint yet, and need a warning at run time.
- **Files run in the order of their names** (said 2026-11-18): a constant that uses a constant of a file read later is `No_Value_Yet`, and the message now names the file that gives the value and says files are read in the order of their names; reading definitions first was not done, because a file's top-level lines are statements that run in that order. **Constants are made first** (decided 2026-11-18, for #41): `hoist_constants` in `roci/lang/hoist.odin` puts every top-level constant whose value is only literals and other constants ahead of the other lines, each after what it uses (a loop of constants or a constant given twice stays where written and says what it said before), in the checker's list of statements, so the interpreter and the code generator run the same order. The words inside the quotes of a text are only text and never decide it (found 2026-11-20, #52: a record of texts holding `ship` or `OCTOPUS` was not moved, so a constant made from it by a verb, written above it, was an empty record), but a name in a hole (`"n is {n}"`) counts. A constant that uses a variable, a verb the sketch taught, or `random`, `noise`, `load`, `recall`, `read`, `clipboard`, `chosen`, `time`, `frame`, `mouse` or `now` is not moved, nor one that waits on such a constant. Variables are not moved: two lines of a variable may change it in turn, so their order is the sketch's; inside a verb, `draw` or an event every name exists, as before.
- **A headless canvas does not pile up** (fixed 2026-11-18): a canvas drawn into every frame of a headless run kept every frame's commands until `picture` drew them all at once, which ran Vulkan out of descriptor sets after about a hundred frames of four hundred shapes. Now, past 16 waiting frames or 2000 waiting commands, `flush_canvases` draws them as they come when there is a device, and lets them go when graphics are refused (nothing could show them). **The GPU is waited for** (fixed 2026-11-18, after the first fix was not enough on lavapipe): every canvas round is a command buffer of its own, and the descriptor sets its draws take go back to their pool only when the GPU has finished it, so a headless run that drew over a canvas every frame (the round binds the canvas's last texture) still ran out after about 200 frames. `canvas_submit` in `renderer_canvas.odin` now submits with a fence and waits on every 16th round (`CANVAS_ROUNDS_PER_WAIT`); checked on Ubuntu 24.04 in a container: the one-circle sketch crashed at 2000 frames before and passes 6000 after. There is no unit test, because the crash needs a Vulkan device and the tests run with graphics refused. **Only a run that will take pictures draws as it fills** (fixed 2026-11-18, after a segfault in `interp.test_the_studio_example...` on ORION): `flush_canvases` started SDL's video system (`drawing_renderer`) from a unit test as soon as the studio's trails crossed the 2000-command limit, which is racy among test threads and crashed there. `start_headless(seed, pictures)` now says whether the run will take pictures; `roci run`, `roci test`, the golden scenes, the site tools and `roci_start_headless` say so, a unit test never does, and without it (and with no device yet) the rounds simply wait as before.
- **Keys pressed by a script** (decided 2026-11-18, `cli/inputs.odin`): `roci run --frames N` takes `--press key@frame` and `--hold key@from-to` (a key named as `on key` names it, frames counted from 0 as `frame` is, a hold including both ends), and `roci test --accept` takes the same and stores them in `tests/snapshot.txt` as `press key@frame` and `hold key@from-to` lines, so the picture is compared after the same play. They reach the sketch as key events in the first window through `roci.step`, so `on key`, `held`, `key` and the actions bound to keys all see them; mouse buttons and pads are not scripted. **Fingers too** (decided 2026-11-20): `--touch x,y@frame` (one frame), `--touch x,y@from-to` (held) and `--drag x1,y1-x2,y2@from-to` (moving in a straight line, one step a frame) give `Finger_Changed` events in the first window, with places in the window's units (never negative) turned into the fractions the system gives, and stored as `touch` and `drag` lines; each step is its own finger, so two at once make `touches` hold two; `roci.handle_finger` rounds a place to a thousandth of a unit because the 32-bit fraction does not give back the units it was made from.
- **`no smooth` and `smooth`** (decided 2026-11-18): a style word, like `blend`, that lasts until changed (the frame in a `draw` block). After `no smooth` the pictures and canvases drawn read with a nearest sampler (`Style.nearest`, `Typeset_Draw.nearest`, `typeset.sampler_nearest`) so each pixel stays a hard square at any size; filled shapes, text and `fill`/`stroke` with a picture are not changed. `smooth` is the start. The other two ideas of the issue (`canvas w h exact` and `window ... pixels w h`) are not built.
- **Where `remember` and the vault keep their files** (decided 2026-11-18): `memory.toml`, `vault.bin`, `vault.bin.bak` and `vault.key` stay beside the sketch for `roci run`, which is where a sketch is made; the guide tells writers to keep the folder out of a cloud-synced one (iCloud keeps both copies of a file that is replaced, which made `vault 2.bin`), and `roci vault show` starts with a comment line giving the vault's full path. A packaged sketch cannot write beside itself (an iOS bundle is read only, and an app in `/Applications` or `Program Files` should not), so `roci package` (Phase 8) puts the same files in the system's app-data folder, `SDL_GetPrefPath` with the sketch's id from `sketch.toml`, and embeds `vault.key`; moving `roci run` to that place too was left out, so the files stay where a developer can see and commit-ignore them.
- **Operators.** From tightest to loosest: calls (§5.1), `^` (decided 2026-11-17: numbers only, groups to the right, binds tighter than a unary minus in front, so `-2 ^ 2` is -4; a result that is not a number is 0 as a division by zero is), unary `-` and `not`, `*` `/` `%`, `+` `-`, comparisons (`<` `<=` `>` `>=` `is` `is not`), `and`, `or`. Equality is written `is`, because `=` is assignment. What other languages write is a teaching error that gives the word and the line that works (`Habit_Operator`, decided 2026-11-11): `==` for `is`, `!=` for `is not`, `&&` for `and`, `||` for `or` and a leading `!` for `not`; the lexer makes each one token that stands for its word, so it is said once and the rest of the line is read as meant, with nothing else said. `%` is modulo. `+` with text on either side joins as text (`"fps: " + fps`). A minus is unary when there is a space before it and none after (`-10`), and subtraction otherwise (`x - 10`); the lexer decides, as `roci fmt` writes it (§5.1).
- **The name of a `for each` is the item the loop is on** (decided 2026-11-11): reading it reads the item, and assigning it changes that item, so `for each w in words [ w = w + "!" ]` changes the list (numbers and text too, as a field of a record already does, because the items of a list are shared). It makes no name of the sketch and is not there after the loop (the same for `for i from 1 to 10`), and a name of the sketch with the same spelling is hidden inside the loop, which the checker says. Changing an item of a constant list, by assigning the loop name or one of its fields, is `Constant_Changed_By_Verb` with a `copy` of the list as the fix.
- **Lists.** Indexing starts at 1, like `for i from 1 to 10`: an item is read with `item 1 of scores` (`of` joins the connecting words). `count scores` gives the length, `add x to scores` and `remove x from scores` change it, and changing a list while `for each` walks it is a load-time error when the checker can see it and an overlay error otherwise. Lists of records from different creation sites unify to the union of their fields, missing fields at zero (§5.3).
- **Tables** (decided 2026-10-29, replacing the planned `map`, which stays the number remapper, and `set`, which changes a window). A table holds text names with a value each, all values of one kind, inferred, kept in the order they were put so both back ends agree (§4.7): `counts = table`, `put counts "cat" 3`, `get counts "cat"` (the empty value of its kind, 0 or empty text, when the name is missing), `keys counts` (a list of text, in that order), `count counts`, `remove "cat" from counts`. Like lists and records, a table is shared and `copy` makes another; a constant table never changes. It is walked by its names, `for each name in (keys counts) [ ... ]`, and walking the table itself is a teaching error that shows that line. `print` shows `{ "cat": 3, "owl": 1 }`. Names are text for now; numbers as names can come later.
- **Text.** `length`, `split text by ","`, `join list by ","`, `number text` (number from text, 0 if it isn't one), `upper`, `lower`.
- **`print` and logging.** `print value` writes to the console by default, in the interpreter and in packaged sketches. `log value` does the same with a timestamp, and `log to "file.txt"` also sends everything `log` prints to that file (in `output/` unless the path is explicit). Packaged sketches also write runtime errors to a log in the platform's usual place, since they have no overlay (§5.7).
- **Scope.** Assigning a name inside `draw`, `repeat`, `ui` or an `on` block, or the block of any other verb (`push [ n = 3 ]`, since 2026-11-03), creates a sketch-wide name, unless the name is a verb parameter or local (§5.11). `ui [ size = slider … ]` relies on this.
- **Drawing outside `draw`.** Drawing verbs inside an `on … receive` or other event block go into that window's next frame, in the order they ran.
- **Conventions.** The origin is the top left with y pointing down. Positions and sizes are in logical pixels; HiDPI is automatic. Angles are in degrees everywhere, including `sin`, `cos`, `right` and `angle`; `pi` and `tau` exist for the radians-minded, with `radians` and `degrees` to convert. Time is in seconds. A gamepad stick or `dpad` y of -1 is up, matching the screen.
- **Defaults.** Window 800 × 600, background light grey (230), fill black, stroke off, thickness 1, `fps` 60, font rocinante's default (§4.8). The first `window` call replaces the default window; a sketch that never calls `window` gets the default one.
- **Fields.** A record's fields are those its literals give and those assigned anywhere in the sketch, even on a later line (decided 2026-10-28). Reading a field that none of them gives is an error with the fields there are ("did you mean `vel`?"), because a typo is far more likely than a field that is meant to read 0; a field some line gives reads 0 until set (§5.2). Records made in different places that meet in one list or one name become one shape with all their fields (§5.3).
- **True and false.** `true` and `false` are built-in values (decided 2026-10-28), so a flag is `running = true` and `if running [ ... ]`; a comparison, `and`, `or` and `not` give them too, and a condition has to be one of them.
- **Names** (changed 2026-11-18, #42: **contextual words**). Only the keywords (`registry.KEYWORDS`: `and check draw each else every expect for give hidden if is it learn not on or receive repeat result stop ui use while with`) and the grammar words (`registry.GRAMMAR_WORDS`: `as at by from in of over to`) cannot be names (`Reserved_Name`, `Learn_Reserved_Name` for a parameter). Every place a line gives a name says it the same way (2026-11-24): an assignment whose first word is a keyword is read as the assignment it is, not as that keyword's statement (`repeat = 2` once said "I did not expect this here" and `hidden = 1` "learn needs a name"), and a loop's name and a message's name are said in the parser (`reserved_as_name`); the parser then keeps the word in `Parser.taken`, so a later use reads as that name and the one message is all that is said. A verb `learn` cannot teach under a kept word or a word of the language (`learn post`, `learn repeat`) goes in `Parser.untaught`, and a later line calling it is skipped quietly (for a keyword, only when the word stands alone, so `repeat 3 [ ... ]` is still a `repeat`); a keyword as a parameter's name is its name inside that verb only; and the checker drops what any line says about a word already said to be taken (`Wrong_Argument_Count`, `Missing_Block` and the like, by the word they name). Every other word of the language, a verb, a built-in value, a color or a word that goes with a verb, is a word only where the grammar expects it and a sketch's own name anywhere else. How: `own_names` (`roci/lang/prescan.odin`) finds before parsing every name a file group gives a value (`name =` at the start of a line, a `for` loop's name) that is also a word of the language, per library (`Own_Name.library`), so a library that uses `count` keeps the verb whatever a sketch calls its numbers (`lookup_here` does the same for reading and writing in the checker); the parser then reads such a name as a name where a value is due (`find_callee`, `is_stop_word`), while at the start of a line the verb wins (`count = 0` is an assignment, `count 5` the verb) and where the verb takes an option word the option wins (`circle at spot radius radius`: the first is the option, the second the sketch's value). A name that hides a verb, read right after `=` or `(` with a word after it (`n = count herd` with `count = 0` above), gets the error `Name_Hides_Verb`, pointing at that place and at the line of the name, since the reader meant the verb (the stray word after the name is an error whichever way it is read, and `roci fmt` must not run on it: with only a hint, the formatter dropped the word). A parameter of a taught verb hides a word only inside its verb. `width`, `height` and `size` were already free as parameters. Not done: `roci fmt` adding brackets round a name that looks like an option word (`size`), and a message where a name hides a verb that is used at the start of a line (the verb wins there, silently).
- **`copy`** is deep: lists and records inside are copied too (§5.11).
- **Spelling.** Language words use US spelling (`color`, `colorpicker`, `gray`, `center`), and so does everything users read (decided 2026-10-30, so a reader never sees "colour" in the text and has to write `gray` in the code): the documentation site, the reference and `roci man`, every error message, the `roci` program's output, and the examples' comments and names. The design files and code comments may stay British; the C API is US too (`roci_fill_gray`, `roci_color`, renamed the same day). The checker accepts `colour`, `grey` and `centre` as friendly errors that name the right word.
- **Runtime failures** (non-negotiable 3). An index out of range or a division by zero shows the overlay and gives an empty value or 0, and the sketch continues. Recursion deeper than a fixed limit raises an overlay error in both back ends, so compiled code never overflows the stack. In the interpreter a watchdog stops a `draw` block that runs far too long, keeps the last good frame and explains. Packaged sketches log these errors instead of drawing an overlay.
- **Mouse buttons and the wheel** (decided 2026-10-29). `on mouse down` and `on mouse up` take a button word, `left`, `middle`, `right`, `back` or `forward` (the side buttons), and without one run for any button: `on mouse down right [ ... ]`. Whether a button is held now is `held "mouse right"`, the input names of §5.12 (`held "left"` stays the left arrow key); `pressed` stays "any button". `wheel` is a built-in value, a vector of how far the wheel turned since the last frame, y up and x to the right for sideways scrolling: `zoom = zoom + wheel.y * 0.1`.
- **Touch** (decided 2026-10-29). `on touch down [ ... ]` and `on touch up [ ... ]` run when a finger goes down or comes up, and inside them `finger` is that finger; anywhere else `finger` is a teaching error that points to `touches`, and a taught verb that needs the finger is given it (`mark finger`). `touches` is every finger down in the window now, in the order they went down. A finger is a record: `pos` (window coordinates, like `mouse`), `start` (where it went down), `id` (a number that stays the same while it is down and is never given to another finger in a run) and `pressure` (0 to 1); an `on touch up` finger is where it was last. Mouse and fingers stand in for each other both ways: a finger also moves `mouse` and runs `on mouse down left`, and on the desktop the left button is a finger too, so a touch sketch can be tried without a touch screen (a sketch with both kinds of block sees one click twice). A finger the system cancels counts as lifted. `examples/touch`.
- **Tweens and easing** (decided 2026-10-29). `tween place to value over seconds as curve` starts moving a number, a vector or a colour from where it is to `value`, a little each frame, and lands exactly on it: `on mouse down [ tween ball.pos to mouse over 0.8 as elastic ]`. The place has to last from frame to frame, so it is a sketch-wide name or a field of a record (a loop's item works, because records are shared); a local name, a worked-out value, or text is a load-time error, and a constant is the constant error. A new tween of the same place replaces the old one; a reload or a restart drops every tween. Seconds are plain numbers, as all time is (`over 0.5`). `ease amount as curve` is the same curve as a plain verb, an amount from 0 to 1 (held there) onto how far along a movement is, for sketches that drive time themselves: `x = 40 + (ease t as out) * 480`. The curves are `smooth` (the default: slow, fast, slow), `linear`, `in`, `out`, `back` (a little past the end and back), `elastic` and `bounce`, Robert Penner's cubic and out-curves, in `roci/maths` for both back ends. Colours are mixed in Oklab on the way, like gradients.
- **Mixed 2D and 3D** (§4.3). In one `draw`, a 2D verb after a 3D verb draws in screen space on top of the 3D scene. The default mouse orbit steps aside while the mouse is over a widget or when the sketch reads the mouse.


- **`level` and `bands` written alone, in a sketch that makes no sound** (nothing plays, loads a sound file, makes a `synth` or reads `mic` or `speakers`) is the warning `Level_Without_Sound`: both words are taken, so a writer who meant a name of their own gets a silent 0 and no other message (found 2026-11-17, when `remember "level" level` stored 0). The first pass of `check` records where a sound can come from (`sound_source`), the second says it.

### 5.16 Checks and snapshots (decided 2026-11-02)

Tests are optional and light, because classic unit testing is a hard sell to artists and making it compulsory would go against the manifesto. Two groups gain from them: library authors (the index asks that a library passes `roci check`, §5.10, which proves only that it has no errors, not that it works) and anyone whose sketch must keep looking the same (installations, commissioned work, teaching examples). The design is one keyword and one verb for behaviour, one stored picture for looks, and one command for both. Pyret's `where:` and `check:` blocks, which sit beside the code so a test reads like an example of use, are the model.

```
learn double n [ give n * 2 ]

check "double" [
  expect double 21 is 42
  expect double -1 is -2
]
```

- **`check "name" [ ... ]`** is a statement outside every block (like `use`; `Check_Not_Top`), with a name in quotes (`Check_Needs_Name`). It may stand in any file, including a library's and the sketch's other files, where it counts as a definition (`Definitions_Only` allows it), so it can sit right under the verb it tests. **`expect condition`** is a statement that may only stand inside a check (`Expect_Outside_Check`); its condition is an ordinary expression that has to be true or false (`Condition_Not_Boolean`), so `expect double 21 is 42` needs no brackets and `expect (double 21) is 42` works too.
- **`roci run` and a packaged sketch never run a check** (the interpreter skips `IR_Check`, and code generation will leave it out), so a check can never change what a sketch does or draws. `roci check` still reads and checks its words. Non-negotiable 1 holds: nothing is required.
- **What a check sees.** It runs once, after the sketch has set itself up (so every sketch-wide name has its value), in a frame of its own: the names a check makes are its own, like a taught verb's locals (`check_check` in the checker reuses that rule), and it reads and may change the sketch's names. It cannot `give` or `stop` (the parser refuses, as outside a `learn`). Calls of taught verbs inside a check make instances like any call.
- **A failed `expect` is a message** (`Expect_Failed`, a runtime message found by the interpreter, with a sketch in `tests/errors/runtime/expect_failed/`), in the teaching style of every other error: the line with carets under it and a sentence of what both sides of `is`, `is not`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` were (each side is worked out once, so a `random` or a call that draws is not run twice). Numbers are compared exactly like `is` in a sketch; when two numbers differ only by rounding (within 1e-9, relative) the sentence says so and suggests whole numbers or a small difference, because `expect (0.1 + 0.2) is 0.3` is the one failure every beginner meets. A check also does not hold when something else went wrong while it ran (an item that is not there, a division by zero) or it ran past its time budget, whatever its `expect` lines said. A failed `expect` is not painted on the picture (`Runtime_Error.in_check`).
- **`roci test [folder or file] [--accept] [--frames N] [--seed N]`** runs every check of a sketch in order and says which held, then compares the sketch's picture with the stored one. A folder without its own `sketch.roci` is walked for the sketches in it (not `assets`, `libs`, `output`, `tests` or hidden folders), so `roci test examples` tests all of them and passes over those with nothing to test. Exit codes: 0 everything held, 1 a check failed, a picture changed or a sketch would not run, 2 the words were wrong or a file was missing. Output goes through `tools/term` like every command.
- **Snapshots** are stored in the sketch folder's `tests/` folder, which is committed: `tests/snapshot-N.png` for window N and `tests/snapshot.txt` (a word and a number a line: `frames`, `seed`, `windows`), so a sketch drawn from the same seed and frame count gives the same picture. `roci test --accept` runs the sketch headless (`--frames` after which the picture is taken, 1 by default; `--seed`, 1 by default; both remembered) and stores a picture of every window at logical size (scale 1). A plain `roci test` with a stored picture runs the same frames at the same seed and compares each window: a pixel counts as changed when a channel is off by more than 12 and up to 40 of 120,000 pixels may be (the golden images' own allowance, because graphics drivers draw edges a little differently), and a difference is said in plain words (how many pixels, what share, in which area, or that the size changed), the new picture is saved in `output/test/window-N.png`, and `--accept` is named as the way to take the change. A sketch with no stored picture and no checks has nothing to test, and a computer that cannot draw (no graphics driver) says the picture was not drawn, which is never a failure. The checks run before the frames, from the sketch's set-up state, and a sketch's own runtime errors during the frames are said after the results.
- **Not offered (yet):** a tolerance word for `expect` (`expect x is about 0.3`), a way to test input (sending events to a sketch), picture comparison of one region, checks that run on `roci run` with a key, and a `where:` form that attaches checks to a verb in the `learn` header. The first stable release can add any of them without breaking a sketch, because `check` and `expect` are the only new words.
### 5.17 Remembering between runs (decided 2026-11-17)

`remember "best score" score`, `recall "best score" start 0`, `forget "best score"` and `forget memory`: a light key-value store in `memory.toml` beside `sketch.roci`, for a high score, a setting or a count that the next run should start from. It is state, not a data set.

- **The name is text written in the sketch** (`Memory_Name_Not_Text`). The checker sees every `remember` and every `recall ... start` of a name, so what `recall` gives comes from them, as `msg` comes from every `send` (§4.5): `check_memory.odin` records the kinds in the first pass of `check` and reads them in the second. Constants as names were decided and are not done: a name worked out from a constant needs constant folding the checker does not have; only a literal works for now.
- **One name, one kind** (`Memory_Kinds_Differ`, said at the second site and naming both kinds). A `recall` of a name nothing remembers and with no `start` is `Memory_Never_Kept`, with the nearest name. A kind that cannot be written down (a window, picture, sound or gradient) is `Memory_Kind_Unsupported`. What can be kept: numbers, text, true or false, vectors, colours, and lists and records of those (a table cannot yet).
- **Missing is not an error.** `recall` gives `start`, or the zero of its kind. A file holding another kind under the name (edited by hand, or by an older sketch) is the runtime message `Memory_Mismatch`, once, and `start` is used; the file is untouched until the next real write, so a hand edit is never lost to a typo.
- **The file** (`roci/memory.odin`; the codec between values and TOML is `roci/interp/memory.odin`): a whole number is written without a point, a vector is an array of numbers, a colour is `"#rrggbbaa"` (0 to 255), a list an array and a record a table in curly brackets. The type the checker settled decides how it is read back, so text that looks like a colour stays text. `roci/toml` grew to read and write what that needs: arrays of text and of mixed values, tables in curly brackets, single-quoted text and, with `exact` set, `nan` and `inf` (the interface files still write them as 0).
- **Writing.** `remember` only changes the value in memory. The file is written after a second without changes (not on every frame), when the sketch ends, and when the program is about to go to the background or is ended by the system, which `roci/platform` hears through an SDL event watch because a phone sends no quit first (unchecked on a phone). It is written beside the file and renamed over it, never half. The header comment is regenerated; keys keep the order they have and new ones go at the end. A file past 300 KB is said once. The file is not watched while the sketch runs.
- **Reload keeps the values** (they live in `roci`, not in the interpreter); only `forget` clears them. `roci run` of a sketch folder keeps `memory.toml` there; a lone `.roci` file keeps nothing on disk. `--frames`, `roci test` and the golden images read the file and never write it, as `ui.toml` is never written by them.
- **Git.** `roci new` and `roci init` in a new folder put `memory.toml` in `.gitignore` (an existing `.gitignore` is never touched); commit the file on purpose to ship a starting state.
- **C.** `roci_remember_number`, `_text`, `_bool` and `_numbers`, the matching `roci_recall_*` (which take the start value) and `roci_forget` and `roci_forget_memory`, over the same store.
- **Packaged sketches** (built for macOS 2026-11-20, `roci/rt/app.odin`): the program finds its `assets` in the bundle (`SDL_GetBasePath`) and works from the app's own folder (`SDL_GetPrefPath` with the bundle id, so `~/Library/Application Support/<id>`), where `memory.toml`, `vault.bin`, `vault.key` and `ui.toml` that the sketch folder had are copied once (`seed_home`, never over a file that is there) and where `output/` is made. The other systems wait for their packaging; `SDL_GetPrefPath` is also what iOS uses.
- **The vault** (`in vault` on any of the three verbs, `forget vault`; `roci/vault.odin`): the same store in `vault.bin`, for what a player must not edit. The values are written in a compact binary form (one tag byte for each kind, little-endian) and sealed whole with XChaCha20-Poly1305 from `core:crypto` (checked against the installed Odin's sources, 2026-11-17), the key made by HKDF-SHA256 from the 32 bytes of `vault.key`, a fresh 24-byte nonce from the system's random numbers for each write (a test sets its own through `the.vault_random`), and the magic, version and nonce covered by the tag, so a change to any byte (every one is tested) or a cut file does not open. A file that does not open is said once, moved to `vault.bin.bad` and the vault starts empty; the file before the last write is kept as `vault.bin.bak` and read when the newest is missing or ruined. A run that may not write never moves or makes a file. Names in the vault and in `memory.toml` are separate, and the checker types them apart. `roci vault show|set|forget [--folder f]` (`cli/vault.odin`) reads and changes it with the folder's key. `roci new` ignores `vault.key`, `vault.bin`, `.bak` and `.bad`. C: `roci_vault_set_key` and the `roci_vault_` remember, recall and forget functions.
- **What the vault is not.** It keeps honest players honest. The key must travel inside the program, so someone with tools can find it; an old copy of the file can be put back (no offline counter can stop that); a score that must be trusted belongs on a server.
